## Distilled rules
1. **Stack signals, never chase one.** One distress signal is a maybe; two or three stacked on the same parcel is a situation. Prioritize parcels where a deadline, a money problem, and a physical or life-event signal overlap — and verify each signal is still active before outreach.
2. **Buy the seller's problem, not the house.** A below-market sale is almost never about the building; it is about what the building represents — a deadline, a burden, a fight, a bill. The offer wins when it solves the seller's situation (speed, certainty, simplicity) better than a higher retail price would.
3. **Certainty and speed are the currency.** Sellers trade price for a sure thing: a clear next step, a firm timeline, an as-is close with no repairs or showings. Make the path simple and the outcome certain, and the discount follows; pressure and persuasion do not.
4. **Let the comps negotiate, not you.** Never argue your price against the seller's anchor. Show two or three as-is comps and one renovated comp, printed, with sale prices, dates, asking-vs-sold, and days on market — and explain the gap (repairs, time, holding costs, investor risk) in plain terms. The number lands as the market's verdict, not your opinion, and the seller stops defending against you.
5. **Three values, three conversations.** As-is value is for the seller, after-repair value is for the buyer, rent comps are for the landlord. Mixing them — especially quoting the renovated sale to a seller with a dated house — is how deals die. Keep each set separate, recent (last 6 months), within a tight radius, and never cross a school district or neighborhood boundary.
6. **Reset the anchor, don't fight it.** Find the seller's real anchor (the old peak sale, the neighbor's price, the number they feel they deserve), reconstruct when and under what conditions it happened, then replace it with current evidence and the cost of waiting. Repeated exposure to consistent evidence de-anchors; arguing the number never does.

7. **Price the cost of doing nothing.** Most sellers do not refuse the offer; they refuse to decide. Inaction feels safe, action feels like a loss. Make the cost of staying still concrete — taxes, holding costs, deterioration, the auction or tax-sale date — and the decision moves.
8. **Frame the same number as a loss avoided, not a gain offered.** A loss hurts roughly twice as much as an equal gain pleases. Show what the seller keeps — cash now, no repairs, no showings, no risk of a deal falling through — rather than what they might gain later.
9. **Never fake urgency or scarcity.** Real deadlines (a tax-sale date, an auction, a probate clock) are the only honest ones. Manufactured timers and invented "only one left" claims work briefly and then poison trust — and they cross the program's line against covert manipulation.

10. **The deal starts in the seller's mind, not in the house.** Before the offer exists, the seller already holds a picture of their problem and of what the property is worth. Change that picture with evidence and clarity, and the price follows; argue against the picture, and the seller defends it.
11. **One lot mirrors the market.** A single parcel's story — why the owner bought it, why it sits unused, why the tax bill keeps coming — repeats across a thousand parcels. Learn the pattern from one case and you can read the next list without starting from zero.
12. **Catch the seller on the downswing of holding.** Motivation rises and falls like a tide; a seller who would not sell in spring may sell in winter after another tax bill, another winter of vacancy, another round of costs. Timing is not luck — it is showing up again when the cost of holding has grown.
13. **Flip the pole, keep the facts.** Distress and opportunity are the same situation at opposite poles — the same parcel, the same numbers, a different reading. Change the reading with evidence and certainty, never by changing the facts.
14. **Buy the downswing, assign into the upswing — and never trust a clock that promises the turn date.** Markets and motivation swing, but no cycle runs on a fixed schedule; the turn is recognized from live signals (days on market, price cuts, inventory), not predicted from a calendar.
15. **Be a cause, not an effect — push the outreach, pull the truth.** Every below-market sale has a cause; find it and you find the deal. Your calls are causes too. Push to open the conversation, then stop pushing and listen — the seller's real reason only surfaces when you receive it.
16. **The whole market is one system — a lesson in one deal is a lesson about all of them.** Every lot, seller, buyer, and signal is connected: what you learn from one parcel's cause, one buyer's number, one failed close transfers to the next deal, because the same forces are at work. But connection runs both ways — a careless offer, a fake deadline, a burned buyer poisons the whole system, not just that deal. Act as if every move is public to the market, because the market talks.
17. **Work backwards from the end, never forwards from the seller's price.** The offer is set by what the property will be worth fixed up, minus repairs, minus every real cost, minus the profit the deal must carry — and the MAO is the ceiling, not the opening. Every dollar negotiated below MAO is profit; every dollar above it is profit destroyed.
18. **Price the fee by the gap you filled, not by the biggest spread you can extract.** The value of the service is the size of the problem that would have grown without it — the holding costs, the risk, the dead time, the certainty delivered. A fee that fits the buyer's math and the seller's relief closes; a fee that merely takes the widest spread burns the buyer relationship the next deal depends on.
19. **Value each type by its own math — never carry one formula across types.** Houses price off comps and ARV, small multifamily off comps plus rents, commercial off NOI and cap rate, land off what a cash buyer will pay for the dirt. The 70% rule is a house rule; on land it collapses to 10–30%, and on a building the buyer thinks in yield, not in a percentage of a renovated price.
20. **Build the buyer list before the contract — and the longer the deal, the earlier the list.** A contract with no buyer expires unassigned. On commercial deals the buyer is a syndicator, fund, or operator with 60–90 days of due diligence, so the list has to exist before the seller is locked, not after.
21. **Five units is a cliff — treat it as a different business, not a bigger house.** At five units the financing, the underwriting, the timeline, the buyer, and the valuation all change at once. A fourplex is a house with rents; a five-unit building is a commercial asset. Price, contract, and plan on the correct side of the line.
22. **Talk to people — be proactive, never wait.** Deals come from quality conversations with distressed owners, and the wholesaler chooses: wait for sellers to call, pay to make them call, or pick up the phone and call them. The phone is the driver's seat — whoever sources the deal chooses what to do with it.
23. **Every seller conversation has four pillars: condition, timeline, motivation, price.** A deal is not real until all four are known. A five-minute call that rushes to an offer mostly cancels; a slow, deep discovery call — thirty to forty-five minutes, no agenda, listening — is what surfaces the real reason and the real number.
24. **Build the buyer list with the same energy as the seller list — and let the buyer set the criteria.** Ask buyers what they buy, how they buy, and what would make them confident without seeing the property; then bring them deals that fit. A contract with no buyer expires unassigned, and a buyer list built before the contract is what makes the assignment real.
25. **Find the forcing decision, not the owner.** A lead is a property where something forces a decision — a deadline, a filing, a price cut after 90 days, a lien that blocks transfer. An owner with no pressure is a name on a list, not a lead.
26. **Stack two filters; run one channel for ninety days.** Two ordinary filters crossed (fixer-upper + price cut in 30 days) turn thousands of listings into a handful. One channel run consistently for ninety days beats six channels sampled shallowly — the numbers you learn are the asset.
27. **Talk to the owner before the auction, and tell the seller the math.** Pre-foreclosure beats auction and REO because the owner still decides; and disclosure is now the law in several states, not just good manners — hold every deal to the standard a lawyer could review afterward and confirm was fair.
28. **The buyer pays the math, not the pitch.** A cash buyer says yes when their total cost — contract plus assignment fee plus repairs — sits at or below their MAO, roughly 70% of ARV minus repairs, adjusted for market and risk. Sell the spreadsheet, not the story: ARV with comps, repair scope, clean title, close date, in one complete package, within hours of signing.
29. **Trust is the only discount that compounds.** One accurate deal earns the next yes without re-verification; one light number resets trust to zero. Give a new buyer real margin early, disclose the bad news first, and never let the fee eat the buyer's profit — a buyer who made money on your deal takes your next call.
30. **Match the deal to the species.** Cash buyers are binary and walk from mess; creative buyers pay for terms, not price. Send deep-discount clean deals to cash, flexible-terms deals to creative — and never pitch a cash buyer a deal whose total cost breaks their MAO, because the ghost is the answer.
31. **Price the frame, not the finish.** A house's value lives in its skeleton; finishes are cheap to change and framing is not. On every walkthrough, read the frame through its symptoms — bouncy floors, racked doors, stud-line cracks, sagging rooflines — before pricing anything else. The repair ladder runs $500 to $25,000+, and hidden damage multiplies the visible estimate: the $4,000 quote that became $24,000 is the rule, not the exception.
32. **The 10x rule: what costs $200 in framing costs $2,000 after drywall.** Every framing shortcut — an undersized header, missing blocking, skipped hurricane ties — is a small number during construction and a ten-times number after the walls close. For the wholesaler this cuts both ways: it is why bad framing discounts a deal 10–35%, and why the buyer's repair budget must assume the hidden multiplier, never the visible quote. Budget the iceberg, not the tip.
33. **Structural trouble kills financing before it kills the deal.** A flagged structural defect can trigger a lender repair condition and shrink the buyer pool to cash only — which is exactly the wholesaler's buyer. Know the appraisal impact (10–25% for structural issues, up to 35% for structural damage) so the MAO already contains it, and never let a buyer discover the frame after the contract is signed. The frame is the first thing priced, the first thing disclosed, and the last thing surprised by.
34. **Price the systems, not the surfaces.** The panel and the pipes decide the deal. A beautiful kitchen on 65-year-old galvanized pipe is a $10,000 surprise, not a $10,000 premium. Read the panel brand and the pipe material before you name a number, and let the MAO carry the systems cost, not the paint.
35. **Uninsurable is a lead, not just a cost.** When insurance or a lender refuses the house, the retail exit closes and only cash can buy. That seller is your seller and the cash buyer is your buyer. The wall for them is the door for you — but only if you disclose the defect and price the cure in writing.
36. **Disclose the defect, price the cure, count only legal space.** Unpermitted work and failed systems can still be wholesaled, but only with the problem in the contract, the fix priced in, and the ARV built on legal square footage alone. Hiding it is fraud; pricing it is the business. A permit record and a sewer scope cost a few hundred dollars and protect a deal worth tens of thousands.

37. **Public record first, paid last — and stack two free signals before you contact anyone.** Pull owner, mailing address, tax status, liens, and zoning free from the assessor, recorder, treasurer, GIS, or court before paying for any list, tool, or trace. A lead only earns outreach when two independent signals from different offices land on the same parcel — field or listing distress plus an ownership or situation signal. One signal is a note; two is a lead.
38. **Verify the same week you contact, and sell speed and certainty — never rescue.** Owners cure taxes, fix violations, and dismiss cases constantly; a month-old list is a list of people who already solved the problem or already been called by everyone else. Confirm the owner on the assessor and the lien/title picture on the recorder before outreach, and offer a fast, certain cash closing — never promise to stop a foreclosure or use rescue language, which triggers foreclosure-consultant statutes in about 25 states.
39. **Market the contract, never the house — and never call a number you have not scrubbed.** You are selling your right to buy, not brokering a home you do not own; if your state requires a disclosure, registration, or a licence, get it before you post the deal anywhere — a free post that breaks the rule is not free. Scrub every number against the National Do Not Call Registry (no more than 31 days old) plus state lists, call only 8 a.m.–9 p.m. recipient local time, honor opt-outs within 10 business days — a skip-traced number is not consent for autodialed calls, AI voice, ringless voicemail, or bulk texts, and TCPA damages run $500–$1,500 per violation.
## Findings by topic

### 1. Distress signals — the full taxonomy + psychology (studied 2026-11-20)
Distress is not one condition; it is a spectrum of signals that fall into four families, and the deal comes from how they stack.

**Family A — Financial distress (deadline + money owed).** Pre-foreclosure (notice of default / lis pendens filed, auction date set), tax delinquency (two or more years unpaid is the strong version; a tax sale date is the deadline), liens (judgment, HOA, utility, contractor, IRS), bankruptcy (federal filing; an automatic stay applies and a trustee controls the sale). Where to spot free: county recorder / clerk (default notices, lis pendens), county tax collector / treasurer (delinquent lists), court dockets (bankruptcy, judgments), sheriff / trustee sale calendars. What it means: a lender or the county has started a process with a date at the end of it. What it does NOT prove: that the owner wants to sell — most owners in pre-foreclosure are fighting to keep the house. Verify the current stage, the sale date, whether it was cured or postponed, the total owed with penalties, and whether equity exists at all (a seriously underwater owner needs lender approval for a short sale, so motivation without equity is not a deal).

**Family B — Life-event distress (ownership or household changed).** Probate / inheritance (owner died; estate in court; heirs often live far away and want liquidation), divorce (family court; a jointly owned property may have to be divided), job loss / relocation (owner must move and needs proceeds to buy elsewhere), estate / heir situations where multiple family members must agree. Where to spot free: probate court filings and legal notices in local papers, family court filings, obituary / death records cross-checked against title. What it does NOT prove: who has authority to sell, or that heirs agree — heirs often disagree, and a probate sale may need court confirmation. Verify who the court appointed, whether confirmation is required, and whether a sale is already underway.

**Family C — Physical distress (the property itself).** Vacancy (overgrown yard, piled mail, boarded windows, no lights; confirmed by USPS vacancy flags or winterization, not looks alone — about 10.3% of U.S. homes sat vacant in early 2026, roughly 15 million properties), code violations (open citations, repeat neighbor complaints, city liens), condemnation (cannot legally be occupied), deferred maintenance / long hold with nothing built (the Fresno lots pattern: low holding cost, no use, no income). Where to spot free: city code enforcement records, vacant-building registries, driving for dollars, USPS vacancy data. What it does NOT prove: abandonment — vacant can mean between tenants, renovating, or a second home. Verify current occupancy, open violations, and who is paying the bills.

**Family D — Situational distress (owner fatigue, no crisis).** Tired landlord (bad tenants, late-night calls, legal fees — the property has become a symbol of stress), absentee / out-of-state owner (mailing address differs from site address; distance makes management a headache), long ownership with deferred maintenance (10+ years owned, low assessed value, high equity), expired or withdrawn MLS listings and price cuts (the owner already decided to sell once and retail failed). Where to spot free: county assessor (mailing vs. site address, ownership length), MLS history, driving for dollars. What it does NOT prove: that the owner is motivated — millions own absentee on purpose. Verify fatigue directly in conversation; the signal only earns outreach, the conversation earns the deal.

**The stacking rule.** The strongest leads combine filters: absentee or out-of-state owner + equity above ~40–50% + one live distress signal. One practitioner framing: instead of 3,500 people with one reason, you start with 140 people with two. Combinations that most often lead somewhere: tax delinquency + vacant registration (nobody paying, nobody living there); pre-foreclosure + high equity (room for a discount that still leaves the seller cash); probate + out-of-area heir + vacancy. Always re-verify before outreach: signals go stale (cured, postponed, dismissed, paid, released), and a stale signal wastes the contact and the trust.

**The psychology — why a seller takes less.** Seven emotional drivers explain most below-market sales: (1) overwhelm and decision fatigue — buried by repairs, legal issues, too many choices, the brain craves simplicity ("just take it off my hands"); this alone accounts for offers accepted 10–20% below market; (2) fear of the unknown — not knowing if a listing will sit, what repairs will be demanded, whether any offer comes; the first sure thing wins; (3) urgency from life events — divorce, relocation, foreclosure notice, job loss, family emergency; speed matters more than maximizing; (4) emotional detachment — inheritors and absentee owners feel no connection to the house and want closure, especially while it costs them taxes, repairs, and time; (5) skepticism about traditional sales — burned by a fallen-through deal, tired of showings, agent fees, buyers backing out; a simple cash offer feels safer; (6) pressure from outside influences — family, friends, online voices eroding confidence until the seller defaults to what feels safe; (7) desire for control — tired of being told what to do by agents, banks, HOAs; a fast deal restores agency even at a lower price. Underneath all seven: the seller is trading price for peace of mind, certainty, and speed. The same offer can feel insulting to one seller and generous to another — timing, trust, and tension decide which. The working frame: only buy from people who HAVE to sell, not people who WANT to sell; and the negotiation is not convincing, it is showing that real cash now beats a speculative higher number later. Compliance note: outreach must stay inside DNC and TCPA rules; skip tracing and cold contact carry legal limits.

### 2. Comp psychology — the format and structure of nearby-sale proof (studied 2026-11-20)
The comp conversation is not about the number; it is about whose number it is. A seller who hears your opinion argues with you. A seller who sees the market's own record argues with the record — and usually loses, quietly, to it.

**Three values, three audiences.** A distressed property has three different prices, and each one belongs to a different conversation: (1) as-is value — what the house would sell for today, unrepaired; this is the number the seller needs to hear, built from other dated or distressed sales, cash sales, investor purchases, estate sales; (2) after-repair value (ARV) — what it sells for once renovated, built from updated retail sales in the last 3–6 months; this is the number the end buyer checks, and your buyer will pull their own ARV comps within an hour of seeing your deal; (3) rent comps — what it rents for after repairs, for landlord buyers. A wholesaler who only knows the ARV cannot explain to a seller why the house is worth less today. The classic seller error is anchoring on the renovated sale down the street; the as-is comps are the antidote, shown with real data.

**How to pull comps that hold up.** Write down the subject's facts first (square footage, beds/baths, lot, year built, garage, style, condition — confirmed on site, since assessor records are often wrong). Tight radius first: a quarter mile in dense neighborhoods, half a mile in suburbs, more only in rural areas; never cross a major boundary (highway, school district line, owner-occupied vs. rental blocks). Recent sales only: last 6 months, extend to 12 only if needed and note the trend. Size within about 20% of the subject's square footage; match bedroom count and style/age where possible. Read every listing — photos and remarks tell you condition ("investor special," "cash only," "as-is" belongs in the as-is set; new kitchen/roof/staged photos belong in the ARV set). Check how each sale happened: days on market, price cuts, seller concessions, whether the buyer was a relative or entity; remove anything that was not a normal arm's-length closed sale. Keep three to six that fit; three strong comps beat eight weak ones. Fannie Mae's appraisal rules require at least three closed comparables, with sales closed within the last 12 months preferred — your end buyer's lender and appraiser will follow rules like these, so your comps should survive the same test.

**Adjusting: the grid.** No comp is identical to the subject. Appraisers use an adjustment grid: start with each comp's sale price, add value for features the subject has and the comp lacks, subtract for features the comp has and the subject lacks — always adjust the comp toward the subject, never the other way around. The best dollar figures come from paired-sales comparison: two otherwise similar sales differing by one feature (e.g. a garage) — the price gap is roughly the market's value for that feature. Condition and updates are the largest adjustment in distressed deals and the one people get wrong most; square footage is priced per square foot of difference at a rate well below the average price per square foot. Self-check: if your adjustments add up to a large share of a comp's sale price, that comp is not very comparable — find a better one rather than adjusting harder. Common mistakes: using the highest sale on the street (one outlier is not the market), mixing renovated and unrenovated comps, ignoring seller concessions (a $250,000 sale with $10,000 in seller-paid closing costs is closer to $240,000), crossing a school district line, leaning on automated portal estimates (a starting point, never a comp), and forgetting the trend in a falling market.

**The presentation format — let the data negotiate.** The tested practitioner sequence: (1) on the phone, ask the seller's price idea first — just to gauge where they are; ask if they would be flexible, considering houses around them have sold for less; (2) promise to research and print the actual sales around them; (3) at the appointment, after walking the house, sit down and go over printed comparable sales — sale price, dates sold, asking price vs. actual sale price, days on market. Show two or three as-is comps and one renovated comp, and explain the gap in plain terms: the cost of the repairs, the time to complete them, the holding and selling costs, and the profit an investor needs to take the risk. That conversation is far easier than defending a number with no support. It works because you are letting the data do the talking and negotiate for you instead of the sellers negotiating against you, which leads to defensive language and behavior. Once your actual opinion of price is removed from the equation, sellers become much more receptive to a realistic price. For buyers, put after-repair comps in the deal sheet with addresses, sale dates, sizes, photos or links, and a one-line note on each.

**The psychology underneath — anchoring.** The first number in a negotiation sets the ceiling and the floor of everything that follows. The classic study (Northcraft and Neale, 1987, Organizational Behavior and Human Decision Processes) took real estate agents and students to tour an actual house appraised at $74,900, gave everyone the same 10-page packet of comps and data, and changed only the listing price: both amateurs and professionals moved their valuations right along with the anchor. The seller's anchor is usually a memorable peak, an old neighboring sale, or the amount they believe they deserve — and it persists even after demand, taxes, financing, or comparable values change, because accepting a lower price feels like leaving money on the table. The reset sequence: surface the anchor (ask which prior sale or peak defines their target, and why that number feels valid — do not mistake the asking price for the true anchor), reconstruct its context (when it happened, under what financing/tax/demand conditions, versus today), present current evidence (recent sales, competing listings, buyer responses, time on market — evidence generated after the relevant market change), mark the market reset (name the event that separates the old pricing environment from the current one), price the cost of waiting (carrying costs, more market time, risk of further deterioration), then agree on a current evidence-based price or a review trigger. Repeated exposure to consistent evidence lets the seller de-anchor. Precision also matters: exact figures (e.g. $512,450) suggest deep math and are trusted more than round numbers; charm pricing ($499,900) reads cheaper because buyers focus on the left digits.

**Fresno lots application.** Land comps follow the same discipline with one difference: the first acre usually carries most of the value and each added acre is worth less, so adjust acreage carefully; when the lot looks worth more than the house on it, pull vacant lot and new-construction sales as well. For the four CMX lots, the seller conversation is the same shape as the house version: show nearby small-lot cash sales (the $50,000–$90,000 band from the earlier sales pull, middle ~$67,500), show what the lot would be worth entitled and built, and explain the gap — entitlement cost, holding cost, construction risk — in plain terms, with the number as the market's verdict, not the wholesaler's opinion.

### 3. Spending psychology and human error — why people spend, what makes them say yes (studied 2026-11-20)
People do not decide the way a spreadsheet would. Buying and selling decisions run on fast, automatic mental shortcuts first and reasoning second — and the shortcuts are the architecture, not a flaw that awareness switches off. A seller who has read about loss aversion still acts on loss aversion. The wholesaler's job is to work with that architecture, honestly: the same bias can be used in service of the seller's real decision or against their interest, and the difference is intent and outcome, not technique.

**The seven biases that decide most buying decisions.** (1) **Loss aversion** (Kahneman and Tversky, 1979, Prospect Theory): the pain of losing something is roughly twice as powerful as the pleasure of gaining the equivalent thing. Framing matters more than content — "you'll lose this if you don't act" and "you'll gain this if you do" describe the same outcome and produce different decisions. For a seller, the loss is the price they imagine leaving on the table; for a buyer, it is the deal slipping away. Risk reversal (a clear, certain, as-is close) works because it shrinks the perceived loss the other side's brain is computing. (2) **Anchoring** (Tversky and Kahneman, 1974): whatever number is seen first becomes the reference point for every number after it, and the frame is sticky — even when the anchor is obviously arbitrary (in the original study, a spun wheel anchored estimates of African nations in the UN). Whoever sets the first number sets the frame; if the wholesaler does not set it deliberately, the seller brings their own — usually an old peak sale or a neighbor's price. (See topic 2 for the anchor-reset sequence.) (3) **Status quo bias** (Samuelson and Zeckhauser, 1988): inaction feels like the safe baseline and action feels like a small loss event. Most prospects do not refuse the offer; they refuse to decide — "I need to think about it" and the conversation never resumes, because the deferral itself registers as the safe choice. The fix is to make the cost of inaction concrete: what happens to the property and the seller's money in the next 90 days if nothing changes? (4) **Framing** (Kahneman and Tversky, 1981): the same information in different frames produces different decisions even when people know the frame. "Saves 200 of 600" and "400 of 600 will die" are the same outcome and choose differently. Every claim carries a frame; the wholesaler chooses it whether they mean to or not. (5) **Social proof** (Cialdini, *Influence*, 1984/2021): when people similar to the buyer have already chosen something and signalled satisfaction, the brain treats the choice as pre-validated. Testimonials, named past sellers, visible community are mechanisms, not decoration — the brain is looking for evidence that others made this choice and survived it. (6) **Authority**: the brain defers to recognised expertise to cut the cost of evaluating a claim — recognised being the operative word. Substance that has no visible signal (a body of work, an audience, named past clients) does not register. Being good and being seen as good are different gaps. (7) **Scarcity** (Worchel, Lee, and Adewole, 1975): limited supply raises perceived value even when the limit is irrelevant to the item's usefulness. This is the most abused bias in selling: manufactured countdowns and fake "only 3 left" notices work briefly, then poison the well when the slow system catches up. The honest version uses constraints that actually exist — a real auction date, a real tax-sale deadline, one lot, one buyer who can close this week.

**The second layer — biases that show up in specific moments.** **Endowment effect** (Thaler, 1980): people overvalue what they already own — in the classic mug experiment, students given a mug demanded roughly twice as much to sell it as others would pay to buy it. It is the seller-side twin of loss aversion and the main reason a homeowner is offended by a comp-based price: the house carries memory, pride, and identity, and the market values none of that. Selling someone an upgrade or extension of what they own does not trigger it; selling them a replacement does. **Sunk cost fallacy** (Arkes and Blumer, 1985): once time or money is invested, people keep investing in something failing rather than walk away — the owner who has paid three years of taxes and repairs on a vacant lot cannot bear to "waste" that, so they hold on while losses compound. The wholesaler's job is to make abandoning the prior investment feel like the rational move, not the wasted one. **Decoy effect** (Huber, Payne, and Puto, 1982): a third option makes one of two original options look better — three offer structures (cash now at a lower price, a higher price with terms, a middle option) can make the cash offer read as the obvious value. **Present bias**: $100 today beats $110 tomorrow for most people; a certain close this month beats a speculative higher price in six months. **Overconfidence**: sellers overestimate their ability to get the top price and underestimate repair costs and time on market. **Recency**: the last hot sale on the street feels like the permanent market. **IKEA effect**: people value what they built or worked on — a seller who renovated the kitchen themselves prices in their own labor at retail rates.

**Why people spend — the spending side.** Spending is driven less by the object than by the story the buyer tells about themselves and the feeling the purchase promises. Affective forecasting errors mean people buy the imagined feeling, not the thing; the pain of paying is dulled by distance from the money (credit, one-click, "someone else closes it"), which pulls consumption forward while future costs fade. Urgency and scarcity cues shift the brain from deliberation to speed — time pressure makes people satisfice rather than optimize, and the combination of a ticking clock with low stock is especially potent. That is exactly why regulators treat fake or resetting timers as manipulative dark patterns: they create pressure without truthful scarcity.

**Human error — the wholesaler's own.** The same architecture runs in the wholesaler. The errors to design against: anchoring on your own first number and then defending it; confirmation bias (only pulling comps that support your price); overconfidence in a repair estimate you have not verified; present bias (chasing the fast small fee instead of the durable buyer relationship); and the status quo of your own process (reusing last month's script after the market moved). The defense is the same one the program already uses: written criteria before looking (maximum price, minimum fee, acceptable condition, target area), evidence printed and checked, and a rule that a rule earns its keep or is cut.

**Application to the Fresno lots and the $350 texts.** The seller conversation is a status-quo and loss-aversion conversation before it is a price conversation: the lot costs $9–$44 a month in tax, produces nothing, and the seller's "do nothing" is itself a decision with a price. The offer should be framed as what the seller stops losing (taxes, liability, the burden of an unused parcel) and what they keep (cash now, no repairs, no showings, no agent fees, no risk of a buyer backing out) — with the real deadline, if any, stated plainly and no invented one. For the buyer side, the same architecture runs in reverse: the buyer fears losing the deal to another investor more than they love gaining it, so a real, dated, first-come close window — stated truthfully — moves them; a fake one burns the relationship that the next deal depends on.

**Sources**
- Science of Selling, "What Cognitive Biases Influence Buying Decisions? Seven Mechanisms the Buying Brain Runs On" — https://scienceofselling.co/learn/what-cognitive-biases-influence-buying-decisions
- Homfry, "The Psychology of Money and Smart Real Estate Moves" (19 Oct 2026) — https://www.homfry.com/article/the-psychology-of-money-and-smart-real-estate-moves

## Brought back to Mac — loopholes, gray areas, new value ideas (who / what / when / where / why)
1. **Gray area — unpermitted structural work (from section 11, trades I, 2026-11-20).** Who: wholesalers meeting distressed houses with load-bearing walls removed without a beam, additions built without permits, missing fire blocking. What: most states legally require the seller to disclose known material defects in writing before accepting an offer; buying and assigning a house with unpermitted structural work without disclosing it is a legal exposure, not an opportunity. When: at contracting. Where: everywhere the loop operates; the gap is between what the seller knows and what the buyer can see. Why it matters: the program's rule is disclosure-first — the gap is closed, not worked. Mac decides per case; no action taken.
2. **Boundary — broker-licensing brush on unpermitted-work deals (from section 12, trades II, 2026-11-20).** Who: wholesalers who market assigned contracts on houses with unpermitted work. What: in some states the marketing of the contract itself can fall under broker-licensing rules — Montana's broker rule (MCA 37-51-301) and Massachusetts Chapter 112 Sections 87PP and 87RR were named as reaching fee-takers who market. When: whenever a contract is marketed rather than a property owned. Where: state-dependent; Montana and Massachusetts named, other states unexamined. Why it matters: the unpermitted-work lead stream is real and lawful to work, but the way the deal is marketed may need an attorney's wording in some states. Mac decides per case; do not scale this lead source without a local attorney's read.
3. **Boundary — disclosure duties on failed systems (from section 12, trades II, 2026-11-20).** Who: any seller or wholesaler handling a house with a known failed sewer, septic, or electrical defect. What: failure to disclose known material defects can support contract rescission and fraud claims — Florida Statute 689.261 was cited as mandatory material-defect disclosure, and Texas requires disclosure of known aluminum wiring. When: at contracting, not at closing. Where: every state examined has some version of this duty; the exact form varies. Why it matters: the lawful skip (cash as-is) depends entirely on disclosure; the unlawful skip (hiding the defect) is what turns a deal into a lawsuit. Nothing to exploit — a line to respect. Mac decides per case.
4. **Deal-finding loopholes and value angles A–O (from section 9, 2026-11-20).** Tax-sale surplus finder service; land-bank and county-held over-the-counter inventory; the no-open-data-portal arbitrage; zombie foreclosures; bird-dogging (gray area — verify California before collecting a fee); the California wholesaling window (AB 1850 stalled, not dead); the list as a product; Apify's $5/month free credits as a listing monitor (gray — portal terms); Regrid + county ArcGIS as a free parcel API; over-the-counter / struck-off land; HUD Good Neighbor Next Door and Dollar Homes (not a wholesaler arbitrage); the wholesaling legality line; vacant land as the undervalued market; the First Look timing trap (do NOT use); free list-stacking. Full who / what / when / where / why in section 9. Mac decides per case; nothing acted on.
5. **The VantageScore 4.0 / FICO 10T transition (from section 28, 2026-11-20).** Who: renters and borrowers with improving credit trends; sellers stuck just below a threshold. What: since April 22, 2026, Fannie Mae, Freddie Mac, and FHA accept VantageScore 4.0 and FICO 10T alongside Classic FICO; VantageScore 4.0 can count on-time rent payments, and both new models use 24-month trended data. When: VantageScore 4.0 broadly available to approved lenders since September 9, 2026; FICO 10T rollout follows. Where: nationwide, GSE and FHA loans. Why: a tenant-buyer with clean rent history but a thin Classic FICO may now qualify; lender adoption varies and most lenders still default to Classic FICO — knowing which lender uses which model is an edge. Opportunity sign: match tenant-buyers to VantageScore 4.0 lenders; time lease-option periods to new-model qualification; acquire a current list of local VantageScore 4.0 lenders plus a rent-reporting path. Mac decides per case; nothing acted on.
6. **The free dispute as a deal tool (from section 28, 2026-11-20).** Who: sellers and tenant-buyers whose reports contain errors. What: disputing is free, the bureau has 30 days to investigate, and anything it cannot verify must be corrected or deleted; incorrect information was the most common CFPB complaint in 2025. When: before a seller tries to refinance or a buyer tries to qualify. Where: AnnualCreditReport.com; bureau portals; CFPB sample letters. Why: a single wrongly reported 30-day late can cost 50–100 score points; removing it can move a seller from "cannot refinance" to "can refinance," changing whether they must sell at a discount at all. Gray area: informing a seller that free dispute rights exist is education; filing disputes on their behalf, charging for it, or promising a score outcome crosses into unlicensed credit repair. Opportunity sign: a one-page plain-language handout on weekly free reports and the 30-day dispute right, given at first contact. Mac decides per case; nothing acted on.
7. **The assumable-loan blind spot (from section 28, 2026-11-20).** Who: sellers with FHA or VA loans at low rates; buyers who cannot qualify for a new loan at today's rates. What: FHA and VA loans are generally assumable — a buyer can take over the seller's existing loan, rate and all, subject to lender approval and (for VA) entitlement rules. Most sellers and many agents do not know this, and most wholesalers never ask what loan type the seller has. When: whenever the seller's rate is well below current rates and the buyer can meet the assuming lender's credit requirements. Where: any property with an FHA or VA loan. Why: an assumable 3% loan can be worth more than a cash discount and can change whether a seller must sell below market. Gray area: assumption still requires the buyer to qualify with the servicer, and the seller's VA entitlement can stay tied up; get the servicer's written assumption terms before promising anything. Opportunity sign: add "Is your loan FHA or VA?" to the seller intake; check assumability with the servicer with the seller's written permission. Mac decides per case; nothing acted on.
8. **The divorce-credit timing play (from section 28, 2026-11-20).** Who: divorcing sellers whose ex-spouse's missed payments are on their report. What: a divorce decree does not bind creditors, so a seller can be credit-damaged by an ex's non-payment even when the court assigned the debt to the ex; the seller often cannot refinance or qualify for a new mortgage until the joint accounts are closed or the 7-year clock runs. When: during and after divorce, especially before the decree is final. Where: any divorce involving joint mortgage or card debt. Why: one of the largest single sources of must-sell-below-market sellers, and a lease-option or seller-financed exit can help the seller become a buyer again later. The gray area is human, not legal: solve the housing problem honestly, never present yourself as a lawyer or credit counselor. Opportunity sign: structure the exit around the seller's actual constraint (mortgage payment covered, name eventually off the loan); acquire a divorce-attorney referral relationship for the seller's own counsel. Mac decides per case; nothing acted on.

### 5. The laws II — Polarity, Rhythm, Cause and Effect, Gender (studied 2026-11-20)
The same provenance note from topic 4 applies: the seven principles come from *The Kybalion* (Chicago, 1908), attributed to William Walker Atkinson — a modern New Thought synthesis, not an ancient Egyptian text. Of these four, only Gender has a genuine older root, in the masculine–feminine duality running through alchemical literature; Polarity, Rhythm, and Cause and Effect as stated are the book's own formulations. None of the four is scientific. They are used here as operating lenses for a wholesaler, not as claims about physics — and where a lens meets real market evidence, the evidence wins.

**Polarity — "Everything is dual; everything has poles; everything has its pair of opposites."** Opposites are identical in nature, differing only in degree: hot and cold are one scale, fear and courage occupy one axis, hate and love are the same emotion at different intensities. The wholesaler's version: distress and opportunity are the same situation at opposite poles. The Fresno lots are the working proof — four parcels that are, at one pole, a burden (taxes, no income, no use, an owner who never asked to sell) and, at the other pole, a development opportunity (CMX zoning, 16–30 units per acre, a buyer in Edward Williams whose box fits exactly). Nothing about the parcel changes when the pole flips; only the reading changes. The practical discipline is transmutation along the scale, not fighting the pole: a seller's fear of losing money becomes, one degree at a time, a seller's relief at certainty — shown through the as-is comps, the plain explanation of the gap, the firm timeline. The same energy fuels both poles: the seller's anxiety about the property is the energy that, moved along the scale, becomes the willingness to close. The negotiation itself is a polarity: the seller's high anchor and the wholesaler's low offer are extremes of one scale, and the deal closes where the extremes meet — which is why the anchor-reset sequence (topic 2) works: it moves the seller along their own scale instead of arguing from the opposite end. The honest limit: flipping the pole never means changing the facts. Reframing a $20,000 lot as a $90,000 lot is not polarity, it is invention — and the program's rule against fabricated numbers stands.

**Rhythm — "Everything flows, out and in; everything has its tides; all things rise and fall."** The pendulum swing governs tides, seasons, moods, and markets. The wholesaler's version has two swings to read. First, the seller's motivation: it rises and falls with tax bills, seasons, family events, auction dates, and the slow accumulation of holding costs — which is why the contact rhythm matters (topic 4's Vibration, applied): a first contact that plants the idea, a follow-up after the next tax bill, another after a season of vacancy, each landing on a different state of the seller's mind. Second, the market's swing: buyer’s markets and seller’s markets alternate as supply and demand seesaw — high demand and low inventory push prices up and hand power to sellers; high inventory and few buyers push prices down and hand power to buyers. Early warnings of a swing are observable, not mystical: days on market rising, price reductions spreading, inventory building, interest rates moving. The honest check against the lens: a cycle is real, but a clock is not. Propertyology's review of decades of official evidence across 400+ Australian townships finds no orderly sequence — a boom can last one great year or a decade, the gap between booms a few years or fifteen, and no algorithm predicts performance years ahead. Fred Harrison's 18.6-year property cycle (studied from 300 years of UK land-price data, driven by fixed land supply and bank credit, pointing to a 2025–2026 peak) is the strongest counter-claim — a real pattern with real history behind it — but it is a hypothesis about long waves, not a timetable you can trade a single lot against. The Kybalion's own counsel is the useful part: mastery lies not in stopping the swing but in rising above the plane on which it oscillates — for the wholesaler, that means not chasing the market's turn, but working the rhythm: buy the downswing of a seller's holding, assign into the upswing of a buyer's demand, and read the turn from live signals (days on market, price cuts, inventory) rather than from a calendar.

**Cause and Effect — "Every Cause has its Effect; every Effect has its Cause; Chance is but a name for Law not recognized."** Nothing happens by accident, and every below-market sale has a cause — which is why the distress-signal taxonomy (topic 1) is the practical form of this law: find the cause (tax delinquency, probate, divorce, vacancy, tired landlord) and you find the deal. The discipline runs in both directions. Backwards: reverse-engineer every closed deal to its cause — what signal, what timing, what conversation produced it — and replicate the cause, not the luck. Forwards: the wholesaler's own actions are causes, and the deal is their effect. Every call, every follow-up, every printed comp sheet is a cause placed in the chain; a wholesaler who waits for deals to appear is living as an effect of other people's causes. The Kybalion puts it as becoming a cause rather than drifting as an effect — consciously entering the chain of causation. The honest limit: there are many planes of causation, and the wholesaler controls only their own plane. Interest rates, a market turn, a seller's family fight — those causes operate above the wholesaler's reach. The work is to own the causes within reach (outreach volume, follow-up rhythm, evidence quality, speed to close) and to read the causes outside reach as signals, not as excuses. A "lucky" deal is a cause you did not see; a lost deal is a cause you did not place.

**Gender — "Gender is in everything; everything has its Masculine and Feminine Principles."** The most misread of the seven, and the Kybalion is explicit: gender here means generation or creation, not sex — a philosophical polarity of a projective, generative principle and a receptive principle, present in all things on all planes, not a statement about human gender identity, roles, or the superiority of either pole. Every act of creation needs both: a force that directs and a force that receives. The wholesaler's version is the push and the pull of a deal. The push (projective): the outreach, the offer, the lock — the wholesaler goes out, finds the seller, puts a number and a date on the table, and drives the deal to a close. The pull (receptive): the listening, the waiting, the letting the seller's real reason surface, the letting the buyer's criteria volunteer themselves — the deal develops because the wholesaler received what the other side actually said. A deal closed by push alone is pressure, and it fails or poisons the relationship; a deal attempted by pull alone never closes, because nobody ever put the offer on the table. The two show up inside every conversation: push the outreach, pull the information; push the offer, pull the seller's timeline; push the assignment to the buyer, pull the buyer's proof of funds and criteria. The same balance governs the wholesaler's own work: generation (finding deals, building lists, making calls) and reception (reading the market, hearing the seller, learning from a lost deal). Creation — a closed assignment — happens only where both are engaged.

**How the four laws work together in one deal.** Cause and Effect finds the deal (a distress signal is a cause; the below-market sale is its effect). Polarity reads it (the same parcel is a burden to the seller and an opportunity to the buyer; flip the pole, keep the facts). Rhythm times it (the seller's motivation is on its downswing; the market's swing is turning; the follow-up lands now). Gender closes it (push the offer, pull the seller's real reason and the buyer's real criteria — and the deal is generated only when both poles are working). A wholesaler who holds all four at once, with the first three laws from topic 4, stops chasing and starts causing: the deal is found in the mind, recognized by the pattern, caught on the timing, read at both poles, traced to its cause, and closed by the balance of push and pull.

**Sources**

- Grimoire.love, "The Kybalion and the Seven Hermetic Principles" — https://www.grimoire.love/encyclopedia/kybalion-seven-hermetic-principles/
- Arcane Library, "The Kybalion — Seven Hermetic Principles" — https://arcanelibrary.org/kybalion/

### 4. The laws I — Mentalism, Correspondence, Vibration (studied 2026-11-20)
**Provenance first, because the program's rule is proof or mark it unverified.** The seven Hermetic principles do not come from ancient Egypt. They come from one book, *The Kybalion* (Chicago, 1908), usually attributed to William Walker Atkinson, a writer of the New Thought movement that also produced the law of attraction. No classical Hermetic text lists seven principles. Two of the seven have real older roots: mentalism's divine Mind echoes Nous in the Corpus Hermeticum (treatises I and XI), and correspondence paraphrases the Emerald Tablet (first attested in Arabic, 8th–9th century). Vibration has no Hermetic source — it is the vocabulary of 1900s physics and New Thought. None of the seven is scientific; read as laws of nature, none of them holds up, but read as metaphors for attention, habit, and pattern, several are useful. That is how they are used here: as operating lenses for a wholesaler, not as claims about physics.

**Mentalism — "The All is Mind; the Universe is Mental."** The deal starts in the mind: the seller's picture of their problem, the buyer's picture of value. Nothing in a wholesale transaction happens before someone pictures it — the seller pictures relief from a burden, the buyer pictures a finished project worth more than the price, the wholesaler pictures the gap between the two and the fee for closing it. The practical discipline is identity before tactics: the wholesaler who pictures themselves as a problem-solver who brings certainty finds different conversations than the one who pictures themselves as a bargain-hunter. On the seller side, the work is to understand the picture the seller already holds — what they believe the property is worth, what they fear, what "done" looks like to them — because the offer is judged against that picture, not against the wholesaler's spreadsheet. The anchor-reset sequence from topic 2 is mentalism in practice: you do not fight the seller's picture, you replace it with current evidence. The honest limit: a mental picture does not move a market. It moves what you notice, what you offer, and how you speak — and those move outcomes.

**Correspondence — "As above, so below; as below, so above."** As above, so below: one lot mirrors the market; one seller's reason mirrors a thousand. The same structural pattern repeats at every scale. The Fresno lots are the working example: four parcels, four different owners, but one pattern — bought cheap, held for years, no use, low tax, no income — and that pattern recurs in every off-market list. Learn it once from one lot and the next sweep reads faster: the signal stack (topic 1) is correspondence applied to lists. It works in the other direction too: the market's condition shows up inside a single deal — a rising market shows up as a seller who will not discount, a falling market as a buyer who will not pay the fee. The practical discipline is pattern transfer: when a deal works or fails, write down which level the lesson belongs to — the parcel, the seller, the street, the city, the market — and check whether it holds at the other levels before generalizing. The trap is false correspondence: assuming one lot's story is every lot's story. The Fresno work already showed this — county data said R-1 for 333 and 433 while city GIS said CMX — so the pattern is a hypothesis to verify per parcel, never a conclusion.

**Vibration — "Nothing rests; everything moves; everything vibrates."** Everything moves: motivation rises and falls; timing is a frequency; catch the seller on the downswing of holding. A seller's willingness to sell is not a fixed number; it moves with tax bills, seasons, family events, auction dates, and the slow accumulation of holding costs. The four Fresno lots sat because nobody ever asked — and because the owners' motivation had not yet fallen far enough to meet a low offer. The practical discipline is rhythm of contact, not one call: a first contact that plants the idea, a follow-up after the next tax bill, another after a season of vacancy. Each touch lands on a different vibration — a different state of the seller's mind — and the deal closes when the seller's downswing meets the wholesaler's offer. The same applies to the wholesaler's own state: energy, focus, and clarity move, and the quality of a call made tired and scattered is measurably worse than one made rested and clear. Track your own state as closely as your metrics; a bad state is a cost, not a mood. The honest limit: vibration is a metaphor for change over time, not a frequency you can measure. What is measurable is the record — dates of contact, tax-bill dates, days vacant, market direction — and the record is what tells you when to call again.

**How the three laws work together in one deal.** Mentalism sets the picture (what the seller wants, what the buyer will pay, what the gap is worth). Correspondence reads the pattern (this lot is like the last lot that closed; this seller's reason is the same reason the last three sellers gave). Vibration sets the timing (the seller's motivation is falling; the market's swing is turning; call now, not last month, not next quarter). A wholesaler who holds all three at once stops chasing — the deal is found in the mind, recognized by the pattern, and closed on the timing.

Flag (not a correction): the Hermetic School page maps vibration and cause-and-effect to Corpus Hermeticum XII as a "closest parallel," where the Hermeticum page says neither has a Hermetic source. The corpus follows the Hermeticum's stricter reading.

### 6. Oneness + Mac's law of causality + the gap math (studied 2026-11-20)
**Provenance first.** Oneness is not one of the seven principles in *The Kybalion* (1908) — it comes from the modern "twelve universal laws" expansion, where it is listed first as the Law of Divine Oneness: everything is connected, part of one whole, every thought and action rippling through the field. Its older roots are real but separate: the Upanishads' "tat tvam asi" (you are that), the Stoics' Logos, the Hermetic idea of reality as a single mental organism, and the Tao. It is not scientific; like the seven, it is used here as an operating lens, not a claim about physics. Mac's law of causality is Mac's own formulation, not a published law — "see a need, fill the need, get paid for the needs you filled — exponentially larger, because the gap grows without what you are providing, doing, and servicing." The gap math is the industry's own practice, formalized as the MAO (Maximum Allowable Offer) — and the three fit together as one system: Oneness says the market is one connected whole, Mac's law says the fee is earned by filling a need inside it, and the gap math says the size of the need is measurable in dollars.

**[RESTORED 2026-11-20 — the original tail of this section was lost in the run-7 overwrite and is unrecoverable verbatim. What follows was restored from the sources (Real Estate Skills MAO guide fetched live in full, plus the fee-band sources) and verified 2026-11-20; it is verified content in the corpus's voice, not the original text. The 403 flag text is ledger-verified verbatim from run 6.]**

The gap math, in its standard form: MAO = (70% × ARV) − repair costs − wholesale fee. The MAO is the ceiling — the highest price that still leaves the buyer their margin and the wholesaler their fee — and every dollar negotiated below it is profit, every dollar above it profit destroyed. The 70% is a filter, not a law: it quietly bundles the buyer's fixed costs, holding costs, closing costs, and profit into one number, and the long form spells it out as MAO = ARV − fixed costs − repair costs − wholesale fee − desired profit. **[VERIFIED — guide: MAO = (70% × ARV) − Repair Costs − Wholesale Fee; long form MAO = ARV − Fixed Costs − Repair Costs − Wholesale Fee − Desired Profit; the 30% covers financing, front-end closing, holding (taxes, insurance, utilities), back-end closing and commissions, and profit.]**

Calibrate the percentage by market and deal type — the guide's table: 80–85% in a strong seller's market, 70% balanced, 65–68% in a buyer's or softening market, 65% where rehab costs are high, 75–80% in high-demand urban markets, 60–65% in rural or tertiary markets — and never let the formula substitute for the buyer-criteria check: what will the actual buyer in this market pay, and what fee will they tolerate? **[VERIFIED — guide's market-condition table. The reconstructed tail's compressed version ("higher where homes sell fast… lower on heavy rehabs and slow markets") is consistent with the table.]**

The guide's pro method is the buyer-criteria check in procedure, in three moves: first, know the cash buyer's criteria before any offer — the minimum net profit in dollars they need and the minimum deal ROI percentage, both of which have to clear; second, start with the end in mind — ARV first, subtract the real costs the buyer will carry, and the price where the buyer still hits their criteria, minus your fee, is the offer; third, adjust to win — re-check the repair estimate, and flex your own fee before touching the buyer's margin. The worked proof is a San Diego property: the strict 70% rule gives a $452,500 offer that loses; reverse-engineering from the buyer's real criteria supports ~$560,000 — over $100,000 higher — with the same $20,000 fee, and wins. Two wholesalers, same property, same fee; one offers on autopilot and loses, one offers on purpose and wins. **[VERIFIED — guide's "Pro Method: Reverse-Engineer From Your Buyer" section and San Diego example. This is new detail the reconstructed tail did not carry.]**

Mac's law of causality prices the fee by the gap, not by the spread: the value of the fill is the size of the problem that would have grown without the service — the holding costs compounding, the tax bill arriving, the property deteriorating, the seller's time and certainty lost. That is the honest measure of the fee, and it is also its limit: a fee larger than the gap the service actually filled is not value, it is extraction, and the buyer relationship it burns is worth more than the fee. **[CORPUS REASONING — Mac's law is Mac's own formulation (section 6 opening, verbatim). Adjacent guide guidance supports the discipline: "It's better to make $5,000 on a deal that closes than $10,000 on a deal that dies. Momentum beats greed, especially early," and the fee-flexing lever (drop a $10,000 fee to $5,000 to raise the offer $5,000). The "extraction" judgment is the loop's own, not a source claim.]**

The gap math cuts both ways on the contested 403 numbers, and run 6 flagged it: if the buyer pays ~$65,000 and the contract is $23,000, the spread (~$42,000, ~65% of the buyer price) sits far outside the normal 5–15% fee band. Verify what a cash buyer will actually pay for a CMX lot before locking a price that implies a fee outside the market's own range. Oneness is the reason the check matters: a fee that breaks the buyer's math does not stay inside one deal — it poisons the buyer pool the next deal depends on, because the market is one connected system and it talks. **[VERIFIED — arithmetic: $42,000 ÷ $65,000 = 64.6%. The 5–15% band is now verified from three live sources: OpenHouseLab ("a flat fee that typically ranges from 5% to 15% of the market value"), Real Estate Skills' assignment-fee guide ("often 5% or 15%"), and ibuyhaus 2025 analysis (standard 5%–10%; under-$50k properties 6%–10%). The flag text is ledger-verified verbatim. The oneness reasoning is corpus reasoning from the section's own verbatim opening.]**

### 7. Property types beyond lots — SFR, small multifamily, commercial, mixed-use; MAO and assignment per type (studied 2026-11-20)
The wholesale equation is the same shape at every size — contract below market, assign, keep the spread — but the math that sets "below market," the buyer who pays, the time it takes, and the paperwork that closes it all change with the property type. Copying the house formula onto a building is the mistake that costs the most.

**Single-family (SFR) — the baseline.** Value is set by comparable sales and after-repair value (ARV). The standard MAO is MAO = (70% × ARV) − repair costs − wholesale fee; the 70% is a quick filter, not a final answer — in fast, high-priced markets buyers accept a higher percentage, on heavy rehabs or slow markets a lower one, and the long form spells out what the 70% hides: MAO = ARV − fixed costs − repair costs − wholesale fee − desired profit. Typical assignment fee $3,000–$15,000 (a first deal at $3,000–$5,000 is a fine win; $10,000–$15,000 becomes normal with relationships). Due diligence 14–30 days. End buyer: individual flippers and small landlords, often cash or hard money. Contract: short purchase agreement. Exit: assignment is the default; double close when the contract bars assignment, the spread is large enough that its visibility would cause friction, or the end buyer's lender will not accept an assigned contract. Novation (replacing the original contract with a new one, seller's written consent, original contract voided) fits sellers who want a retail price and can wait for a retail timeline, with gross spreads of $30,000–$60,000 realistic on the right property — but the fee must be secured by a separate written agreement before signing, because once the novation is signed you are not a party to the contract anymore; and it is the exit most exposed to licensing questions, since you are marketing the property itself. Wholetailing (buy, light cosmetic work, sell at retail through an agent) fits houses needing paint, flooring, and cleanup rather than structural work.

**Land — the type this loop started with.** The 70% rule collapses here: for low-value land, practitioners drop the percentage to as low as 10%–30% of value, because there is no ARV to renovate toward — value is what a cash buyer will pay for the dirt, the entitlement, and the use. The Fresno work already showed this: the contested 403 numbers turn on MAO from recent cash sales, not on a percentage of a renovated value. Land comps follow the same discipline as house comps with one difference: the first acre usually carries most of the value and each added acre is worth less. When the lot looks worth more than the house on it, pull vacant-lot and new-construction sales as well.

**Small multifamily (2–4 units) — the bridge type.** Still residential financing (FHA, conventional, VA; owner-occupant as little as 5% down, investor typically 25%), but valued partly as a business. Appraisers use Fannie Mae Form 1025, which weighs the income approach alongside sales comps and captures rental income unit by unit — so the rent roll affects value in a way a single-family home's never does. The buyer pool splits in two: owner-occupant house-hackers and investors, who value the same building differently. Treat a small multifamily like one big single-family home and you make the most expensive mistake in this category: price on NOI and cap rate, not just comps. The wholesaler's MAO here has to satisfy both tests — the flip test (70% × ARV minus repairs minus fee) and the income test (does the rent support the buyer's price at the market cap rate, after vacancy, management at 8–10% of collected rent, taxes, insurance, maintenance). Vacancy risk shrinks with units: one tenant leaving a fourplex leaves it 75% occupied; a single-family vacancy is 100% vacant. Over-improving backfires: granite and designer finishes thrill an owner-occupant and do little for an investor who prices off income. Typical prices run $200,000–$800,000 depending on market; a Central Valley example puts 2–4 units at $420,000–$600,000 with $1,450–$1,800 per unit per month and gross yields around 5.5–7%.

**Five units is a cliff.** A fourplex gets residential financing; a five-unit building needs a commercial loan with 20–35% down and income-based underwriting. Everything below this line is a different business: longer timelines, institutional buyers, and income valuation only.

**Commercial (5+ units, office, retail, industrial, self-storage, mobile home parks) — a different business.** Value = NOI ÷ cap rate. NOI is gross income minus vacancy and operating expenses, before debt service. A building producing $120,000 of NOI at a 7.5% cap rate is worth about $1.6 million; at 9.5% the same building is worth $2.1 million — a 150-basis-point move on the same NOI produces a $400,000 swing, so a wholesaler quoting a contract price must know the going cap rate for that asset class and submarket before negotiating with either party. Rebuild the NOI yourself using a realistic vacancy rate and a management fee even if the owner self-manages. The wholesale math becomes: contract price + assignment fee + any rehab or capex the buyer must deploy ≤ the value the income approach supports at exit. Typical assignment fee $25,000 to six figures, occasionally seven. Due diligence 60–90 days, sometimes longer — Phase I environmental, engineering, lease audits, tenant credit, zoning. End buyers: syndicators, family offices, REITs, private equity, 1031 exchange buyers, local operators. Contract style: letter of intent first, then a longer purchase and sale agreement; the seller is often an LLC, partnership, or trust with several decision-makers. Financing: bank, agency, or private debt with lender approvals. Small multifamily (5–50 units) is the most common entry point; mixed-use (retail ground floor, apartments above) is common in older downtowns; self-storage and mobile home parks are popular with individual investors and often owned by long-time owners nearing retirement; office is the hardest category right now (Class A suburban office cap rates 10–12.5% in major Midwest markets in the first half of 2026, the most bearish outlook in lower-quality office). Distress here has its own signals: loan maturity walls (about $930 billion in commercial loans set to mature in 2026, at least $126 billion classified as distressed at end of 2025), sector dislocations, CMBS watchlists and special servicing transfers, tax delinquency. Lead sources: direct outreach, public records, CMBS watchlists, broker relationships (pocket listings matter far more than in residential), attorneys, lenders, accountants, property managers.

**Mixed-use — two valuations in one building.** The retail ground floor is valued on its lease income and the apartments above on their rents; the two halves have different cap rates, different tenants, different risks. Underwrite each half separately and never let a strong retail lease hide weak residential rents, or vice versa. Zoning and use compliance matter more here than in any other type: verify the commercial use is legal and conforming, because a nonconforming use can kill the buyer's financing.

**Assignment mechanics per type.** Assignment (sell your rights under the purchase contract for a fee) is the default everywhere: one closing, minimal cost, earnest money typically returned or credited — but the fee usually shows on the settlement paperwork, the contract must permit assignment ("and/or assigns" after your name, confirmed rather than assumed), and the end buyer's lender or title company may object. Double close (buy, then sell back-to-back, often the same day) costs more — funding for the first purchase (own cash or transactional lender) plus closing costs on both legs — and if the second closing fails after the first has funded, you own a property you did not plan to hold; but your margin stays private, and it works when assignment is barred or the buyer's lender will not accept an assigned contract. It also puts you in the chain of title as a principal, a different legal posture. Novation replaces the contract entirely with the seller's written consent; the original is voided and you walk away with no further liability — and no further claim, so the fee must be secured separately first. Wholetailing means taking title and doing light work. The choice is a decision tree: clean title + motivated seller + buyer waiting + quick close = assignment; large visible spread, non-assignable contract, or lender refusal = double close; seller wants retail price, can wait, house in decent shape, buyer needs conventional financing = novation; light cosmetic work and funding in hand = wholetail.

**Protecting the earnest money.** It is usually the wholesaler's only real cash at risk. The clauses that matter: assignment language, an inspection or due diligence contingency long enough for the end buyer's environmental and engineering work in commercial deals, a financing contingency (matters most on a double close), a clean title contingency, and realistic timelines with extension provisions — commercial due diligence runs 60–90 days or longer, and a window too short forces a close or a forfeiture before an assignment is ready.

**Licensing — the line that moves by type.** Marketing the contract (your equitable interest, the right to purchase) is generally legal without a real estate license; marketing the property itself, as if you owned it or represented the seller, can constitute unlicensed brokerage and is regulated in most states. Most state wholesaling laws apply only to residential property — Connecticut, Maryland, North Dakota, Oklahoma, and Tennessee enacted new wholesaling laws during 2025, joining earlier statutes in states including Illinois, Pennsylvania, North Carolina, Kentucky, and South Carolina — but a small number have begun extending disclosure and licensing rules to commercial transactions, so the commercial wholesaler is mostly outside the scope of state wholesaling laws, with exceptions. Novation is the most exposed exit because it markets the property itself. Have a commercial attorney review the contract before signing a first commercial deal.

**Fresno application.** The four CMX lots are land, so the 70% rule does not apply — the buyer-minus-contract gap must be tested against what a cash buyer will actually pay for a CMX lot (the $50,000–$90,000 band, middle ~$67,500), not against a renovated value. The 403 contest from run 6 stands: if the buyer pays ~$65,000 and the contract is $23,000, the ~$42,000 spread is ~65% of the buyer price, far outside the normal 5–15% fee band for land and houses — and far outside the $3,000–$15,000 residential fee range. The next step up the ladder is a 2–4 unit building in the same market: same distress signals, same outreach, but the MAO must pass both the comp test and the rent test, and the buyer pool doubles (house-hacker plus investor). Commercial is a later rung, not a first deal: 60–120 day timelines, institutional buyers, and cap-rate math do not fit a $1,000-by-Sunday clock.
