### 8. Competitor methods — Brent Daniels / TTP "Talk To People" (studied 2026-11-20)
Pipeline 369 and Zach Ginn / Flip with Rick are already absorbed. This run takes the next name in the wider field: Brent Daniels, founder of the TTP (Talk To People) cold-calling program inside Wholesaling Inc. His volume claims (over a quarter-billion dollars in deals; 90 leads a week at his peak) are his own, repeated in interviews — not independently verified here, and the method is judged on its mechanics, not its marketing numbers.

**The core idea — be proactive, never wait.** The whole method reduces to one sentence: talk to people. When it comes to sourcing a deal, a wholesaler has three choices — wait for sellers to call (referrals, inbound), pay for sellers to call (marketing that buys leads), or pick up the phone and call distressed owners directly. TTP chooses the third. The reasoning is control: whoever sources the deal gets to choose what to do with it — wholesale it, flip it, or hold it — and the phone is the cheapest way to manufacture that control. The simple secret, as Daniels puts it, is consistent quality conversations with distressed sellers, cash buyers, agents, local title companies, and anyone else who can help; if those conversations keep happening, the business does not fail. The discipline is volume plus depth: his phone battery dying twice a day from talking to buyers, sellers, and agents is the image he uses for the work rate. The honest check: cold calling only works where it is lawful — DNC and TCPA limits apply (already flagged in topic 1), and accurate numbers matter more than raw dials.

**The four pillars of a seller conversation.** Every seller call revolves around four things, and the deal is not real until all four are pulled out: (1) **condition** of the property, (2) **timeline** to sell, (3) **motivation** to sell, and (4) **price**. The method's central warning is about speed: a five-minute conversation that jumps to an offer mostly ends in a cancellation. The best calls in his team's recordings run thirty to forty-five minutes — deep discovery, letting the seller open up. The technique is the opposite of a hard script: do not rush, do not sound like a robot, do not run down a checklist. The vocal first impression is decided in the first five seconds — sound like someone the seller has talked to before, not like a telemarketer. Ask the question, then shut up and listen; have no agenda beyond the four pillars. Tone of voice carries the call. One operational detail: when a seller agrees to a deal and then ghosts after the contract is sent, the failure is usually a shallow discovery — the seller was never really sold, only hurried.

**The ethics line, stated by the practitioner himself.** When a seller shows signs of mental illness or incapacity — his example is a seller who says the police pulled her to the station and mentions grandchildren — get the family involved. Do not trick people who obviously have problems: it is gross, it destroys the operator, and it leaves them looking over their shoulder. If the whole family, the attorney, or the agent is not on board, the deal will haunt the wholesaler. This is the same line the corpus already holds (topic 1: only buy from people who have to sell; topic 3: no covert manipulation) — here it is the method's own guardrail, and it is kept.

**The buyer list is half the business.** Most wholesalers spend everything on the seller side and neglect the buyer side; TTP treats the buyer database as a parallel build. Once a buyer is on the list, call them and ask how they operate: do they buy sight unseen, what would make them confident buying without seeing the property, what are they looking for, and how do they want deals brought to them. The sources for buyers: fix-and-flip groups on Facebook (join and participate, don't just lurk), local meetups, every title company in the area (tell them who you are and how many deals you can bring, then ask who is buying), private lenders the same way, and other investors — swap leads and grow together, because there is enough for everyone. This matches rule 20 from topic 7 (build the buyer list before the contract) and gives it a procedure: the buyer volunteers their criteria when you hold the relationship, and the deal sheet is then built to match.

**The marketing mix and speed to lead.** Cold calling is the volume engine; pay-per-click is the second channel, catching people actively looking for a solution right now. The rule that governs both: answer live when a lead calls, or reach out immediately after a form is filled in. Speed to lead is the multiplier — a text alert the moment a lead arrives, and if you cannot always answer the phone, hire someone who can. The money made in three months is based on what is done today; pulling back marketing in a slow market is how fly-by-night operators disappear while the proactive ones double down.

**Agent referrals — be the guy who gets things done.** Agent relationships are a major channel. The positioning: be the person in town who buys the ugly properties and the damaged properties and can put a cash offer to their seller almost immediately. Agents do not want a lot of offers; they want one person they know can close. To reach them, check the MLS or Zillow for agents representing damaged homes owned by motivated sellers — the cash offer may be exactly what that listing needs. Revenue from agent referrals is described as very large relative to the effort, because one trusted agent can feed deals repeatedly.

**Niche lists and dead leads — the advanced plays.** Two moves from his students show the method extending beyond standard distress lists. First, niche public-record lists: fire-damaged homes and water shutoffs, pulled by filing a Freedom of Information Act request with the local fire marshal — a lawful public-records route to a list few wholesalers pull. The questions on those calls are specific to the damage (structural, not cosmetic), and the warning is sharp: do not redesign a floor plan without a general-contracting background, or the loss is guaranteed. Second, other wholesalers' dead leads: one pair of students built a pipeline by asking wholesalers in Facebook groups for their dead pay-per-click leads — leads that were dead for wholesaling — and converting them into novations, including a $63,000 novation in a small West Virginia town. The insight: a lead is only dead for the exit its owner tried; a novation (retail price, retail timeline, seller waits) can revive it. A $50,000 novation in a first-six-months case study ($212,000 generated in six months by a new wholesaler, per the show's account — unverified here) shows the same pattern. This connects directly to topic 7's novation mechanics and its warning: the fee must be secured by a separate written agreement before signing, because after the novation you are not a party to the contract.

**The business-growth audit — a second set of four pillars.** In a later episode Daniels and Cody Hofhine describe a different four pillars for scaling the business itself, in strict order: Marketing, Sales, Team, Leadership. Diagnose and fix in that sequence; grinding harder on the wrong pillar does not grow the business. The supporting practices: daily standards over income goals, quarterly audits of the four pillars, and the 1-3-1 leadership rule — team members bring one problem, three solutions, and one recommendation, so they solve their own problems instead of turning the operator into a full-time firefighter. The operational corollary from another episode: track backend KPIs, and review sales calls daily — call review is described as the ultimate training tool for an acquisitions team. The method also works fully virtual: one featured virtual wholesaler closed 600+ deals without ever seeing the properties, running the whole acquisition process by phone.

**What TTP adds to this loop, and what it does not.** It adds a complete outbound engine the loop's Fresno work does not yet have: the Fresno avenue so far is seller-first calls to four known lots plus a 50-target list, with scripts written for a first approach. TTP's contribution is the call itself — the four pillars as the discovery checklist, the 30–45-minute depth standard, the vocal first impression, the no-agenda listening, and the buyer-list procedure that makes the assignment real. It also adds the niche-list idea (fire and water-shutoff lists via public records) and the dead-lead novation play, both lawful and both testable against the Fresno market. What it does not add: it does not solve the $1,000-by-Sunday clock — cold calling is a volume game measured in weeks, and the loop's honest flag stands (study does not bank money; the $350 texts and the Fresno seller calls are the cash levers). It also does not change the contested 403 numbers: the fee must still fit the buyer's math (topics 6–7). One caution carried forward: TTP is a paid training program (TTP Insider, Wholesaling Inc coaching) — this study used only its free public interviews and show notes; no trial was accepted, no card entered, and no paid material was used.

- Flipping Junkie, "221: Be Proactive: Talk To People" (Brent Daniels; the six responses to cold calls; the Motivated Seller Journey checklist) — https://flippingjunkie.com/221-be-proactive-talk-to-people/
- Wholesaling Inc with Brent Daniels, WIP 2128 (four pillars: condition, timeline, motivation, price; deep-discovery call recordings; the ethics line on vulnerable sellers) — https://podscan.fm/podcasts/wholesaling-inc-with-brent-daniels
- Wholesaling Inc, WIP 2130: "Why Your Wholesaling Business Isn't Growing (And How to Fix It)" (four-pillar business audit: Marketing, Sales, Team, Leadership; 1-3-1 rule) — https://moon.fm/itunes/1051156990
- Wholesaling Inc, WIP 2129: "How I Closed 600+ Wholesale Deals Without Ever Seeing the Properties" (cold calling, fire-damage and water-shutoff lists via FOIA, virtual wholesaling) — https://moon.fm/itunes/1051156990
- Wholesaling Inc, WIP 2125: "'Your Offer Is Too Low!' Here's What to Say Next" (top five seller objections; re-anchoring by pricing repairs) — https://podcast.app/wholesaling-inc-with-brent-daniels-p2334
- Wholesaling Inc, WIP 2124: "The Cold Calling System That Generated $212K in Six Months for a Brand New Wholesaler" (dead-lead novations; KPIs; daily call review) — https://podcast.app/wholesaling-inc-with-brent-daniels-p2334
- Wholesaling Inc, WIP 1868: "How Two Teenagers Got 20 Deals in 6 Weeks" (dead PPC leads from other wholesalers converted to novations; $63,000 Ripley, WV novation) — https://podscan.fm/podcasts/wholesaling-inc-with-brent-daniels/episodes/wip-1868-how-two-teenagers-got-20-deals-in-6-weeks-while-youre-making-excuses

### 9. Deal-finding — spotting below-market sellers free (studied 2026-11-20; expanded 2026-11-20 by three-researcher synthesis — supersedes run 9's version, written under the old single-threaded body)

**Corpus section 9 — researched 2026-11-20.** Method: three parallel researchers (public records; field and list-building methods; online sources and human workarounds) plus direct searches, so no single pass carried the topic. Public, free sources only — browser search and live pages. Summarized entirely in my own words; no text copied from any source. No paywall, login, or bot check was bypassed; no trial accepted, no one contacted, nothing sent, signed, spent, or published. Where a "free" claim rests only on a vendor's own page or a secondary roundup, that is stated, not smoothed over. Rules are notes until tested on real replies or dollars.

**TIME-SENSITIVE FLAG — Fresno, Mac's market**

Fresno County's tax-sale re-offer runs **Monday 23 November 2026, online on RealAuction, 26 parcels, opening bids $2,200–$86,400, 19 of them vacant land** in one count. The main 2026 sale was 10–11 September 2026 (161 parcels). Redemption for the re-offer ended **Friday 20 November 2026 at 5:00 p.m. PT — today**. Deposit to bid is reported at $5,000, by ACH/wire only. Unsold parcels are re-offered, often at a reduced minimum under Revenue & Taxation Code §3698.5; 13 Fresno parcels drew no bid in the last 12 months in one count. Paywalled copies (TaxSalesHQ / Properant) lock the full address list behind Pro at $24.99/mo — the county plus RealAuction list is the free source. This is reported, not acted on; bidding needs cash and a deposit and is a different business from wholesaling.

**The core principle**

The raw record is free at the county or city that created it. Paid data vendors (PropStream, BatchLeads, PropertyRadar, Foreclosure.com, Lispend, PropertyShark) resell the same filings, cleaned and skip-traced. The free edge is therefore not the list itself — everyone can pull it — it is speed, cleaning, stacking signals, and honest, lawful outreach. A wholesaler still needs four things on every lead before offering: a reason to sell fast, condition justifying a discount, enough equity to sell below market, and a signer with legal authority under an assignable contract.

**1. The two channels**

**On-market** means filtering listing sites for distress signals — price cuts, long days on market, fixer-upper language. **Off-market** means public records — foreclosure filings, code violations, probate, tax delinquency. Both channels have free versions. Paid data and direct mail run real money per lead, and the number that matters is cost per deal, which can run 20 to 40 times higher. The standard advice is to buy a list — absentee owners, high equity, pre-foreclosure — but so does everyone else in the market, and the same homeowner then gets four postcards in a month. The leads worth having are the ones found yourself, in plain sight: a price cut on a listing sitting 90 days, a foreclosure notice that became public record two weeks ago, a code-violation lien recorded against a title.

**2. On-market free filtering**

**Redfin — the strongest free filter set found.** Time on Redfin (e.g. more than 90 days), Price reduced (e.g. in the last 7 days), and keyword search (investor, as-is, fixer, handyman, TLC). One demonstrated search (Cleveland, October 2026) combined 90+ days plus a price cut in the last 7 days plus the keyword investor and returned 17 homes, mostly $85,000–$110,000. Redfin also publishes free downloadable market data (price drops, delistings, relistings, cancellations) in its Data Center.

**Zillow — no expired filter.** Multiple guides confirm this. What Zillow does give, free: days on Zillow (filter where shown, otherwise visible per listing — the watch-list threshold agents use is 60+ days in fast markets, 90+ days in slow ones); price cuts (visible per listing and in price history); and off-market / listing-removed events in price history — a listing that sat long, cut price, then disappeared without a sold event is the expired candidate. Method: pick one ZIP, save the stale listings (favorites / saved search / email alerts), re-check weekly for disappearances, confirm in price history, then look the owner up on the county assessor. Never assume disappearance means expiration — it can be a withdrawal, a pause, or a data lag.

**Realtor.com** has days-on-market and price-reduced filters, but a 2025–2026 community report notes they have been unreliable (days-on-market showing 6+ month-old listings regardless of setting; price-reduced returning listings that went up) — cross-check Realtor.com hits on Redfin.

**MLS expired/withdrawn:** the clean Expired status exists only in the MLS, which requires a license or an agent partner. The free workarounds are the Zillow watch-list method above, or asking a friendly agent for an expired pull. No free public feed of MLS expireds was found.

**FSBO sources — free to search.** Zillow FSBO: free for owners to post (90 days, extendable, unlimited photos plus video); searchers use the By owner (FSBO) / By owner (other) filter — FSBOs sit under a separate Other listings tab, easy to miss. Facebook Marketplace: free to browse and post, hyperlocal, fast turnover, but heavily mixed with rental scams and stale listings — verify address and seller against the county assessor before any meeting. Craigslist: free for most housing posts ($5 for commercial real estate, services, and dealer car posts — check the fee page before posting in a paid category); skews toward investment properties and starter homes. ForSaleByOwner.com: free to post a basic listing (per a November 2026 comparison); FSBO.com has a $0 Starter tier (1 photo, 30 days) with paid upgrades. Nextdoor and yard signs with a QR code are also named as free channels. None of the free sites put a home on the MLS; MLS access runs $85–$399 upfront via flat-fee services. Context: about 6% of 2024 sales were FSBO (NAR), and FSBOs sold at a median $380,000 versus $435,000 agent-assisted — FSBO sellers already accept a lower number, which is the wholesaler's opening.

**3. Off-market public-record lists**

Each list lives in a different office. All are reachable free or for copy fees under a state's public-records law — **not federal FOIA**. Federal FOIA covers federal agencies only (HUD, IRS). Local records come under each state's own law — Public Records Act, Open Records Act, Right-to-Know, Sunshine Law, or OPRA depending on the state. Cite the state law when asking a county or city. Response times are set by state law, commonly 5–10 business days, with extensions allowed. Copy fees are usually small (often under $25); many counties will email a spreadsheet for free if you ask for a report they already run.

**Tax-delinquent lists.** A roll of parcels whose owners have not paid property taxes — address, owner name, parcel/APN, mailing address, amount owed, years delinquent. The classic motivated-seller list, because the owner faces losing the property for a debt often far smaller than its value. Where: county treasurer / tax collector / tax commissioner (the office that collects taxes, not the assessor who values property). Search "[county] delinquent tax list" or "[county] tax sale list." Many post a PDF or spreadsheet before a sale. State revenue or attorney-general sites aggregate in some states (Florida publishes tax-sale information at state level; Texas does not — county by county). Newspaper legal notices: most states require the delinquent/sale list to be published in a local paper for several weeks before a sale. By records request: ask for all real property parcels with unpaid ad valorem taxes for any tax year, as of the most recent update, in spreadsheet format, with parcel number, property address, owner name, mailing address, amount owed, and years delinquent — and ask for a fee estimate first. Examples found: Cook County, IL Treasurer posts delinquent lists by ward/municipality/township, flagging undeliverable mail; its tax-year 2023 properties were eligible for an annual tax sale scheduled 15 December 2026. Wayne County, MI Treasurer publishes yearly foreclosure listings as PDFs back to 2011. Maricopa County, AZ sells some data files through a research-material order form — a paid exception to check before assuming free. Freshness: monthly to quarterly on assessor/collector sites; sale lists often published weeks to months before the sale date. How used: contact the owner before the auction and offer a fast cash sale that pays off the back taxes. Filter for multiple years delinquent, out-of-area mailing address, and low amounts owed relative to value — and verify the debt is still unpaid, since owners cure delinquencies constantly. Lien-vs-deed matters less for wholesaling than for tax-sale bidding: in a lien state the county sells a lien certificate; in a deed state it eventually auctions the property. Either way the wholesaler's play is pre-auction, with the owner.

**Pre-foreclosure notices — Notice of Default / lis pendens / Notice of Trustee Sale.** The first public step of foreclosure. In non-judicial (deed-of-trust) states the lender/trustee records a Notice of Default with the county recorder, then later a Notice of Trustee Sale setting the auction date. In judicial states the lender files a lawsuit and records a lis pendens — notice that litigation affecting title is pending. In a few states (Connecticut, Maine, Vermont) strict foreclosure applies. Missed payments themselves are private for at least the first ~120 days; only the recorded notice makes it public. Where: county recorder / register of deeds / county clerk — free online official-records / public-records search, usually by grantor-grantee index (owner name) or parcel number. Search by owner name and parcel, not street address — recorder indexes often do not search by address. Look for document types: Notice of Default, Lis Pendens, Notice of Trustee Sale / Notice of Foreclosure Sale. Court docket / clerk of courts for judicial foreclosures and lis pendens. State-by-state: California records NOD first, then NOTS at least 20 days before auction, published weekly for three weeks and posted on the property. Arizona records the notice of sale, sale no sooner than the 91st day after. Nevada records a Notice of Breach and Election to Sell. Colorado files with the county public trustee (Notice of Election and Demand). Texas records no NOD at all — the notice of sale is posted at the courthouse door and filed with the county clerk at least 21 days before sale. Newspaper publication of the notice of sale (required in most states) and the trustee/sheriff sale calendar. Freshness: daily to weekly — recorded the day it is filed. This is the fastest-moving list and the most competitive: every investor in the county can pull the same filings the same day. How used: the window between NOD/lis pendens and auction is the deal window — the owner can still sell, cure, or do a short sale. Non-judicial foreclosures can move in months; judicial ones can take 2–3 years. The edge is reading the filing correctly for the state, cleaning out cured/reinstated loans, and contacting the owner early with all options, not just a low offer. Compliance warning found repeatedly: about 25 states regulate foreclosure consultants (e.g. California Civil Code 2945, New York Real Property Law 265-a, plus Maryland, Minnesota, Florida variants). Never use language like help you avoid or stop foreclosure or rescue your home — use language about helping the owner understand their options if selling is one of them. Never imply you are a rescue service. Check the state statute before calling this list.

**Probate filings.** The court-supervised settling of a deceased owner's estate. Filings name the decedent, the personal representative/executor, the heirs, and the property in the estate. Heirs — often out of state, paying carrying costs on a dated, unmaintained house they do not want — are among the highest-converting seller leads, but also the slowest. Where: county probate court / clerk of court / chancery court (in some states, e.g. Mississippi, the Chancery Clerk holds estates, wills, and land records together). Most counties publish case filings through the clerk's office; some post them online, others require a courthouse visit. The filing gives names and case number; you must then match to the assessor/recorder to confirm the property, the vesting deed, and who actually has authority to sign. Freshness: filed at death/estate opening; cases run months. Realistic timeline found in the sources: a 6–12 month pipeline, not a 3-week deal. Expecting contracts in three weeks from a bought probate list is how investors conclude probate does not work. How used: write to the personal representative (never approach a grieving family at a funeral), explain you are a cash buyer interested in the estate's real estate, and be patient. Only the authorized representative can sign; multiple heirs must agree. The best long-term position is relationships with probate attorneys, who see situations before they become public records — slow to build, highest quality. Treat a bought probate list as the weakest version: four other investors in the county bought the same one.

**Code-violation notices.** Citations from city code enforcement / building / neighborhood-services departments for tall grass, trash, open/unsafe structures, illegal occupancy, unsanitary conditions, unpermitted work, or structural hazards. Repeated citations plus an out-of-area owner is one of the strongest neglect/abandonment signals available. Where: city code enforcement or building department; county code office outside city limits. Ask under the state public-records law. Check the city's open-data portal first — many large cities publish code cases free as downloadable CSV/JSON via Socrata, ArcGIS, or CKAN, often daily. Examples verified in search results: NYC Open Data (HPD housing violations), Chicago Data Portal, Seattle (data.seattle.gov — code compliance complaints, last updated November 2026), San Diego (data.sandiego.gov — code enforcement cases CSV), Austin, Orlando, Denver, Boston (Analyze Boston), Philadelphia (OpenDataPhilly), Detroit. Smaller cities often publish nothing — then a records request or in-person visit is the route. What you get: address, parcel, violation type, open/close dates, fines or liens. Freshness: daily on big-city open-data portals; otherwise as cases are opened/closed, typically weekly–monthly batches from a records request. How used: a house with repeated open violations, fines accruing into liens, and an absentee owner is a seller who is paying to own a problem. Cross-reference with tax delinquency and vacancy flags. Verify the violation is still open — many are cured — and verify ownership and equity before offering.

**Water shutoff lists.** Service addresses where water was shut off, or where usage has been zero or very low for several billing periods — a proxy for vacancy/abandonment. Ask for a report the utility already runs (disconnect report, zero-usage report), not a custom build. Where — with a major limit: only from a government-run / municipal water utility (city utility billing, public works). Private water companies are not covered by public-records laws and will not hand over lists. Texas is effectively closed: Texas Government Code §552.1331 / Utilities Code §182.052 bars government utilities from releasing whether an account is delinquent, set for disconnection, or disconnected, plus advanced-meter usage and billing data. The Texas Attorney General has ruled even a list of addresses with water shut off must be withheld (Open Records Letter OR2022-17135; earlier rulings OR2012-09753, OR2017-17812). Since a 2021 change (HB 872), confidentiality is the default unless the customer requests disclosure. Other states vary — check the state rule before requesting. Freshness: per billing cycle (monthly), when the utility runs its disconnect report. How used: as a vacancy confirmation stacked on top of tax delinquency, code violations, and returned mail — never as proof of vacancy on its own. A shutoff can also mean a billing dispute, a seasonal home, or a meter change. Verify in person (drive-by: overgrown yard, piled mail, boarded windows, disconnected meter) without trespassing.

**Vacant-property registrations.** A registry created by a city vacant-building ordinance requiring owners — and often lenders/trustees on defaulted properties — to register vacant buildings and pay a fee. Entries give address, date registered, status, and the responsible party. Lender-registered entries point directly at zombie foreclosures (owner gone, bank has not yet taken title). Where: city code enforcement or building department — only in cities that have a vacant-property ordinance. Ask first whether one exists; many cities have none. Seattle, for example, has a Vacant Building Monitoring Program but no registry and no registration charge — it inspects monthly and charges the owner instead. Las Vegas has an Abandoned Property Registry under Ordinance 6586 (vacant foreclosed properties, penalties up to $500/day residential, $750/day commercial for non-registration). Yakima, Spokane, Bremerton (Washington) and Gregory, South Dakota (Ordinance 2026-06, vacant = unoccupied 180+ consecutive days) were found with registries or registry ordinances. Where no registry exists, the free substitutes are code-violation records, USPS vacancy/undeliverable-mail flags (via data vendors), and driving for dollars. Freshness: updated as properties are registered/deregistered; typically monthly. How used: a ready-made vacant list with a named responsible party. Prioritize lender-registered entries (zombie foreclosures) and long-registered properties with tax delinquency stacked on top. Confirm vacancy and ownership — registration does not prove the owner will sell, and some registered properties are being actively marketed or renovated.

**Divorce filings.** A divorce filing in family/domestic-relations/circuit/superior court creates a case index (party names, case number, filing date). The marital home is usually the couple's largest asset and cannot be split in half — it must be sold or one spouse bought out — which creates a forced-sale timeline. Where: county or state court case index / clerk of court / family court portal, searchable by party name and case type. Access varies sharply by state: California is restrictive — under Rule of Court 2.503, family-code records may be viewed electronically only at the courthouse, not remotely; the index may be online but reading the petition requires a trip to the clerk. Maryland is more open — statewide Case Search shows case summaries, names, hearing dates, and document titles, but financial statements in divorce/child-support cases are not public. Most states fall in between, and many leave it to each county clerk. The recorder/register of deeds for the vesting deed, mortgages, any recorded lis pendens, or the divorce decree once recorded. A divorce often leaves no recorded document until the house is deeded. Freshness: filed the day the case opens; the sale decision typically comes months later, at settlement or decree. How used: a case index alone does not prove anyone owns a house — match both names against assessor and recorder records, check whose names are on the vesting deed, check the loans, and let equity decide whether there is a deal. Both spouses (and sometimes a court-appointed receiver) must agree; automatic restraining orders in some states freeze transfers when a case is filed. Approach diplomatically, as a problem-solver, never by inserting yourself between the couple. Referrals from family-law attorneys are the higher-quality, slower path. This is a sensitive list: handle it with restraint and never use personal details from the filing in outreach.

**Eviction filings.** A landlord's court filing to remove a tenant — usually a Complaint for Unlawful Detainer — filed in county civil/district/superior court. For wholesaling the target is not the tenant, it is the tired private landlord: an owner who let months of rent build up, listened to hard-luck stories, and ended up with a trashed property. Skip apartment buildings — professional owners treat evictions as routine. Where: county clerk of court / state court portal, searchable by landlord name, tenant name, or property address, filtered to eviction/unlawful-detainer case type. Examples found: Illinois Circuit Court docket, Florida Courts portal, California Judicial Branch portal (redirects to each county — no unified statewide eviction index), New York eAccessNY, Cook County Clerk court search. Docket entries and PDFs are usually free to view; some jurisdictions hide recent filings behind a fee or in-person request. City/municipal housing department or sheriff sites sometimes post weekly PDFs of active removals. Austin Open Data publishes eviction notices as downloadable CSVs. Free aggregators that point to the right portal: CourtRecords.org (directory), Docket Alarm (free tier), CourtListener (mostly federal/appellate — will miss most lower-court evictions). Freshness: filed the day the case opens; dockets update daily, though some counties lag online publication. How used: search by landlord name (the property owner), not tenant. Look for a private owner with repeated filings on the same property, long arrears, or a filing followed by a damaged/vacant house. The pitch, in the sources' framing, is essentially an offer to take over the property so the landlord never gets another phone call about that house again. Verify the landlord still owns the property (assessor/recorder), check equity and liens, and confirm the eviction's disposition — a dismissed filing is not a motivated seller.

**County tax-sale / auction lists.** The list of properties actually scheduled for sale after taxes stay unpaid long enough — or, in lien states, the list of lien certificates for sale. This is the end of the delinquency pipeline, and the wholesaler's last chance to reach the owner before the auction, plus a source of buyer intelligence (who is bidding, at what prices). Where: county treasurer / tax collector / sheriff — Tax Sale, Delinquent Taxes, or Auction section of the county site. Most counties post the sale list and schedule weeks before the auction; some post downloadable spreadsheets sortable by amount, location, or property type. Others post only a few weeks out. Newspaper legal advertisements (3–4 consecutive weeks in most jurisdictions) listing every property, owner, and amount. Online auction platforms — free to browse/register, fees only if you bid/win: Bid4Assets (30+ states, tax deeds/liens/sheriff sales, free registration, buyer's premium 0–10% by county, deposits typically $2,500–$5,000 refundable if you don't win); RealAuction family (RealTaxLien / RealTaxDeed / RealForeclose — all 67 Florida counties for tax liens; dominant in Texas and Arizona; Ohio sheriff sales via county portals); GovEase (15+ states, dominant in Tennessee, Alabama, Mississippi); Zeus Auction / SRI (Colorado, Florida, Indiana, Iowa, Louisiana, Michigan, Tennessee); CivicSource (Louisiana primarily, 5% premium); Auction.com (bank-owned and foreclosure auctions, usually 5% or $2,500 buyer's premium, cash or hard money on most foreclosures); CivilView / salesweb.civilview.com (75+ counties, one URL pattern). A genuinely free directory of sale dates sourced from county/state pages: TaxLienSimple (408 jurisdictions, 50 states + DC, sources re-verified on a rolling basis, latest check 5 November 2026) — e.g. Cook County, IL annual tax sale 15 December 2026 for tax year 2023; Harris/Dallas/Tarrant/Bexar/Travis Counties, TX first-Tuesday sales 3 November 2026. Redemption rules matter: many states are redeemable-deed states (Georgia, Texas) where the owner can redeem after sale; lien states give the owner a redemption window with interest (10% in Indiana/Missouri up to 24% in Iowa, per the sources). If you do not know the county site, use NACo's free Find a County tool to get the official website, phone, and officials for any of the 3,068 counties. Lien vs deed — check first: about half of jurisdictions sell a lien certificate (you buy the debt, earn interest, owner can redeem), the rest sell a deed (you buy the property). California is deed-only: no lien certificates are sold, sale after 5 years delinquent (3 years for some non-residential). Freshness: sale lists publish weeks to months before the sale; auction calendars are fixed by statute in many states (Georgia and Texas first Tuesday of the month; Cook County annual sale). How used: two uses. (1) Pre-auction: the sale list with a date is the highest-urgency delinquent list — the owner has a hard deadline, so contact now. (2) Post-sale intelligence: watch who buys what, at what price, and which properties go unsold — unsold parcels often become county-held / over-the-counter inventory or land-bank inventory (see the opportunities below). Do not confuse bidding at a tax sale with wholesaling: bidding requires cash/deposit, carries title and redemption risk, and is a different business from assigning a contract with the owner before the sale.

**4. Field methods — driving for dollars and free mapping**

**Driving for dollars.** Need it serves: finds off-market sellers no list contains, because the signal — a house nobody maintains — does not show up in a database. It is the only method here that produces leads competitors have not already mailed. What to look for (consensus across every guide checked): physical distress — tarped/missing-shingle roof, peeling paint, missing/damaged siding, rotted trim, broken or boarded windows, hanging gutters, cracked foundation/steps. Vacancy — overgrown lawn/weeds, piled mail and flyers, no car for weeks, no lights at night, water shut-off tags, lockbox with no listing, curtains open on an empty house. One guide cites a 1.3% national residential vacancy rate (ATTOM, Q3 2026) — a confirmed vacant house is rare and therefore stands out. Owner situation — the one dated house on a block that has flipped around it, an estate-sale sign with an attorney's number, code-violation or condemned notices on the door, a FSBO sign. Scoring that recurs in the guides: log 2+ signals together, and treat a property as a priority lead when field distress lines up with a county-record signal (absentee owner or tax delinquency). A 1–5 condition score per house keeps the log comparable. How it is done: pick 3–5 target zones first — B/C neighborhoods with older stock (pre-1990), prices inside your end buyers' range, visible pride-of-ownership variance; skip A-class (no distress) and areas with no buyers. Grid the zone so every street is covered once, drive 10–15 mph, log address + photo + distress signals in one pass. One guide estimates 60–120 leads from a month of weekly driving, at zero cost beyond gas and time; another reports 20–40 quality properties per focused hour, or 15–30 per 4-hour Saturday session. The guides are explicit that the hours are the real cost: cash cost is near zero, time cost is high. Free kit (verified): a notebook or phone notes app, phone camera, saved Google Maps pins, and a spreadsheet (address, photo, distress signs, rank, date, notes) are enough to start — multiple guides say so directly. Google Maps / Street View are free to browse. Paid apps — NOT free, do not mistake them for free: DealMachine and PropStream dominate the guides. DealMachine's own comparison page (checked 15 November 2026) says Basic is $119/mo, there is no free trial, only a free account to search/browse — paid plan required for owner data. PropStream Essentials is $99/mo with a 7-day trial including 50 free leads. Note a conflict: several secondary guides still list a DealMachine 7-day trial; the vendor's own page says none, so treat the trial as unverified. LandGlide is named by guides as a one-tap logger, but its pricing was not verified — no price claim made here. What must be acquired: a defined target area (from Zillow/MLS sold fixer-uppers in the last 12 months), a logging method, and access to the county assessor site for owner lookup afterward. Never money, never holding money. **Virtual driving for dollars (free variant):** reverse driving for dollars — walk Google Street View street by street (arrow keys, 3–5 streets per ZIP), cross-check with satellite imagery, then county GIS and city code-violation portals. Caveat from the workflow found: Street View images are often 2–5 years old, so a vacant-looking house must be re-checked against current satellite or a physical pass. Paid AI versions exist (Invelo AI DFD at $0.03 per verified property) — the manual Street View version costs nothing. Why it beats a list: visible distress precedes every list — the house you log today shows up on a vacancy list months later, sold to hundreds of subscribers. You met the owner first.

**County GIS (verified free, public).** Every county maintains a GIS parcel viewer; Fresno County's official portal is fresnocountyca.gov (Departments > Information Technology Services > GIS). It shows parcel boundaries, APN, zoning, flood zones, and aerial imagery in the browser, no login, no install. The assessor's parcel search returns owner name, mailing address, assessed value, lot size, and use. Caution: search results also surface lookalike domains (e.g. gis-map.fresnocounty-ca.org, fresno-gis-maps.propertyappraiserss.org) that are NOT the county's official domain — use the .gov address. Some counties also expose public ArcGIS REST endpoints (Fresno County's parcel MapServer is published and queryable by APN or site address) — a free, programmable parcel lookup with no account. No live in-browser check of Fresno County's official GIS viewer was performed in this research (the subagents have no live browser); the .gov portal address comes from search results, and two lookalike domains were flagged above.

**Regrid — verified free tier, with hard limits.** Regrid's own support page states Starter accounts are free with no credit card required, limited to 25 property-detail views per day, with map browsing and satellite/street toggle included, but no mapping tools, filters, projects, or exports on Starter. Pro is $10/mo ($100/yr) with 1,000 lookups/day, filters, and 5,000-row monthly exports. The App Store listing claims no lookup caps for the free Starter account, which directly contradicts the support page's 25/day cap — trust the support page; the store copy is stale marketing. Practical meaning: Regrid free is a one-parcel-at-a-time viewer (25/day), not a list builder. It is still useful for confirming boundaries, APN, lot size, and zoning on a D4D lead before outreach. Combined with the county's free ArcGIS endpoint, lot size, boundaries, and zoning confirmation cost $0 — and for land wholesaling, lot size and zoning are the deal, while paid data vendors charge for the same fields.

**Absentee-owner detection — the single highest-signal, fully free list-building method.** Owners who do not live at the property (landlords, heirs, out-of-state owners) are structurally more open to a fast, clean offer. How it is done: the county assessor (or appraiser / tax office) publishes owner name, property (situs) address, and tax-billing mailing address. Flag every record where mailing address differs from property address — that mismatch is the definition of absentee. Out-of-county and out-of-state mailing addresses score higher. In a spreadsheet: export or transcribe owner name, property address, mailing address, purchase date, assessed value; mark mismatches; standardize addresses; dedupe owners. Limits, stated honestly: assessor sites work one parcel at a time and cannot filter by owner characteristics — building a 2,000-record list this way is slow, manual work, and every county has a different interface. Bulk export availability varies by county; some post downloadable rolls, some require a records request (sometimes with copy fees from a few dollars to a few hundred). The assessor tells you who owns it; the recorder/clerk tells you how they got it (deed type, loan recorded, liens). What must be acquired: the county assessor URL, a spreadsheet, and time. No money, no permission beyond public-record access (ownership is public record in every U.S. state).

**5. Free aggregators — browse free, pay only to transact**

These are free to search; several require free registration only to bid or offer, and all charge fees/premiums only on a completed transaction:

- **HUD HomeStore (hudhomestore.gov)** — FHA foreclosed homes (REO). Free to browse/search/export, no account needed to search (verified live). Owner-occupants get first bid (15 days insured, 5 days uninsured, per HUD FAQ — other guides cite 30 days; check the listing's own period); investors wait; offers via a HUD-registered broker with a NAID number (use the site's Find a Broker tool). That is the paid-prerequisite workaround in reverse: you do not buy access, you borrow a broker's registration; the broker is the gate, not a subscription. Inspect before bidding (HUD urges it).
- **Fannie Mae HomePath (homepath.com)** — Fannie REO. Free search; filter by Listing Type to HomePath homes only (not every home in the database is one). Free account to make an offer. First Look: 30 days for owner-occupants, public entities, approved nonprofits before investors — older sources say 20 days, the 30-day figure is the FHFA-extended rule cited in 2026 guides. Flags HomeReady-eligible homes.
- **Freddie Mac HomeSteps (homesteps.com)** — Freddie REO. Free search, same First Look structure, 20 days in some sources, 30 days in others. Every home has a listing agent; offer through an agent.
- **USDA RD/FSA resales (resales.usda.gov)** — rural foreclosed homes/farms. Free, rural/small-community skew. Offers through agent/broker or USDA servicing rep; some sell at public auction.
- **VA VRM (vrmproperties.com)** — VA-acquired properties. Free map search. Open to all buyers, not only veterans. Vendee financing reported as available to veterans and non-veterans, little/no down on eligible homes — verify on the listing, do not assume.
- **IRS Auctions (irsauctions.gov)** — IRS-seized property. Free, very low volume.
- **Treasury forfeiture / GSA surplus (treasury.gov, realestatesales.gov)** — seized/surplus federal real property. Free, very low volume (about 5–16 live at a time).
- **TaxLienSimple (taxliensimple.com/auctions)** — tax lien/deed auction dates, 408 jurisdictions. Free directory; dates only, not parcel-level inventory; verify with the county.
- **LandBankSearch (landbanksearch.com)** — land-bank inventory, 61,000+ homes/lots, 23 states. Free search plus 1 saved search/weekly alerts, no card. Pro ($19/mo) for daily alerts; every listing links to the official land-bank source; land banks set their own rules, prices, rehab commitments.
- **GovAuctionAlerts (govauctionalerts.com)** — government/land-bank/HUD auctions by county. Free to browse; bid at the official source.
- **Bid4Assets / RealAuction / GovEase / Zeus / CivicSource / Auction.com** — county tax sales, sheriff sales, bank REO. Free registration/browse; buyer's premiums (0–12% by platform/county), deposits to bid, cash required.
- **City open-data portals (Socrata / ArcGIS / CKAN)** — e.g. data.cityofnewyork.us, data.seattle.gov, data.sandiego.gov, Chicago Data Portal. Code violations, permits, complaints, evictions (Austin). Free, downloadable CSV/JSON, often API access. Big cities only; many mid-size cities publish nothing.
- **CourtListener / state court portals** — court dockets (federal/appellate mainly for CourtListener). Free; misses most lower-court evictions/divorces.

**Not free (do not mistake for free):** PropStream, BatchLeads, PropertyRadar, DealMachine, Foreclosure.com, Lispend ($199–$249/mo), PropertyShark (3-day trial, then paid), RealtyTrac. They are convenience layers on the same public records above. Also noted: an open GitHub compilation of government/bank auction sources (see sources) — a useful checklist of official portals, not a data vendor.

**Bank REO pages.** Each large bank has its own portal, usually buried in the footer under Real Estate Center / Foreclosures / REO Properties / Bank-Owned. Search "[bank name] foreclosure listings" rather than navigating menus. Bank of America Real Estate Center was reported as free to browse by location without logging in. Wells Fargo REO is listed through agents, not direct sale. Listings show the listing agent / asset manager; most banks require offers through an agent, not through the portal — the portal is for browsing, not bidding. Aggregator shortcut, free: Zillow (More filters > Listing Type > Foreclosures / Pre-foreclosures / Foreclosed), Realtor.com (foreclosure filter), Redfin (foreclosure filter) — all free to search, free account unlocks full details in some reports. Pre-foreclosure data can lag 2–6 weeks.

**Auction.com — free browsing.** Verified from Auction.com's own help pages: create a free account — no payment to browse. Search by county, city, or ZIP, save properties, get free research (photos, title info, comps, education). Bidding is where money enters: some properties carry a buyer premium, usually 5% of winning bid or $2,500, whichever is greater; bank-owned sales typically require a deposit of 5% or $2,500 within 1 business day; foreclosure sales may require government ID and proof of funds to register. Do not trespass, disturb occupants, or contact borrowers — Auction.com states this explicitly. Some state pages say online bidding is not available in that state, or only in certain counties. Verdict: free to browse/research, not free to bid. One secondary review notes you cannot access foreclosure property information without creating the (free) account. Other free-to-browse auction sites: Xome, Hubzu / Equator — free listings of foreclosures, short sales, and Hubzu auctions, per Kiplinger / NewSilver guides. Their current account gates were not verified live.

**Social signals — Facebook, Nextdoor, Craigslist, and friends.** Need: off-market sellers and cash buyers who never hit MLS or a paid list, plus a free place to post a deal you already hold under contract. Facebook Groups + Marketplace: free with a Facebook account. Search local groups: "[city] real estate investors", "wholesale real estate [state]", FSBO, buy-sell-trade, landlord groups. Post digital bandit signs — "We Buy Houses Cash — Close Fast" — and post deals in investor groups. Marketplace local in-person sales are free; shipped sales via Meta incur a fee. Warning from BiggerPockets threads: verify every deal yourself, do not rely on the poster's rehab/comp numbers. Nextdoor: free, verified neighbours only. For Sale & Free section, plus neighbourhood posts about vacant / distressed houses. Best for hyper-local signals — a neighbour knows the house has been empty for months before any list does. Craigslist: free for most housing / real-estate posts; $5 for commercial real estate posts, $5 for services, $5 for cars/dealer posts — check the fee page before posting in a paid category. Use it two ways: (1) search FSBO, for-rent, and "we buy houses" ads — call the cash-buyer ads with a deal, (2) post a free housing ad. Set up free alerts via IFTTT / Craigslist search RSS where available. Zillow FSBO: owners can post For Sale By Owner free. Search FSBO + expired listings. BiggerPockets forums / Meetup.com / local REIA: free to read/post in forums, free/cheap meetups; the buyer list is built in person — courthouse auctions are specifically cited as a place to meet repeat cash buyers. OfferUp: free local listings, paid promotion optional. What must be acquired: a Facebook / Nextdoor / Craigslist account (free), a neighbourhood or city to farm, photos + basic property facts for a post, and — critically — a check of the wholesaling-marketing rule (section 9, item L) before posting a property you do not own.

**6. Free skip tracing — honest accounting**

**The truth: there is no free bulk skip tracing.** Every source agrees: free works one owner at a time; for hundreds of records, paid per-record tracing (about $0.02–$0.20 per hit) beats the hours. What IS genuinely free:

| Route | Cost | What you get | Limit |
|---|---|---|---|
| County assessor / recorder | Free | Owner name + mailing address, sale date, deed/loan info | No phone or email |
| Secretary of State business search | Free or small fee by state | Registered agent, often LLC managers/members | Commercial registered agents hide the owner |
| TruePeopleSearch | Free, no signup, no paywall (basic search) | Phones, current/past addresses, relatives, associates | US only; data often a year+ stale; detailed reports are paid |
| FastPeopleSearch | Free, no signup (basic search) | Same category; best for reverse phone/address | Same staleness; heavy overlap with TruePeopleSearch |
| That'sThem | Free | Reverse email/phone/name/address | Same category limits |
| Google, LinkedIn, Facebook | Free | Business sites/profiles listing contact info | Slow; many owners have no public profile |
| Obituaries / probate filings | Free | Heirs and estate contacts | Manual |

**Caveats that matter.** A November 2026 automated test of 13 free/freemium people-search sites found 12 answered with a 403 block or bot check — "free" means manual human browsing in a browser, not automation. Do not build a scraper on these sites, and do not circumvent a bot check. Free sources do not screen against the Do Not Call registry (258.5 million numbers on it as of 30 September 2025, per FTC figures cited in the research) and give no line-type or consent flags. A free phone number carries the same calling/texting rules as a paid one. TCPA/DNC exposure is real. Numbers from free sites are unverified and stale — cross-check on a second site before dialing. Free accuracy is reported at 30–50% for phones versus 70–85% paid; no confidence score, no line-type flag, no Do Not Call screening. Free works for 1–20 owners, collapses at 20–50/month, no bulk/export.

**Vendor free allowances (secondary-source claims, NOT independently verified on the vendors' own sites in this pass — treat as leads to confirm, not facts):** Skipify.ai free plan: 500 free property records, no card — but each skip still costs 15¢; extra records 4¢. The "500 free" is records, not traces. DirectSkip free account: 500 free records, unlimited searching, then pay-per-hit. Its 98% hit-rate claim is flagged as unverified even by the roundup citing it, and its footer was dated 2021. FlipMantis (vendor's own comparison page): free tier forever, no card — 50 property searches/mo, 10 skip traces/mo, D4D included; Pro $99/mo. DealMako (vendor's own site): free plan $0 forever, no card — 100 property records/mo; skip tracing starts on the $99/mo Starter plan. Tracerfy: $0 to start, no card, $0.02 per hit, misses free — no free credits stated. Propelio free plan: $0/mo, 10¢ per trace — the cheapest pay-as-you-go step found. **Trials (NOT free tiers, time-boxed, card sometimes required):** PropStream 7 days / 50 free leads; BatchLeads 7 days; REsimpli and InvestorBase 14 days. These are noted only — no trial was accepted. **Stacking insight:** the free allowances above are per-vendor. Using Skipify's 500 free records + DirectSkip's 500 + DealMako's 100/mo + FlipMantis's 10 traces/mo is a legitimate way to cover a first few hundred leads at $0 — provided each account is genuinely used as offered and not created to evade a limit.

**7. Free deal-finding tools and apps — honest verdicts**

| Tool | Verdict | What is free | What is not |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Propwire** | Strongest free data tool found — secondary-source, not vendor-verified live | Search 157M+ properties, comps, list downloads, 21 lead types (pre-foreclosure, probate, vacant, absentee, tired landlord, zombie), lead alerts, 10 skip traces/mo free, no card | Skip trace $0.10 per successful match after the 10 free; Gold $97/mo in one source, $119/mo in another — price disputed, confirm on propwire.com; no CRM/dialer/SMS |
| **DealMachine** | Free to explore, paid to use — vendor help page | Sign up, browse/search, no card, no expiry, no trial countdown | Owner names, contact details, property info blurred/locked; data credits, Driving for Dollars + Mail at full capacity need a plan from $119/seat/mo |
| **DealCheck** | Free plan — secondary-source only | Starter: up to 15 saved properties, rental/flip/BRRRR/wholesale calculators, comps, owner lookup, branded PDF, no card reported | Plus $10/mo, Pro $20/mo for more properties/comps |
| **RentCast** | Free plan — secondary-source only | Rent estimates, 5 rental comps per property, track 5 properties | Pro $12/mo billed annually |
| **FlipperForce** | Free plan — secondary-source only | Rookie: deal analysis + rehab estimator for 5 leads | Solo $39–$59/mo billed annually |
| **PropStream** | TRIAL-ONLY, not free | 7-day trial, 50 free leads | $99/mo Essentials, $0.12 per skip trace on Essentials; no permanent free tier found |
| **BatchLeads** | TRIAL-ONLY | 7-day trial | $119/mo |
| **REsimpli** | TRIAL-ONLY | 14-day trial | $249/mo billed annually |
| **REIPro / REI BlackBook / Wise Agent** | TRIAL-ONLY | 14-day trials | Paid after |
| **Google Maps / Street View, Google Sheets** | VERIFIED FREE | Virtual driving-for-dollars, satellite view (empty pools, junk yards, tarped roofs), free spreadsheet CRM | No owner/contact data |

**Do not assert as free:** Foreclosure.com, RealtyTrac, ListSource, PropertyShark (free account reported in older guides, but current paywall not verified in this session).

**8. Human workarounds — skipping a paid prerequisite entirely**

Each entry: need → how → what must be acquired (never money).

**a) Public-records request instead of paid data.** Need: a complete bulk list the county has not posted online — delinquent taxes, code violations, water shut-offs, evictions, probate. How: ask the specific office in writing (email is accepted in most states; FOIA does not require the words FOIA in Virginia, and many states accept phone/in-person). Be narrow: a CSV of all parcels with taxes delinquent 2+ years, fields APN, owner, mailing address, amount owed, as of a given date. Ask for a cost estimate in advance, and for electronic format. Cost reality — not always $0: inspection in person is free everywhere checked. Copies: first 50 pages free in Illinois (McHenry County), $0.15/page after; Arlington, Virginia Treasurer $25/hr for search, routine / under 1 hour free; Kent County, Michigan may require a deposit if fee exceeds $50, first $20 waived with an affidavit of indigence. Commercial-purpose requests can take longer (21 business days in Illinois vs 5 for non-commercial). Ask for inspection + electronic copy to keep it at $0. Acquired: the office name + records officer email, a precise written request, patience for 5–21 business days.

**b) County recorder's free index instead of a paid title search.** Need: who owns it, chain of title, mortgages/liens/judgments, whether a loan was released. How: (1) Assessor / property appraiser site — free, search by address/APN/owner: owner of record, mailing address, assessed value, last sale date/price. (2) Recorder / Register of Deeds / Clerk Official Records — free index search by grantor/grantee name; filter Deed / Warranty Deed, then trace backwards owner-to-owner; check each mortgage for a later satisfaction/release/reconveyance; search for tax liens, mechanics liens, HOA liens, lis pendens, judgments. (3) Court index for lawsuits/probate/divorce. Cost reality — varies by county: many counties give free index + free document images. Some split access: Winnebago County, Illinois example — Direct Search free, limited, no images; Tapestry $8.75/search + $1/page; Laredo subscription for commercial users. OKCountyRecords example: basic name/document search free, full scanned images may require account/paid plan. Certified copies cost; uncertified viewing is usually free. This is a first-pass title check, not title insurance and not a legal opinion. Acquired: APN (from assessor or tax bill), owner's full legal name, recorder portal URL (.gov/.us).

**c) County GIS + assessor instead of a paid list / paid map.** Need: parcel boundaries, zoning, lot size, land use, owner, assessed value — for land especially. How: county GIS Parcel Viewer, free, no login in the counties checked. Search by address/APN/owner, toggle layers for zoning, flood, land use, aerial imagery. Click a parcel for owner, acreage, legal description, assessed value. Use it to find vacant lots, odd lots, and lots whose zoning (e.g. CMX) does not match the county's R-1/RS-5 label — then confirm with Planning by phone. Acquired: county GIS URL, APN or address, zoning code to check.

**d) Driving for dollars — instead of buying a list.** Need: properties no list contains yet. How: drive/walk B/C neighbourhoods with older stock at 10–15 mph, log tall grass, boarded/dark windows, tarped roof, stuffed mailbox, notices on door, full gutters. 20–40 quality properties per focused hour reported; 15–30 per 4-hour Saturday session. Log address + photo + distress signals in a spreadsheet or free app. Then look up owner via assessor (free), contact by mail / door knock / call where legal. Virtual version, $0 gas: Google Maps Street View + satellite, scroll streets, look for distress, empty pools, junk yards. Works best where imagery is recent. Why it beats a list: visible distress precedes every list — the house you log today shows up on a vacancy list months later, sold to hundreds of subscribers. You met the owner first. Acquired: a target zone, a phone/camera + spreadsheet, time, gas if driving. Only cash cost in guides is skip tracing at $0.10–$0.20/record — see workaround (f) for the free version.

**e) Free public-record lists instead of a paid motivated-seller list.** All from county/city offices, mostly free, stack 2+ signals on the same property: pre-foreclosure (Notice of Default / lis pendens at recorder); probate (probate court filings + legal notices in local papers; verify who the court appointed to sell and whether court confirmation is needed); code violations (city code-enforcement cases, often published or releasable by records request); evictions (court records — a landlord after an eviction, damaged unit, lost rent, may be done being a landlord); divorce (family-court filings / lis pendens); tax-delinquent (treasurer list); absentee / tired landlord (assessor mailing address differs from property address); water shut-off / utility delinquency (city utility records where releasable); expired listings + FSBO (Zillow / Realtor / Craigslist, free).

**f) Free skip tracing instead of paid skip tracing.** Need: owner phone/email from a name + mailing address. How — the free stack, one owner at a time: (1) assessor for owner + mailing address, (2) Secretary of State business search (free) if owner is an LLC — registered agent, managers/members, (3) TruePeopleSearch / FastPeopleSearch — free, no account reported, search by name + city/state, get possible phones/emails/past addresses/relatives, (4) cross-check on a second people-search site + Facebook/LinkedIn (search name + city; Marketplace/groups often show a number openly), (5) Google the address in quotes — old listings, expired rentals, Craigslist/Marketplace posts, HOA docs surface. Honest limits: free accuracy reported at 30–50% for phones vs 70–85% paid; no confidence score, no line-type flag, no Do Not Call screening — a free number carries the same calling/texting rules as a paid one (FTC: 258.5 million numbers on the National DNC Registry as of 30 September 2025). Free works for 1–20 owners, collapses at 20–50/month, no bulk/export. A November 2026 automated test of 13 free people-search sites found 12 answered with a 403 block or bot check — do not circumvent a bot check; use the site in a normal browser or move on. Cheapest paid step-up found in that test: Propelio Free plan $0/mo + $0.10/trace; Tracerfy $0.02/hit reported. Acquired: owner name + city/state, assessor mailing address, time for manual lookups.

**g) Free cash-buyer list instead of a paid buyer list.** Need: who will actually pay, before you sign. How: recorder search for recent sales in your target ZIP with no mortgage recorded = cash purchase. Note buyer name/entity, price, date. Trace the buyer (see f). Also: attend courthouse / sheriff / tax auctions and note repeat bidders; call "We Buy Houses" ads on Craigslist; ask investor-friendly title companies and agents who their cash buyers are; post in local REIA / Facebook investor groups. Acquired: recorder portal, target ZIP, a spreadsheet. No money, no list purchase.

**h) Bird-dogging / JV instead of holding a contract or money.** Need: cash flow with no contract risk and no capital. How: find a distressed property, hand the lead (address, owner, distress, your comp notes) to an active wholesaler/flipper for a bird-dog fee — $500–$2,500 reported in one guide — paid at closing, or JV the deal: you bring the lead/contract, partner brings the buyer list / experience, split the assignment fee. Never hold money; the fee is paid via title/escrow at closing. Acquired: a buyer/wholesaler who will pay for leads, a written bird-dog/JV understanding before handing over the address, proof the deal closed. (Legality is a gray area — see section 9, item E.)

**i) Newspaper legal notices + library / courthouse terminals instead of paid alerts.** Need: probate, foreclosure, tax sale, and court notices before they hit a database. How: read the county's designated newspaper legal-notice section (often free online via the Public Notice Resource Center), and use the recorder/clerk's free public-access terminals in person. The daily court/legal publication in some markets lists every property transfer, foreclosure, and civil case — scan the transfer section for reduced-price sales, then write to both seller and buyer. Acquired: newspaper name, courthouse hours, time.

**9. Loopholes, gray areas, and faster value-creation angles — reported in full, NOT acted on**

**A. Tax-sale surplus / excess proceeds — the largest undervalued, underworked pool in this research.** Who: owners (and their heirs) whose property sold at a tax deed / sheriff sale for more than the taxes, penalties, interest, and costs owed. The excess — the surplus or overage — belongs to the former owner, but counties hold it, and in many jurisdictions it is claimed by only a small fraction of owners because they do not know it exists, have moved, or are deceased. What: a finder/recovery service that (1) pulls the free tax-sale results and surplus-funds lists counties publish (treasurer, clerk of courts, or unclaimed-funds office — all public records), (2) identifies the former owner or heirs, (3) helps them file the claim, and (4) is paid a contingency percentage of the recovered funds under a written agreement. This is an established niche (surplus-funds recovery) but far less saturated than wholesaling lists, because it requires reading sale results rather than pre-sale lists. When: after the sale — the surplus exists only once the auction closes and the excess is calculated. Claims typically have a statutory window (varies by state, often years, but some states sweep unclaimed surplus into the general fund after a deadline — check the state statute). In California, claim within 1 year after the tax deed is recorded; filing with the county is free. Where: any county that holds tax deed or sheriff sales and publishes results/surplus lists — the same treasurer/clerk offices above. Florida, Texas, Georgia, Ohio, and Pennsylvania were prominent in the auction sources found. Why it is an opportunity: the value is created from a public record the county already publishes, the owner is owed money rather than being asked to sell at a discount (a much easier conversation than a distressed purchase), and the work is document-driven rather than sales-driven. The money already exists, the owner often does not know it exists, and the claim is a paper filing, not a rehab or a closing. Gray areas / cautions (not acted on, needs a lawyer before touching): some states regulate surplus recovery, cap finder fees, or require attorney involvement; contacting heirs of a deceased owner has the same sensitivity as probate; claiming funds on someone else's behalf without proper authorization or a valid assignment is fraud; and a few states treat unclaimed surplus as forfeited after a period. This is a legal-services-adjacent business, not a wholesaling play — verify the state statute and fee caps first. No verified fee schedule or licensing rule was found in this session — verify with a California attorney before offering this as a service, and never claim the money is yours.

**B. Land-bank and county-held over-the-counter inventory — the cheapest parcels, invisible to Zillow.** Who: land banks (public/nonprofit entities that take tax-foreclosed, vacant, and abandoned parcels) and counties holding unsold tax-sale parcels. What: land-bank homes commonly sell for $1,000–$20,000 and lots for less, with prices set by each land bank — far below market, and never listed on the MLS, so Zillow/Redfin never see them. LandBankSearch aggregates 61,000+ such parcels free. When/where: continuously, in 23 states per the aggregator; Cleveland (12,683), Genesee County/Flint (10,575), St. Louis LRA (8,094), Kansas City, Philadelphia, Birmingham, and Michigan State Land Bank were the largest inventories found. Why it is an opportunity: this is the closest thing to a free, legal below-market list that already includes a price. For a wholesaler, the play is not flipping the land-bank parcel itself (many land banks restrict assignment/flipping and require a rehab plan) but using the inventory as (1) a comparable for valuing nearby private parcels, (2) a source of cheap lots for assemblage next to a private deal, and (3) a buyer-side product: a cash buyer who wants cheap lots can be shown land-bank inventory the wholesaler did not have to find by driving. Gray area: buying from a land bank with the intent to immediately assign/flip may violate that land bank's terms or deed restrictions — read each land bank's rules before offering, and do not market a land-bank parcel you do not control.

**C. The "no open-data portal" arbitrage — small and mid-size cities.** Who: wholesalers working cities that publish no open data (most mid-size and small cities publish nothing — no Socrata, no ArcGIS, no CSV). What: in those cities, code violations, vacant registrations, and water shutoffs exist only as paper or as a records request. Paid vendors cover them poorly or not at all, because there is no feed to scrape. A wholesaler who files a standing monthly public-records request (same template, same office, same day each month) for the code-violation log, the vacant registry, and the delinquent roll gets a list competitors are not pulling — the research found this explicitly: county lists are the most accurate and current source, and far fewer investors bother to get them. When/where: any city/county without an open-data portal; the request cycle is monthly. Why it is an opportunity: the cost is time, not money, and the moat is persistence, not data spend. The same template that requests the list can be reused across counties. Gray area: none in the request itself — it is a lawful public-records request. The gray area is what you do with the data: do not republish personal data, do not use utility data where the state bars it (Texas), and do not sell a list built from records whose disclosure was restricted. If you ever package and sell a compiled list, check whether the source county restricts bulk resale (Texas, for example, restricts bulk downloading/reselling of court records without permission under its e-filing rules).

**D. Zombie foreclosures (lender-registered vacant properties) — a narrow, high-signal slice.** Who: owners who have left, lenders who have filed NOD but not yet taken title, and the property sitting vacant in between. What: cross the NOD/lis pendens index with the vacant-property registry (where it exists) and the tax-delinquent roll. A property on all three — in default, registered vacant by the lender, taxes unpaid — is a zombie foreclosure. The owner may still have the right to sell; the lender is the party that will eventually own it. When/where: in cities with a vacant-foreclosure registry (Las Vegas is the clearest example found) and non-judicial foreclosure states where the gap between NOD and trustee sale is long. Why it is an opportunity: very few investors join these three free lists, because they live in three different offices. The seller is often reachable and motivated, and the lender's registration gives you the servicer's name for a short-sale conversation if the owner agrees. Caution: do not contact the lender about a property you have no contract or authorization on, and do not represent that you can stop the foreclosure. The owner is the counterparty; the lender is a later step, with the owner's consent.

**E. Bird-dogging as a no-contract, no-license income lane.** Who: anyone without a license or capital. What: scout distressed properties, hand the address + owner details to an investor, collect a flat finder's fee — typically $500–$2,500 per closed referral — without ever signing a purchase contract or holding an equitable interest. When: now, per-deal. Where: most U.S. states, but the line varies by state. Why it matters: it is the lowest-barrier cash lane in this whole research, and it is structurally different from wholesaling — a wholesaler signs a contract and assigns it (assignment fees $5,000–$25,000), which several states now treat as brokerage. Gray area, stated plainly: some states define brokerage broadly enough that receiving compensation for connecting a buyer and seller is unlicensed activity, with penalties up to criminal charges; one legal Q&A source argues a flat fee (not a percentage of price) is not a commission and therefore not brokerage, while another source warns the flat-vs-percentage distinction is not settled law everywhere and a written agreement plus a check of the state real-estate commission's definition is non-negotiable. Also, federal RESPA bars referral fees tied to settlement services on federally related mortgage loans. This is the single most important gray area in the file: the same lead can be sold as a legal flat-fee referral or punished as unlicensed brokerage depending on state and structure. Verify California specifically before collecting a fee.

**F. California wholesaling window — open now, on the clock.** Who: California wholesalers. What: AB 1850 (introduced 11 February 2026) would have required a real-estate license to wholesale and mandated written disclosure that the wholesaler will not take title. When/where: it passed its first committee 19–0, then stalled — held under submission in Assembly Appropriations on 14 May 2026, with no action since; it is not law, but not formally dead. Why it matters: today, assigning your own signed purchase contract is legal in California under ordinary contract law, but marketing the property itself (rather than your contract position) can count as unlicensed brokerage under Business & Professions Code §10130, with fines up to $20,000 and up to six months in county jail cited for unlicensed broker activity; the standard C.A.R. purchase agreement requires the seller's written consent to assign, and the seller is told the buyer's name and the fee. The window is open but visibly narrowing — a 19–0 committee vote is a warning, not a reprieve.

**G. Sell the list, not the deal — the list-as-product angle.** Who: whoever builds the free list. What: the free methods above (assessor mismatches + tax-delinquent + code violations + stale listings) produce a compiled, scored lead list that other investors will pay for — the same data PropStream/BatchLeads sell as subscriptions. A list built once from free public records can be sold non-exclusively to multiple buyers, or as a refreshed monthly subscription, with zero capital in the properties themselves. When: after the list is built and verified. Where: any county with downloadable assessor/GIS data. Why: it converts free public data into recurring revenue without signing contracts, holding money, or touching a property — and it sidesteps the wholesaling licensing line entirely because no transaction is being brokered. Gray area: reselling public-record compilations is a normal data business, but marketing a specific property you do not control, or charging a percentage of a deal's price for a referral, slides back toward brokerage (see E). Keep the product as data, priced flat, and keep property marketing out of it.

**H. Apify's $5/month free credits as a free listing-monitoring machine.** Who: anyone with a free Apify account (no card). What: Apify's free plan grants $5 of usage monthly; third-party actors scrape Redfin/Realtor.com at about $0.45 per 1,000 results, so $5 covers roughly 11,000 listings a month — enough to run a weekly scan of every listing in a county and diff it for new price cuts, new stale listings, and disappearances (the expired signal), exported as CSV. When: monthly, on the free allowance. Where: any U.S. city/ZIP/county. Why: it automates the Zillow watch-list method in section 2 at $0, turning a manual weekly chore into a repeatable feed. Caveat: this is scraping portal data via a third-party actor — it reads public listings, but portal terms of service typically restrict scraping; treat it as a gray area to confirm against the actor's and portals' terms before relying on it, and never use it to bypass a login or paywall.

**I. Regrid's free tier + county ArcGIS endpoints as a free parcel API.** Who: anyone doing land/lot work (notably the Fresno CMX lots). What: Fresno County publishes a public ArcGIS MapServer endpoint queryable by APN or site address with no key, returning lot area, use, and assessed value as JSON — effectively a free parcel API. Combined with Regrid's 25 free detail views/day, this gives lot size, boundaries, and zoning confirmation at $0. Why: for land wholesaling, lot size and zoning are the deal — and both are free here, while paid data vendors charge for the same fields.

**J. Over-the-counter / leftover / struck-off land — the cheapest buyable inventory.** Who: counties / state land commissioners. What: parcels that drew no bid at tax sale. They do not disappear — they go to an over-the-counter, repository, struck-off, or post-auction list, often at a reduced minimum, first-come, no auction. When: after the sale; in Arkansas, 30 days after the live auction, online. Where: county tax collector / Commissioner of State Lands site; Fresno had 13 unsold in 12 months, re-offered under §3698.5. Why it is undervalued: paid aggregators hide the full list behind Pro; most buyers only watch the auction, not the leftovers. Risk: title is often a limited-warranty / quitclaim deed, no guarantee of usability, access, or clear title; city liens and improvement-district taxes can pass to the buyer. Check zoning/access before any bid.

**K. HUD Good Neighbor Next Door (50% off) and Dollar Homes ($1) — real, but NOT a wholesaler arbitrage.** Who: GNND — full-time law enforcement, pre-K–12 teachers, firefighters, EMTs who will live in the home 36 months as sole residence, in a HUD revitalization area, 7-day lottery. Dollar Homes — local governments only, homes unsold 180+ days, uninsurable, as-is value $25,000 or less. What: 50% off list price as a silent second mortgage (no interest/payments if occupancy is met); $1 to a government. When/where: on HUD HomeStore, weekly changing inventory. Why it matters: these are the deepest discounts in the federal system, but they are occupancy-locked. Using a straw eligible buyer to flip a GNND home, or marketing a Dollar Home as if you could buy it, would violate the program. The legitimate faster-value angle is narrow: partner with / refer an eligible owner-occupant, or work with a local government / HUD-approved nonprofit that can buy at a discount — do not present it as a wholesale deal. Verified live on HUD HomeStore.

**L. Wholesaling legality — the gray line that decides whether a free deal is a legal deal.** Who: every wholesaler, in every state. What: assigning a purchase contract (selling your equitable interest) is reported as legal in all 50 states — no state has banned it outright. It crosses into unlicensed brokerage when you market the property as if you owned it, or act for someone else for a fee.
