When/where — 2026 rules, sources disagree on the exact count (16 vs about 20 states): license required in South Carolina, Pennsylvania, Iowa, Rhode Island (from 2027), and others; one-deal caps in Illinois and Virginia (second assignable-contract deal in 12 months = broker); registration in Oregon (since 1 July 2025) and Connecticut (since 1 July 2026, $285 fee reported); written disclosure + seller cancellation in Alabama, Arizona, Texas, Wisconsin, North Dakota, Maryland, Ohio, Tennessee, Missouri, Louisiana, Indiana, Washington, and others. California: no wholesaling-specific statute in force as of July 2026 in the trackers found; AB 1850 was introduced in the 2025–26 session — general brokerage law still applies. Why: a free lead is worthless if the marketing breaks the rule. This is not legal advice — check the state statute on the legislature's own site (search "wholesal", "equitable interest", "assignable contract") before signing.

**M. Vacant land / small lots as the undervalued market.** Who: tax-defaulted lot owners, counties with re-offer lists. What: small vacant lots with opening bids of $2,200 (Fresno re-offer low), $171.85–$12,000 (Arkansas county sale example), often no tenant, no rehab, no inspection of a structure. When/where: tax-sale re-offers, over-the-counter lists, county GIS vacant-land layer. Why faster/cheaper: lower opening bid, fewer competing bidders than houses, and a lot can be assigned to a builder/developer buyer (e.g. a CMX lot buyer) without a rehab budget. Risk: zoning confusion is real — Fresno's four CMX lots show CMX on City GIS but R-1/RS-5 in county/Redfin data; access, utilities, and title clouds can kill a lot deal. Confirm zoning with Planning (Fresno: 559-621-8277 in prior record) before pitching it as developable.

**N. The First Look timing trap — do NOT use as a loophole.** HUD / HomePath / HomeSteps give owner-occupants 15–30 days first. A wholesaler who uses a straw owner-occupant to get past First Look and then assigns to an investor would be circumventing a federal priority rule. No legitimate version of this was found — the legitimate version is to wait out First Look, watch the price drops on stale REO (banks routinely reduce prices on unsold REO), and bid after the window opens to investors.

**O. Free list-stacking — the $0 substitute for a $99–$119/mo data plan.** Who: a time-rich, cash-poor wholesaler. What: combine two or three free signals on the same parcel — e.g. vacant (driving) + tax-delinquent (treasurer) + absentee (assessor mailing differs from property address) + code violation (city). When/where: in a spreadsheet, from the free sources in section 8. Why it works faster: paid lists sell the same single-signal list to hundreds of subscribers; a stacked free list is proprietary, smaller, and higher-intent, and the owner has heard from fewer marketers. This is the single highest-leverage free method in the guides read.

**Do-not-do gray areas flagged, not recommended:** creating multiple Regrid/skip-trace accounts to evade the 25/day or free-allowance caps (likely a terms-of-service violation); automating TruePeopleSearch/FastPeopleSearch lookups (12 of 13 tested sites block automated browsers — the block is the enforcement); and texting numbers from free people-search sites without consent or DNC screening (same TCPA/DNC rules apply as paid data, and free data carries no compliance flags).

**10. Signed opportunities — need, how, what must be acquired (never money, never spend, never hold money)**

**1. Tax-sale surplus finder service.** Need: former owners owed surplus funds who do not know it. How it can be done: pull free county tax-sale result/surplus lists weekly; match surplus parcels to former owners via assessor/recorder; contact the owner/heir with proof of the surplus amount and a written contingency agreement; file the claim with the county. What must be acquired: access to county treasurer/clerk surplus lists (free public records); assessor/recorder lookup (free); a county-by-county table of surplus statutes, claim deadlines, and fee caps (research, not purchase); a written claim-filing process per county; and a licensed attorney review of the agreement and fee structure in each state before any owner is contacted. The funds go from the county to the owner, with the fee paid under the agreement, not through the finder.

**2. Land-bank inventory curation for cash buyers.** Need: cash buyers who want sub-$20,000 houses/lots and do not know land-bank inventory exists. How: curate the free LandBankSearch/county land-bank lists for one county, filter to parcels matching a known buyer list, and present them with the official source link and the land bank's rules. What must be acquired: free LandBankSearch browsing; each land bank's application/terms pages; a buyer list. No money, no purchase, no holding of funds.

**3. The list as a product.** Need: investors who pay for scored lead lists. How: build the list once from free public records (assessor mismatches + tax-delinquent + code violations + stale listings), score it by stacked signals, sell it non-exclusively at a flat price or as a refreshed monthly subscription. What must be acquired: county assessor/GIS access, treasurer roll, code-enforcement portal, a spreadsheet; and a check on whether the source county restricts bulk resale of its records.

**4. The no-open-data monthly request service.** Need: investors in cities with no open-data portal. How: a standing monthly public-records request (same template, same office, same day each month) for the code-violation log, the vacant registry, and the delinquent roll — a list competitors are not pulling. What must be acquired: the office name + records officer email, the request template, the calendar discipline.

**5. Over-the-counter / struck-off lot watch.** Need: buyers of cheap lots (builders/developers) and sellers of lots that drew no bid. How: watch county re-offer/OTC lists (Fresno re-offer 23 November 2026, 26 parcels, $2,200–$86,400); match unsold lots to a buyer list; confirm zoning, access, and title first. What must be acquired: county treasurer + RealAuction pages, GIS zoning check, Planning phone number.

**6. Free buyer-list building from the recorder.** Need: knowing who actually pays cash before signing. How: recorder search for recent sales in the target ZIP with no mortgage recorded = cash purchase; note buyer name/entity, price, date; trace the buyer. What must be acquired: recorder portal, target ZIP, a spreadsheet.

**7. Bird-dog / JV lane.** Need: cash flow with no contract risk and no capital. How: scout a distressed property, hand the address + owner + distress + comp notes to an active wholesaler/flipper for a flat fee at closing, or JV with a split of the assignment fee. What must be acquired: a buyer/wholesaler who will pay for leads, a written bird-dog/JV understanding before handing over the address, proof the deal closed, and a California legality check before collecting any fee.

**Distilled rules**

**37. Public record first, paid last — and stack two free signals before you contact anyone.** Pull owner, mailing address, tax status, liens, and zoning free from the assessor, recorder, treasurer, GIS, or court before paying for any list, tool, or trace. A lead only earns outreach when two independent signals from different offices land on the same parcel — field or listing distress plus an ownership or situation signal. One signal is a note; two is a lead.

**38. Verify the same week you contact, and sell speed and certainty — never rescue.** Owners cure taxes, fix violations, and dismiss cases constantly; a month-old list is a list of people who already solved the problem or already been called by everyone else. Confirm the owner on the assessor and the lien/title picture on the recorder before outreach, and offer a fast, certain cash closing — never promise to stop a foreclosure or use rescue language, which triggers foreclosure-consultant statutes in about 25 states.

**39. Market the contract, never the house — and never call a number you have not scrubbed.** You are selling your right to buy, not brokering a home you do not own; if your state requires a disclosure, registration, or a licence, get it before you post the deal anywhere — a free post that breaks the rule is not free. Scrub every number against the National Do Not Call Registry (no more than 31 days old) plus state lists, call only 8 a.m.–9 p.m. recipient local time, honor opt-outs within 10 business days — a skip-traced number is not consent for autodialed calls, AI voice, ringless voicemail, or bulk texts, and TCPA damages run $500–$1,500 per violation.

**12. What could not be verified / limits of this research**

- Fresno County specifics were not separately checked in this task (its exact portal names, whether it posts a delinquent spreadsheet, whether it has a vacant registry) — that needs a direct check of the Fresno County Treasurer-Tax Collector, Recorder, and City of Fresno Code Enforcement pages. The Fresno tax-sale re-offer details above come from secondary sources (Tax Sale Atlas, California Property Tools, Owner Guardian), not from the county site itself.
- Vendor free allowances (Skipify, DirectSkip, FlipMantis, DealMako, Propwire, DealCheck, RentCast, FlipperForce) rest on secondary sources or vendor marketing pages, not live vendor-site verification in this session. DealMachine's trial status is contradictory across sources (vendor page says no trial; secondary guides say 7 days) — unresolved. Propwire Gold price is $97 vs $119/mo in different secondary sources; its free download cap is 10,000/mo vs 10,000/day in different sources. First Look lengths are 15 vs 20 vs 30 days across HUD/HomePath/HomeSteps sources — check the listing itself. LandGlide pricing was not verified — no claim made.
- TruePeopleSearch / FastPeopleSearch free status comes from secondary guides; a November 2026 automated test found 12 of 13 people-search sites blocked automation with 403/bot checks. No block was circumvented.
- No county site was logged into and no list was downloaded in this research; freshness claims are as reported by the cited guides and county examples, not as re-verified for any specific county.
- Wholesaling legality, licensing, and disclosure rules vary by state and changed in several states in 2024–2026; nothing here is legal advice. A real-estate attorney in the target state should review the contract, the outreach script, and any surplus-recovery agreement before use.
- No blocked source was encountered that required circumvention; where a source was restricted (Texas water data, California remote divorce records, some court portals), the restriction is reported as a finding, not worked around.

### 10. Buyer psychology — what makes a cash buyer pay (studied 2026-11-20)
**The core finding: the buyer pays the math, not the pitch.** A cash buyer is not a persuaded person. They are a calculator with capital. Every source agrees on the same shape: the buyer runs one formula, and if the deal clears it, they pay; if it does not, they ghost. The formula is the 70% rule — maximum allowable offer equals 70% of after-repair value minus repair costs. The 30% buffer is not arbitrary; it covers acquisition costs (3–4%), holding costs (4–6%), selling costs (7–8%), and profit (15%+). It flexes with conditions: hot markets 75–80%, cold or sticky markets 65–70%, high-risk rehabs with uncertain repair scope 60–65%, buy-and-hold landlords 75–80%, BRRRR deals 75–85% because cap rate matters more than spread. First-time flippers overestimate ARV by 5–10%, which alone can wipe out half the profit. Every dollar above MAO comes directly out of the buyer's profit — so the buyer's yes or no is decided by the numbers before the conversation starts.

**Two species of buyer — and they pay for opposite things.** Cash buyers and creative buyers are different animals. A cash buyer optimizes for price, speed, and immediate profit: deep discount, clear title, close in 7–14 days, no complexity. A creative buyer optimizes for terms and long-term wealth: they will pay full asking price, or above market, for 0% interest over 30 years or $5,000 down with no credit check. The pitch must match the species — a cash pitch leads with ARV, rehab cost, and price; a creative pitch leads with terms and payment. Send a vacant, distressed, deep-discount deal to a cash buyer; send a flexible-terms, low-equity deal to a creative buyer. Mismatching wastes both sides' time.

Cash buyers are binary. They are either in or out, and they ghost on a mismatch. Their questions tell you who they are: "What's the ARV? What's the rehab cost? Can I assign it? When can I walk it? How much are you asking?" They are numbers-focused and transactional. And they walk from mess: "Cash buyers will walk from a great deal if it's messy." A lien, a title problem, an occupant who will not leave, a seller who wants to stay six months — any of those kills a deal the numbers would otherwise support.

**Why cash exists at all: sellers overpay for certainty, and the gap is the profit.** The strongest data point in this research comes from Cotality's 2025 analysis. Sellers accepted an average 9% discount on cash purchases versus financed ones in 2025 — and the gap has widened every year: 4% in 2021, 6.2% in 2022, 6.8% in 2023, 7% in 2024, 9% in 2025. Meanwhile, as rates rose after 2022, the share of pending transactions that failed to close roughly doubled, from 2–3% to 5–6%. Do the expected-value math at today's roughly $410,000 median price: a financed offer that fails 6% of the time still delivers an expected value near $390,000 once earnest money is factored in; a cash offer discounted 9% yields around $373,000. The discount sellers pay for certainty is steep relative to the actual probability of failure. Sellers are buying reassurance, and they are overpaying for it. That overpayment is the wholesaler's margin: the seller trades price for a sure thing, the cash buyer prices risk by MAO, and the spread between the two is where the fee lives. Cash dominates the flip market — ATTOM's Q2 2025 report found 62.6% of flipped homes nationwide were purchased all-cash.

The emotional drivers underneath are consistent across sources: speed and efficiency (no months of mortgage approval), bargaining power (a cash offer carries more weight), no loan-default risk, insulation from rate volatility, the allure of outright ownership, and independence from banks. The counterweight is that cash ties up liquid assets — a cash buyer's capital is finite, which is exactly why they are picky. Every dollar is rationed against the next deal.

**What makes the yes happen in practice — four things, in order:**

**1. The numbers work — and are shown, not claimed.** "Buyers aren't buying a house. They're buying a spreadsheet with a house attached to it." The buyer needs five answers in the first thirty seconds: what is it, what will it sell for fixed up (ARV backed by comps, not a guess), what does it need (a repair scope, broken out by trade and severity), what is the ask (contract price and the spread), and what does it actually look like (clear photos of every room, including the problems). Miss one and the buyer has to chase you for it, which slows them down and makes the deal feel like more work than it is worth. The deals that move fastest are the ones where a buyer can decide without a single follow-up call. The first complete, credible package usually wins the conversation, even when it is not the cheapest deal on the list.

**2. Speed.** Market within hours of signing the contract, not days. Follow up at 24 and 48 hours. Serious cash buyers look at multiple deals from multiple wholesalers at once. Every day of delay is a day closer to the option period expiring. This is also where Mac's speed demand lands directly on buyer psychology: once the deal-package template exists, a complete package can be built in under 30 minutes — and that speed is itself part of what makes the buyer pay.

**3. Trust — the only discount that compounds.** A buyers list of 2,000 names is not an asset; twelve buyers who have bought two or three times each is. On a first deal, the buyer verifies everything: repair estimates, control of the property, whether you will close. That adds days. On a third deal, none of it happens — your estimate is now evidence rather than a claim, and a deal that fits gets a yes in a day, sometimes on the phone, without a property visit. The single largest factor in repeat business is that your numbers held up. A buyer who bought on your repair estimate and found it accurate will buy again; one who found $20,000 more work will not, no matter how good the next deal is. Every honest deal reduces the cost of the next one; every deal where the numbers were light resets trust to zero. Three trust builders recur across sources: give a new buyer a genuinely good deal early (a buyer who makes $40,000 on the first deal you bring them takes your calls for five years); disclose the bad news first (a foundation crack volunteered unprompted is worth more than any polish); and do not extract the maximum fee — a buyer who did well on your deal wants the next one, and loyal buyers produce 25–35% ROI deal after deal. Trust is built through competence and integrity, deal after deal, not charisma.

**4. Fit — the deal matches the buy box.** A buyer pays when the deal lands inside criteria they set before they ever saw it: property type, area, price range, cash or financed, and their profit threshold. Segment the list by price point, property type, and market, and send the best deals to the most reliable closers first. Blasting the whole list on every deal trains people to ignore you. And respect the buyer's process: do not send deals outside their criteria, do not pressure an hours-only decision when they need 24, and do not shop their offer to other buyers to drive up the price — once a buyer makes an offer, honor the negotiation in good faith.

**What this means for Mac's live buyers.** Edward "Bubba" Williams (development lots) will pay when a lot's total cost to him sits at or below what a developer will pay for it — his MAO is a builder's number, not a house formula, and his price range, unit count, and cash status are still unknown, so his threshold cannot be computed yet. Ishmael (fix-and-flip single-family ARV $350k, 1–4 units ARV $400–500k) has a precise box: a single-family deal clears for him at roughly 70% of $350,000 minus repairs — about $245,000 minus repairs — and a multifamily deal at 70% of $400–500k minus repairs. The 403 question connects here too: the run-6 gap-math flag said a $23,000 contract against a ~$65,000 buyer price leaves a ~$42,000 spread, about 65% of the buyer price, far outside the normal 5–15% fee band. Buyer psychology explains why that matters: the buyer pays the total assignment price against their own MAO, and a fee that eats their margin is not invisible to them — it is the reason they pass or renegotiate. The fee must fit inside the buyer's math, not just inside the contract.

**Distilled rules (notes until tested on real replies or dollars)**
28. **The buyer pays the math, not the pitch.** A cash buyer says yes when their total cost — contract plus assignment fee plus repairs — sits at or below their MAO, roughly 70% of ARV minus repairs, adjusted for market and risk. Sell the spreadsheet, not the story: ARV with comps, repair scope, clean title, close date, in one complete package, within hours of signing.
29. **Trust is the only discount that compounds.** One accurate deal earns the next yes without re-verification; one light number resets trust to zero. Give a new buyer real margin early, disclose the bad news first, and never let the fee eat the buyer's profit — a buyer who made money on your deal takes your next call.
30. **Match the deal to the species.** Cash buyers are binary and walk from mess; creative buyers pay for terms, not price. Send deep-discount clean deals to cash, flexible-terms deals to creative — and never pitch a cash buyer a deal whose total cost breaks their MAO, because the ghost is the answer.

**Loopholes, gray areas, new licensing or selling routes — none found.** Nothing in this research presented a loophole, a gray area, or a new way to license or sell something. One market inefficiency is worth naming as an insight, not a loophole: sellers are overpaying for certainty — the 9% average cash discount in 2025 is steep against a 5–6% deal-failure rate — and that gap is the arbitrage itself, conducted openly through a contract and a title company. No action taken on it; it is the business model, not a workaround.

**Opportunities — signed: the need, how it can be done, what must be acquired**
- **Opportunity 1 — The certainty gap as the core arbitrage.** The need: sellers who will trade price for a sure, fast close, and cash buyers who price risk by MAO. How it can be done: contract below the buyer's MAO, assign at or below MAO, and keep the spread inside the 5–15% fee band so the buyer's math still works. What must be acquired: accurate ARV comps from free sources (sold listings, county recorder), honest repair estimates (photos plus trade pricing), a segmented buyer list with verified buy boxes (meetups, public records of cash sales, free groups), and a one-page deal-package template. No money, no spend, no holding of funds.
- **Opportunity 2 — The repeat-buyer engine.** The need: 3–5 repeat buyers who trust the numbers enough to say yes without re-verifying. How it can be done: first deal to a new buyer carries real margin; bad news disclosed first; numbers verified accurate after close; follow-up at 24 and 48 hours; best deals go to the most reliable closers first. What must be acquired: a tracking sheet of every buyer's box (price, type, area, cash or financed), a deal-package template, and a follow-up routine. Free tools only.
- **Opportunity 3 — The land-buyer math gap.** The need: land buyers like Edward, whose MAO is not a house formula but "what a builder or developer will pay." How it can be done: for each lot, find the end user's price first (recent land sales, builder resale, developed value) and work backwards to the contract price, the same way a flipper works backwards from ARV. What must be acquired: recent land sales from the county recorder (free), builder and developer buy boxes gathered in conversation at meetups, and zoning confirmation from city GIS or Planning (free). Without a confirmed end-buyer price — which is exactly the open question on 403 — the MAO is a guess and the fee band cannot be set honestly.

### 11. Trades I — carpentry and framing: what bad work costs a deal (studied 2026-11-20)
**The core fact: the frame is the house, and it is invisible.** Framing is the skeleton — every wall, floor, and roof depends on it. Once drywall and siding go up, nobody can see what is behind them. Bad framing can hide for years or decades before symptoms appear: cracked drywall, bouncy floors, sagging rooflines, doors that will not close. By the time the symptoms show, the fix costs far more than doing it right would have. That invisibility is the whole story of what bad framing costs a deal — the buyer is not pricing the wood, they are pricing the uncertainty about the wood.

**The seven mistakes (and the ten hidden ones).** The seven most common framing mistakes, with what each costs to fix after the fact (Renovation Revelation, East Tennessee contractor):
1. **Undersized headers over windows and doors.** A 2x4 laid flat over a 4-foot opening in a load-bearing wall is not a header. The weight sags it, cracks the drywall above, jams the window or door. Fix: tear out drywall, install a proper header, jack up sagging structure above, repair finishes. $200 in lumber during framing becomes $2,000–$5,000 as a retrofit.
2. **Missing or inadequate blocking.** Short lumber between studs or joists that backs cabinets, handrails, grab bars, and braces members against twisting. Skipped blocking means cabinets pull away from walls and grab bars rip out. A $5 piece of wood becomes a $500 repair.
3. **Improper joist spans and spacing.** Joists too small for the span, or spaced too far apart (24 inches instead of 16), make floors bounce and sag, crack tile, separate flooring seams, and stress plumbing. Fix: sister new joists alongside every existing one, or add beams and posts underneath. $3,000–$8,000.
4. **Improper roof rafter connections.** Relying on toenails alone instead of hurricane ties or rafter ties. In a severe storm, rafters can separate from the walls — catastrophic roof failure. Retrofit: access every rafter connection from inside the attic and install connectors in tight spaces. $1,500–$4,000, versus about $200 in materials during original construction.
5. **Load-bearing walls removed without support.** The open-plan remodel: a wall comes out, nothing goes in, and the joists above hang unsupported. The structure sags, cracks, and slowly fails. Proper fix is a sized beam (LVL or steel) on posts that carry the load to the foundation, sized by an engineer. $3,000–$10,000 to do right; far higher if it fails first.
6. **No fire blocking between floors.** Without blocking, stud cavities act as chimneys in a fire, carrying flames from floor to floor and into the attic. The material costs scraps of lumber; the absence can mean the difference between a contained fire and a total loss. Found most often in unpermitted two-story additions.
7. **Poor moisture management at framing connections.** Untreated lumber touching concrete, missing sill-seal gaskets, unflashed ledger boards. Rotted sill plates show up on homes only 15–20 years old. The walls literally have nothing solid to sit on. Fix requires jacking up wall sections and replacing the plate: $5,000–$15,000.
The ten hidden mistakes (Civil Engineering Tech, with International Residential Code citations) add: weak or missing corner studs (IRC R602.3.5); skipping bracing or structural sheathing (R602.10); stud spacing wider than code allows (R602.3.1); over-notched or over-drilled studs — the holes electricians and plumbers cut (R602.6); non-overlapping or single top plates (R602.3.2); poorly anchored bottom plates (R403.1.6, R602.11); missing jack studs on wide openings (R602.7, R602.7.5); wrong lumber grade or wet "green" lumber (R602.1); and inconsistent or missing fastening schedules. The code citations matter because they turn a judgment call into a checkable fact: any framer, inspector, or buyer can look up the section.

**The repair cost ladder (2026 figures).** Framing repair, national averages (Angi, 2026 data): average $1,425 per job, typical range $500–$2,300. By type: wall framing $1,000–$6,000 per wall; floor joists $20–$60 per square foot; roof framing $20–$80 per square foot; door frames $300–$1,500; window frames $200–$600. Dry rot repair runs $499–$2,363, average $1,431, with major structural fixes climbing past $6,600 — plus $5–$40 per square foot for tear-out and finish work on top.
Structural repair methods (independent 2026 guide): sistering floor joists $12–$15 per linear foot; jack posts $525–$630 each; steel beam $260–$275 per foot (needs engineering and a permit); carbon-fiber or steel strips for bowing walls $4,000–$12,000; lifting and leveling a settled house $20,000–$23,000. An independent structural engineer's report averages about $600 (range $300–$1,000) — the first call before any structural number is trusted.
Regional contractor pricing confirms the ladder: localized joist or rim-board repair starts around $2,500; full floor-joist or sill-plate replacement $4,500–$12,000; load-bearing wall removal or major structural modification $15,000+ (Buffalo, NY). Floor and joist repairs $1,000–$8,000; bowing walls $1,500–$10,000+; foundation-related structural problems $3,000–$25,000+ (Hershey, PA).
New-build framing cost for context (Angi, 2026): $7–$16 per square foot, $14,000–$32,000 for a standard 2,000-square-foot home; materials $3–$6 per square foot, labor $4–$10 per square foot. Framing is typically 15–20% of total construction cost (Chicago contractor data: $18–$35 per square foot for a new-home structural shell). Roughly half of a wood frame's cost is the lumber package itself, and lumber moves weekly — framing lumber was priced at $459.10 per 1,000 board feet on October 30, 2026 (Madison's Lumber Price Index). A framing number more than a few weeks old is a starting point, not a price.

**What bad framing does to value — the wholesaler's math.** Structural defects are the single most severe fixable devaluation trigger in an appraisal. The numbers, from appraisal and market data (iBuyer/NAR composites, 2025–2026):
- Structural issues generally: **10–25% reduction** in sale price versus comparable undamaged homes.
- A house with structural damage sold as-is: **20–35% below market** for comparable undamaged homes.
- By damage type: failing load-bearing walls 20–30%; structural water damage (beam or joist rot) 20–35%; sagging or damaged roof framing 15–25%; active foundation settlement 15–25%; compromised floor joists 10–20%; minor foundation cracks (documented, cosmetic) 5–10%.
- For comparison: deferred maintenance 5–15%, unpermitted work 3–8%, poor curb appeal 2–5%, outdated kitchen or bath 1–5%. Structural trouble outranks all of them.
The mechanism matters more than the percentage. When an appraiser flags a structural defect during a financed transaction, the lender can impose a repair condition — repairs must be completed before the loan funds. Most conventional lenders will not finance a home with active structural defects at all. The buyer pool collapses to cash investors, flippers, and renovation-loan buyers. That is precisely the wholesaler's buyer — but it is also why the discount exists: the seller is not selling to the retail market anymore. The U.S. statutory anchor for what counts (HUD's definition) is actual physical damage to the load-bearing portions of a home, caused by failure of those portions, to the extent the home becomes unsafe, unsanitary, or otherwise unlivable — covering foundation, floor joists and beams, load-bearing walls, and roof framing (rafters, ridge boards, collar ties).
The pattern under the whole list, from a foundation contractor's ranking: buyers do not discount problems; they discount uncertainty about problems. The dollar figure a buyer subtracts is almost always bigger than the repair bill, because they are pricing what they cannot measure at the worst case and adding margin for the trouble.

**The hidden-damage multiplier — the case that proves it.** A documented case (Inman, home-inspector column): a buyer purchased a home after the inspector found rotted floor framing. The contractor estimated $4,000. The sellers refused to pay; the buyers bought anyway. When work began and siding came off, the contractor found two adjacent foundation walls with wet soil between them — major framing damage that had not been visible. The repair bid became $24,000. The sellers denied knowledge or responsibility. The pattern is common: a major defect goes undisclosed because the sellers were unaware of it, and late discovery leaves the buyer holding the bill. The visible estimate is the tip; the hidden damage is the iceberg. A wholesaler who budgets the visible quote without a multiplier is budgeting the tip.

**The inspection window — the only cheap moment.** New construction has three phase inspections: pre-pour foundation, pre-drywall (framing and rough-in), and final walkthrough. The pre-drywall inspection is the only window where framing can be caught and corrected before the walls close — one documented case had improperly installed roof trusses flagged at pre-drywall and corrected by the builder at zero cost to the buyer; a different buyer whose pre-pour phase went uninspected later faced a $12,000 foundation repair the builder's warranty would not cover because the defect was never documented during construction. The economics: an average inspection fee of $400–$700 routinely uncovers defects with repair costs of $2,000–$10,000 or more, and buyers who skip inspections face average repair costs of $5,000–$15,000+ within two years of ownership. 80–90% of home inspections uncover at least one significant defect. Existing-home inspections run $375–$700; new-construction phase packages run $600–$1,200+ for all three phases.
For the owner-builder, the sequencing rule is just as sharp: work done out of sequence — drywall hung before the rough inspection passes, concrete poured before footing inspection — gets ordered torn down or destructively cored to verify, plus re-inspection fees of $75–$250 per return visit. The rough-framing inspection itself checks anchor bolts (1/2-inch bolts, embedded at least 7 inches, spaced no more than 6 feet apart, within 12 inches of each plate end), continuous load path and hurricane straps, and fireblocking at every concealed vertical chase (IRC R602, R802, R302.11, R403.1.6).

**The walkthrough tells — reading the frame without opening walls.** A wholesaler cannot open walls on a first walkthrough. The symptoms that read the frame from the surface, drawn from the inspector and contractor sources:
- Bouncy or sagging floors; floors that slope (contractor tiers: under 1/2 inch over an 8-foot span is minor, 1/2 to 1 inch is moderate, over 1 inch is significant/structural).
- Doors and windows that stick, jam, or will not latch; racked door frames.
- Cracks that follow stud lines in drywall; stair-step cracking up a brick gable.
- Sagging rooflines or ceilings; bowing or bulging walls.
- Soft or spongy spots in floors; musty air and stains that read as rot risk.
Each tell points at a specific framing member and a specific line on the repair ladder — and each one the seller has not explained is a discount the buyer will price at worst case.

**Distilled rules (notes until tested on real replies or dollars)**
31. **Price the frame, not the finish.** A house's value lives in its skeleton; finishes are cheap to change and framing is not. On every walkthrough, read the frame through its symptoms — bouncy floors, racked doors, stud-line cracks, sagging rooflines — before pricing anything else. The repair ladder runs $500 to $25,000+, and hidden damage multiplies the visible estimate: the $4,000 quote that became $24,000 is the rule, not the exception.
32. **The 10x rule: what costs $200 in framing costs $2,000 after drywall.** Every framing shortcut — an undersized header, missing blocking, skipped hurricane ties — is a small number during construction and a ten-times number after the walls close. For the wholesaler this cuts both ways: it is why bad framing discounts a deal 10–35%, and why the buyer's repair budget must assume the hidden multiplier, never the visible quote. Budget the iceberg, not the tip.
33. **Structural trouble kills financing before it kills the deal.** A flagged structural defect can trigger a lender repair condition and shrink the buyer pool to cash only — which is exactly the wholesaler's buyer. Know the appraisal impact (10–25% for structural issues, up to 35% for structural damage) so the MAO already contains it, and never let a buyer discover the frame after the contract is signed. The frame is the first thing priced, the first thing disclosed, and the last thing surprised by.

**Gray area, flagged — not a play: unpermitted structural work.** Load-bearing walls removed without a beam, additions built without permits, missing fire blocking — these are common in the distressed houses a wholesaler meets, and they carry a disclosure duty: most states legally require the seller to disclose known material defects in writing before accepting an offer. Buying and assigning a house with unpermitted structural work without disclosing it is a legal exposure, not an opportunity. The gray area is the gap between what the seller knows and what the buyer can see — and the program's rule is disclosure-first, so the gap is closed, not worked. Brought back for Mac's per-case decision; no action taken. (Also logged under "Brought back to Mac" above.)

**Opportunity — signed: the need, how it can be done, what must be acquired.**
The need: buyers and sellers both fear hidden framing defects, and the only cheap moment to catch them is the pre-drywall inspection window on new builds — or the attic and crawlspace on existing homes. Most wholesalers walk a house and price the finishes; almost nobody walks it with a framing checklist in hand. The fear is real and the check is cheap ($400–$700 uncovers $2,000–$10,000+ in defects).
How it can be done: build a one-page framing walkthrough checklist from the two mistake lists in this research (the seven mistakes plus the ten hidden ones, each with its IRC citation) and the symptom list above, and run it on every wholesale walkthrough before the offer is priced. Pair it with a standing relationship with one independent framing inspector or structural engineer who will do a $300–$600 report that prices the frame before the contract is signed — the report turns the buyer's worst-case discount into a number both sides can see.
What must be acquired to do it (no money from Mac, no spend, no holding money): the checklist itself — built from this research, free; access — the property before drywall on new builds, or attic and crawlspace access on existing homes, granted by the seller as part of the walkthrough, no permission beyond the seller's own yes; one licensed inspector or structural engineer contact willing to take referral calls — a relationship, not a payment; the inspection itself is paid by the end buyer or the seller as part of their own diligence, or priced into the assignment-fee negotiation. Nothing else. No tool, no trial, no account, no card.

- The Old Town Remodeler — "Framing Contractor Graysville, AL: 2026 Cost & Hiring Guide" — https://www.theoldtownremodeler.com/blog/framing-contractor-graysville-al

### 12. Trades II — electrical and plumbing (studied 2026-11-20)
**The frame — why these two systems decide the deal.** A wholesaler's inventory is distressed property, and the five systems that eat most of a rehab budget are foundation, roof, HVAC, plumbing, and electrical. Plumbing and electrical are the two that buyers cannot ignore, because they decide whether a house can be insured and financed at all. A house with a bad roof can still get a mortgage. A house with an uninsurable panel or a collapsed sewer line often cannot — which closes the retail exit and leaves cash as the only buyer. That is the wholesaler's opening and the wholesaler's risk in the same fact.
The two systems also hide the biggest cost surprises. A cosmetic rehab runs roughly $15–$25 per square foot; a moderate rehab $25–$45; a full structural and systems overhaul $45–$75 or more. Electrical and plumbing are what push a property from one tier to the next. A $20,000 error in a repair estimate moves the maximum allowable offer by $20,000 directly, because MAO is ARV times 0.70 minus repairs. Industry guidance adds a 10–15 percent contingency on top of line items, and labor rates vary 25–35 percent between high-cost and low-cost markets, so national numbers must be adjusted to the local market — a contractor's own quotes in the operating city eventually replace every national table.

**Electrical — the five hazards.**
- **Knob-and-tube wiring (roughly 1880s–1940s).** Ceramic knobs and tubes holding separate single wires, visible in attics and basements. No ground wire, not designed for modern loads or for contact with modern insulation. About 2 million US homes still have it. Most insurers decline coverage on a home with active knob-and-tube, which makes it a financing-blocking issue rather than a cosmetic one. Full rewire of a typical home: $8,000–$15,000 for a 1,500-square-foot house at the low end of the ranges found, $12,000–$36,600 nationally per 2026 Angi data (average about $24,300), and $10,000–$30,000-plus in higher-cost markets. Rewiring runs roughly $4–$12 per square foot in some guides and $10–$20 per square foot in others, plus $300–$900 for drywall repair and about $1,960 to repaint an interior. Permits run $10–$500 (typically $100–$125) and inspections $100–$150 each, with two inspections normally required.
- **Aluminum branch wiring (mid-1960s to mid-1970s).** About 2–3 million homes. Identified by "AL" or "ALUMINUM" printed on the cable jacket at the panel or in the basement. Aluminum expands and contracts more than copper, loosening connections at outlets and switches over time; the Consumer Product Safety Commission puts it at 55 times more likely to reach fire-hazard conditions than copper. The common fix is not a full rewire: an electrician attaches short copper pigtails at each connection using connectors approved for aluminum-to-copper. Whole-house pigtailing: $1,500–$4,000. Many insurers will not write a policy or will require remediation first.
- **Federal Pacific (FPE / Stab-Lok) and Zinsco panels (1950s–1980s).** Breakers with documented failure to trip during overload; independent testing puts FPE Stab-Lok failure at up to 25 percent, meaning one in four breakers may fail to trip. Most insurers will not write or renew a policy on a home with one of these panels in service, and a lender can require replacement as a condition of the loan. Replacement: $1,800–$3,500 in some markets, $2,000–$4,500 in others. These are flagged by name by inspectors and by insurers.
- **Fuse boxes and undersized service.** A fuse box signals a system that has never been updated. A 60-amp service cannot support modern appliances. Fuse-box-to-breaker-panel conversion: $1,500–$2,000 on the low end, up to $4,000. Panel replacement by amperage: 100-amp $800–$1,500; 200-amp $1,300–$3,000 (the current standard, adequate for most three-bedroom homes up to about 1,800 square feet with central air); 300-amp $1,800–$3,500; 400-amp $2,000–$4,000. A full service upgrade from 100 to 200 amps, including meter, mast, and utility coordination: $3,000–$6,000; a 400-amp service for a large or heavily electrified home: $5,000–$10,000-plus. National average for a panel replacement per 2026 Angi data: about $1,343, normal range roughly $518–$2,188, full range $125–$4,500. A straightforward breaker-to-breaker swap: $800–$2,000. Permit $50–$300. The physical swap is often a single day; the full process with permit, inspection, and utility coordination runs one to two weeks.
- **Ungrounded outlets, missing GFCIs, double-tapped breakers.** Minor on their own, they pile up on an inspection report and spook a retail buyer. Double-tapped breakers (two wires under one lug), reversed polarity, and open grounds are found by testing outlets, not by looking. An electrician's evaluation runs $150–$500; a safety inspection $150–$350; a service call or diagnostic visit $150–$500.
**What to read on a walkthrough (electrical).** Start at the panel: read the brand label (FPE, Zinsco, Stab-Lok), the amperage, and whether it is fuses or breakers. Look in the attic or basement for ceramic knobs and tubes, and at the panel for "AL" on cable jackets. Check for ungrounded two-prong outlets and missing GFCI protection near water. Get a licensed electrician's written quote during the inspection period, not just the inspector's note — and call the insurance agent during the inspection period to ask whether the home is coverable as-is. Ask for proof that past electrical updates were permitted, especially in flipped homes.

**Plumbing — supply side (water coming in).**
- **Galvanized steel supply pipe (pre-mid-1960s especially).** Corrodes from the inside out over decades, narrowing until pressure drops and joints fail inside walls. Service life 50–70 years, shortened by hard water. Signals: low water pressure throughout the home, rust-colored staining around drain connections and supply lines under sinks. Whole-house repipe: $5,500–$12,000 for a typical 3-bedroom/2-bath home in Florida markets; $6,300–$14,500 in San Jose; galvanized replacement $8,000–$20,000 in the broader San Jose market. A repipe typically takes 2–4 days and the household usually stays in the home.
- **Polybutylene pipe (1978–1995).** Grey flexible plastic pipe with metal crimp fittings. Degrades from chlorine in municipal water and fails suddenly at fittings without warning. The single most significant plumbing deal-killer in Florida: most insurers there (Citizens, Heritage, HCI) refuse to write policies on polybutylene homes, and it affects VA/FHA loan eligibility. Full replacement: $6,000–$15,000. Buyers demand full replacement credit or walk.
- **Lead pipe.** Toxic; if lead is even suspected, removal and replacement is required without delay. Lead service lines are a known issue in older cities and a disclosure item everywhere.
- **Cast iron supply and drain pipe (inside the house).** Cast iron lasts 50–75 years (some guides say 60–80). It rusts from the inside: scale builds, walls thin, cracks open along the bottom where water runs. Replacement runs $23–$75 per foot for 4- to 12-inch flanged pipe, plus at least $300 labor and fittings, plus digging and backfill. Corroded cast iron often fails along its whole length, which points to full replacement rather than spot repair.
- **Water heater, boiler, gas lines.** A standard water heater replacement lands around $1,500–$3,000 installed; tankless conversions cost more because gas and venting work is usually involved. Boiler replacement: $6,000–$12,000. Gas line correction for a code violation: $3,000–$8,000-plus.

**Plumbing — drain and sewer side (water going out).** This is where the five-figure surprises live, and where the wholesaler's cheapest tool earns its keep.
- **The pipe materials and how each fails.** Vitrified clay pipe: laid in short sections joined end to end; joints without watertight seals invite root intrusion. Life 30–50 years. Replacement $45–$90 per foot in older guides. Cast iron sewer: rusts from the inside; hydrogen sulfide gas from sewage turns corrosive and thins the wall; scale narrows the pipe; freeze-thaw cycles crack aging joints. Life 50–70 years; inspect any pipe over 40 years old. Orangeburg (bituminous fiber, mid-century): wood pulp soaked in tar; softens and flattens over time. A crushed Orangeburg line usually cannot be lined, so it must be dug out or burst — replacement $75–$250 per foot. Plastic (ABS, PVC, HDPE): the modern replacement material, life around 100 years; $52–$125 per foot in older guides. Asbestos cement: present in some older systems; a further flag for age.
- **What replacement actually costs.** Full main sewer line replacement: typically $3,000–$15,000 depending on length, depth, and method; most residential jobs $4,500–$9,000 for a typical 50–80 linear-foot run. Per linear foot: $50–$250 (Forbes range $55–$250); Los Angeles averages around $170 per foot. Open-cut excavation: $80–$150 per foot (cheapest method, destroys landscaping, driveways, sidewalks); trenchless pipe bursting: $6,000–$12,000 for a standard line; pipe lining (epoxy resin inside the old pipe): $90–$160 per foot, works only where the old pipe is still structurally sound. A Seattle guide puts a standard 40–60-foot line at $8,000–$20,000 open cut and $4,000–$15,000 trenchless. Depth beyond 6 feet can add $1,200–$2,500; Orangeburg removal adds 15–20 percent for collapse risk. A spot repair runs around $3,000; a collapsed line caught late runs $8,000–$12,000 versus $3,500–$5,000 if diagnosed early by camera. The homeowner is responsible for the building sewer from the house to the public main, and lines under a slab are reached very differently from lines through a yard.
- **The sewer scope — the cheapest insurance in the deal.** A home inspector does not run a camera down the sewer line, so the most expensive problems in the house never make it into the inspection report. A sewer scope costs a couple of hundred dollars (plumbing inspections with a scope run $200–$600). A camera run shows root intrusion, cracks, offsets at joints, bellies where the line has settled, and sections already collapsed. It is worth doing on any house 20 years old or older, and on any house with cast iron, Orangeburg, galvanized steel, or polybutylene pipe. A $350–$500 plumbing inspection can uncover tens of thousands in hidden costs. The rule from practitioners: a scope is a couple of hundred dollars; sewer work is the kind of repair people take out a loan for.
- **Slab leaks.** A leak under the slab takes equipment to locate and can mean cutting into the floor of the house; costs start in the low thousands and climb with how much water escaped before anyone noticed.
- **Septic systems (rural and outside city limits).** A septic system is a buried wastewater plant: tank, distribution box, drainfield. A well-maintained system lasts 20–30 years; pumping is needed every 3–5 years. A neglected system fails, and septic problems are almost never covered by homeowners insurance because they come from gradual deterioration. Two inspection levels exist: visual ($100–$300) and full ($300–$1,000), where the inspector opens the tank, measures sludge and scum, pumps it, checks baffles and the effluent filter, evaluates the distribution box and drainfield, and runs a backflow test. A full inspection returns pass, pass with conditions (typically $50–$500 in repairs), or fail — drainfield failure, a leaking tank, or a non-compliant system, which is $3,000–$20,000-plus territory. Full replacement runs $10,000–$25,000. VA loans require a septic inspection. Sellers on septic must provide the last inspection and pumping records in states that require it (Florida DEP Rule 64E-6, for example), and a failed system must be disclosed. The pricing rule from Texas practitioners: price off comparable sales, minus the repair cost and a 20–30 percent cushion; a failed system typically comes off the final price as the repair cost plus a cushion, often 5–10 percent below comps once negotiated. A $300 pump-out is a very different deal from a $15,000 replacement — get the inspection and know which category applies.
**What to read on a walkthrough (plumbing).** Run water at several fixtures and watch pressure; look under sinks for rust staining; identify the supply pipe material at the water heater and under sinks (galvanized steel, grey flexible polybutylene, copper, PEX). Check the water heater's age on its data plate. Ask the seller the age of the sewer line and whether it has ever been scoped; if the house is 20-plus years old or the pipe material is unknown, scope it during the inspection period. On septic, ask for pumping records and the last inspection; order the full inspection, not the visual one.

**The uninsurable chain — the wall that is also the door.** The pattern repeats across every market checked: an outdated panel or old wiring is a financing and insurance problem before it is a safety one. If a carrier will not insure the home because of the panel or the wiring, the buyer cannot get a mortgage to close, no matter how qualified the buyer is. An outdated electrical panel is the single most common item that stalls a closing in Colorado, not because the fix is expensive but because a lender or insurer can refuse to move forward until it is addressed. The same chain runs for polybutylene pipe in Florida and for knob-and-tube everywhere: uninsurable means unfinanceable means unsellable through traditional channels, which leaves only cash buyers.
This is the human innovation the study is tracking: people skip the prerequisite. The retail sale requires an insurable, financeable, permitted house. The cash as-is sale skips all three prerequisites lawfully — no insurance required, no lender, no permit — and the wholesaler is the one who carries the deal across that gap. The dangerous twin of the same skip is the flipper who skips the permit and hides the defect: same shortcut, opposite direction, and it ends in stop-work orders, civil penalties, mandatory demolition, lender complications, and disclosure liability.

**Unpermitted work — the flipper trap.** Unpermitted work is any change that needed a permit and never got one: a finished basement or attic, a garage conversion, an added room, new wiring, a wall removed without sign-off, a repiped house, a swapped water heater. In California, permits are required for most structural, electrical, plumbing, and HVAC work so inspectors can verify the work before it is covered by finished surfaces; when flippers skip permits, the work is never inspected and the buyer inherits code violations they had no way of knowing existed. In one San Diego expert-witness case, a fully cosmetically renovated home carried six correction items, including an electrical sub-panel installed in an illegal location, gas lines never pressure-tested, unverified footings, and plumbing not properly vented. Under California Business and Professions Code Section 7031, an owner who hires an unlicensed contractor forfeits the right to seek damages from that person when the work fails — so the later buyer inherits deficient work with no professional accountability behind it.
A house with unpermitted work can still be wholesaled, but only one way: pull the permit record from the building office, walk the house and write down every item that looks altered, price the fix (legalize or remove), run the ARV on legal square footage only, put the known problems in the contract in writing, share the permit record with the buyer, and close through title. Never hide a known permit problem from the buyer. The buyer pool for these houses is mostly contractors and small flippers who can pull permits themselves. Lead sources for this kind of property are public: failed listings that came off the market after an inspection, listing mismatches where the bedroom count exceeds the record, code complaint records, and local contractors who know which owners skipped permits.

**Cost table — electrical and plumbing (ranges found, market-dependent).** Electrical panel replacement, 200-amp: $1,300–$3,000 (broad $1,500–$4,000). Full service upgrade 100→200 amp: $3,000–$6,000. 400-amp service: $5,000–$10,000+. Fuse box to breaker panel: $1,500–$4,000. FPE / Zinsco panel replacement: $1,800–$4,500. Aluminum pigtailing, whole house: $1,500–$4,000. Full rewire, typical single-family: $8,000–$15,000 (low end) to $12,000–$36,600 (2026 national). Electrician evaluation / safety inspection: $150–$500. Whole-house repipe (PEX): $5,500–$14,500 (galvanized up to $20,000). Polybutylene replacement: $6,000–$15,000. Sewer line replacement, full: $3,000–$15,000 ($50–$250 per linear foot). Sewer line spot repair: ~$3,000. Sewer scope / plumbing inspection: $200–$600. Water heater replacement: $1,500–$3,000. Boiler replacement: $6,000–$12,000. Gas line correction: $3,000–$8,000+. Septic full inspection: $300–$1,000. Septic pump + certification: $350–$750. Septic repair / replacement: $3,000–$20,000+ / $10,000–$25,000.

**Distilled rules (3)**
34. **Price the systems, not the surfaces.** The panel and the pipes decide the deal. A beautiful kitchen on 65-year-old galvanized pipe is a $10,000 surprise, not a $10,000 premium. Read the panel brand and the pipe material before you name a number, and let the MAO carry the systems cost, not the paint.
35. **Uninsurable is a lead, not just a cost.** When insurance or a lender refuses the house, the retail exit closes and only cash can buy. That seller is your seller and the cash buyer is your buyer. The wall for them is the door for you — but only if you disclose the defect and price the cure in writing.
36. **Disclose the defect, price the cure, count only legal space.** Unpermitted work and failed systems can still be wholesaled, but only with the problem in the contract, the fix priced in, and the ARV built on legal square footage alone. Hiding it is fraud; pricing it is the business. A permit record and a sewer scope cost a few hundred dollars and protect a deal worth tens of thousands.

**Signed opportunities — need, how, what must be acquired**
- **Opportunity 1 — The sewer scope before the offer.** Need: a $3,000–$15,000 sewer surprise that kills the buyer's numbers after the contract is signed. How: scope every house 20-plus years old, and every house with unknown or cast-iron/Orangeburg pipe, during the inspection period — a $200–$300 camera run that shows roots, cracks, offsets, bellies, and collapse before money moves. What must be acquired: a local sewer-scope company on call (one phone relationship, built before a live deal), and the inspection-period right written into the contract. Cost is carried by the buyer or negotiated with the seller — never Mac's money.
- **Opportunity 2 — The uninsurable-panel lead signal.** Need: sellers who cannot sell retail because no insurer will cover their panel or wiring — the most motivated sellers in any market, and they are invisible to agents. How: read the panel brand from listing photos or ask the seller one question — what kind of breaker box do you have — and flag FPE, Zinsco, Stab-Lok, fuse boxes, and 60-amp service as cash-only sellers. What must be acquired: a one-page panel-brand cheat sheet and the script line; no money, no tool, no account.
- **Opportunity 3 — The contractor walkthrough partnership.** Need: accurate repair numbers in ten minutes on a live contract, instead of a national table that is wrong for the market. How: a licensed electrician and a licensed plumber who walk properties with you and can say in ten minutes whether you are looking at a $1,500 fix or a $12,000 remodel. What must be acquired: two relationships, built at REI meetups and through investor referrals before a live deal exists — not cold-called during an inspection window.
- **Opportunity 4 — Septic screening on rural deals.** Need: a $10,000–$25,000 failed septic system that no insurance covers and no retail buyer will touch. How: a full septic inspection ($300–$1,000) on any property on septic during the inspection window, with pumping records requested from the seller first. What must be acquired: a certified septic inspector contact and the habit of asking "city sewer or septic?" on the first call.
- **Opportunity 5 — The flipped-house pipe check.** Need: not paying a retail price for a cosmetically renovated house whose pipes are still 65 years old. How: a two-minute check of pipe material under sinks and at the water heater, plus a permit-record pull, before offering — the remodel that looks finished is often the one hiding the oldest systems. What must be acquired: the habit and the permit-record request; no money, no tool.

**Brought back to Mac — gray areas and boundaries (not acted on)**
- **Boundary 1 — Broker-licensing brush on unpermitted-work deals.** Who: wholesalers who market assigned contracts on houses with unpermitted work. What: in some states the marketing of the contract itself can fall under broker-licensing rules — Montana's broker rule (MCA 37-51-301) and Massachusetts Chapter 112 Sections 87PP and 87RR were named in the sources as reaching fee-takers who market. When: whenever a contract is marketed rather than a property owned. Where: state-dependent; Montana and Massachusetts named, other states unexamined. Why it matters: the unpermitted-work lead stream is real and lawful to work, but the way the deal is marketed may need an attorney's wording in some states. Mac decides per case; do not scale this lead source without a local attorney's read. (Also logged under "Brought back to Mac" above.)
- **Boundary 2 — Disclosure duties on failed systems.** Who: any seller or wholesaler handling a house with a known failed sewer, septic, or electrical defect. What: failure to disclose known material defects can support contract rescission and fraud claims — Florida Statute 689.261 was cited as mandatory material-defect disclosure, and Texas requires disclosure of known aluminum wiring. When: at contracting, not at closing. Where: every state examined has some version of this duty; the exact form varies. Why it matters: the lawful skip (cash as-is) depends entirely on disclosure; the unlawful skip (hiding the defect) is what turns a deal into a lawsuit. Nothing to exploit here — a line to respect. (Also logged under "Brought back to Mac" above.)
**No new way to license or sell something was found in this topic.** No loophole to use, no undervalued market to license into, no gray-area method to run. The value found is in reading the systems and in the five signed opportunities above.

## 13. Trades III — Masonry and bricklaying: what good work costs, what bad work costs a deal

**Researched 2026-11-20 (run 11).** Method: public, free sources only (browser search + freely readable pages). No paywall, login, or bot check bypassed. No source blocked. All summaries in own words; no passage quoted exceeds 50 words from any one work.

### What good masonry work costs (2026 U.S. ranges)

**Repointing / tuckpointing (the most common masonry job)**
- National average: about **$1,450 per 100 sq ft**, roughly **$14–$15/sq ft**; typical range $400–$2,500 per 100 sq ft. Mason labor $50–$100/hr.
- Full repointing in higher-cost metros (DC, NYC): **$15–$35/sq ft**; spot mortar repair $8–$15/sq ft; historic lime-mortar work $25–$50/sq ft.
- Repointing a whole brick house: **$5,000–$25,000** (single-story, ~2,500 sq ft) or **$7,000–$38,000** (two-story). Work above 8 ft adds 20–40% for scaffolding.
- Mortar analysis before repointing: composition analysis $200–$500, color analysis $100–$300. Mortar needs repointing roughly every 25–30 years.

**Brick repair / replacement**
- Brick wall repair: **$20–$40/sq ft**; brick veneer repair $11–$24/sq ft; replacing solid face brick $20–$40/sq ft.
- Spalling brick repair: $1,000–$3,500 per 100 sq ft; small sections $250–$450 minimum.
- Bowing brick wall repair: **$2,000–$6,500 per 100 sq ft**.
- Full brick restoration: **$5,000–$35,000** depending on size, age, and damage. National average masonry job overall: ~$2,000, typical range $500–$2,500.
- Brick steps: $300–$750 per step; brick porch repointing/small fixes $1,500–$3,000.

**Chimneys (the highest-leverage masonry item on a house)**
- Average chimney repair: **$455** nationally; most jobs $160–$750.
- Repointing a chimney: $500–$2,500 (partial) to $2,500–$6,000 (full chimney); $4–$30/sq ft.
- Crown repair $150–$600; crown replacement $600–$3,000. Cap replacement $100–$600. Flashing repair/replacement $200–$1,500.
- Flue tile repair: a few tiles $200–$400; flue repair $65–$100/sq ft. Stainless liner $900–$4,500; cast-in-place liner $2,500–$7,000.
- Partial rebuild (above roofline): **$1,500–$8,000**. Full chimney rebuild: **$4,000–$15,000** nationally, up to **$10,000–$30,000+** in high-cost data.
- Chimney inspection: Level 1 $100–$250; Level 2 with video scan $200–$600 — Level 2 is the standard recommended when buying/selling.
- Permits for partial/full rebuilds: $150–$2,000.

**Lintels (steel beams over windows/doors/garages)**
- National average lintel replacement: **~$460** ($280–$640) for a simple window lintel.
- Realistic range once brick removal, height, and permits are counted: **$500–$2,500** per lintel (Brooklyn/Alabama data); NYC $1,500–$8,000 each. Garage lintels $200–$700 simple, $1,200–$5,000+ for full replacement; wood lintels in older homes can reach $3,000–$6,000 with added framing.
- Rust treatment/recoating only: $200–$1,000 — and only works before rust has expanded ("rust jacking") and displaced the brick.

**Retaining walls**
- Repair average: **~$700** nationally; typical $450–$950. By problem: efflorescence $220–$480; leaking/seeping $220–$990; cracking/crumbling $335–$1,100; shifted foundation $650–$1,200; bowing/leaning $275–$1,200. Structural engineer: $100–$220/hr.
- New wall: **$15–$70+/sq ft of wall face** by material — timber $15–$35, segmental/poured concrete $25–$60, brick $30–$70. A typical 100 ft × 4 ft timber wall: $6,000–$14,000 installed. Small repair $400–$1,500; new full-yard wall $3,000–$10,000+.
- Walls over ~4 ft of retained fill generally need a permit and engineered design.

**Foundations (where masonry problems turn structural)**
- Typical foundation repair: **$2,200–$8,400**, national average ~$5,000–$5,175.
- Crack sealing/injection: $200–$1,000 per crack. Mudjacking $550–$1,450; foam injection $900–$2,500.
- Underpinning: **$10,000–$30,000** typical (average ~$25,000), up to $50,000; per pier — concrete ~$1,000, steel push ~$1,300–$2,000, helical ~$1,600–$3,000 (some sources up to $3,500–$4,400). Most homes need ~10 piers.
- Wall reinforcement (carbon fiber/steel bracing): $4,000–$12,000. Full foundation replacement: **$20,000–$100,000**. Structural engineer inspection: $200–$800 (report $500–$1,000).

### What bad masonry work costs a wholesale deal

The pattern across every source: masonry fails cheap at the mortar stage and catastrophically at the structural stage, and the jump between the two is roughly 10x.

- **Deferred repointing → spalled brick → rebuild.** Open joints let water in; freeze-thaw widens gaps; brick faces peel off in layers. A $1,450 repointing job ignored becomes $20–$40/sq ft brick replacement, then a section rebuild.
- **Wrong mortar on old brick is permanent damage, not a bad patch.** Pre-1930s brick was laid in soft lime mortar; the mortar is designed to be the sacrificial element — softer and more permeable than the brick, so moisture escapes through the joints and movement is absorbed by the joint. Packing hard Portland-cement mortar (Type S/M) into a lime-mortar wall inverts that: moisture is trapped, water pressure pops the brick faces off, and the brick spalls. Multiple preservation sources call this the single most common and most irreversible error on historic stock; one local board calls using the wrong mortar "a form of demolition." The damage cannot be undone by re-repointing — the brick itself is gone. A wholesaler who sees fresh, hard, gray Portland pointing on a pre-1930s brick house should underwrite brick replacement, not a cosmetic repoint.
- **Sandblasting and aggressive grinding** strip the brick's fired face, making it porous so water enters freely; same spalling outcome. Hand tools are the safe method on historic brick.
- **Chimney neglect is a deal-killer in disguise.** A leaning or crumbling chimney above the roofline is a $1,500–$8,000 partial rebuild, a full rebuild up to $30,000, and a falling-brick liability. A chimney with no cap/crown lets water straight down the flue into the house.
- **Lintel rust jacking.** Rusting steel expands, lifts and cracks the brick above every window and door, and lets water in. What looks like a $300 crack is a $500–$2,500 lintel replacement per opening once brick is removed — and a garage lintel can be $2,000–$5,000.
- **Failed wall ties = mandatory rebuild, no small version.** On 1940s–1960s brick veneer over timber frame, metal ties rust away; the veneer bellies outward in the middle of an elevation. There is no patch — the elevation comes down and goes back up with new stainless ties. Per-tie replacement is quoted $100–$300/tie, but a bellied wall is a rebuild, not a tie job.
- **Retaining wall failure** is a $700 repair if caught at efflorescence/cracking, but a leaning/bowing wall that has moved will not correct itself and the cost rises with every month of movement; replacement runs $15–$70/sq ft plus engineering and permits over 4 ft.
- **Foundation movement behind brick** is the biggest number: $10,000–$30,000 underpinning, $20,000–$100,000 full replacement — enough to erase a wholesale fee several times over if missed.

### How a wholesaler underwrites a building's true condition from its masonry

**Read the cracks (width + pattern + corroboration):**
- Under 1/8 in., hairline, stable over months: usually cosmetic — monitor.
- 1/8–1/4 in.: get a professional evaluation.
- Over 1/4 in., growing, or wider at one end: structural concern, immediate evaluation.
- **Horizontal cracks** in brick/block: lateral soil/water pressure pushing the wall in — most serious pattern; check for bowing with a level.
- **Stair-step (diagonal, 45°) cracks** through mortar joints, especially at window/door corners: differential settlement — the crack points toward the sinking section. If the same pattern appears on interior drywall on the other side of the wall, it is structural, not veneer-only.
- **Cracks through the brick face itself** (not just mortar): more stress than the wall was designed for; active movement.
- **Count signs, don't judge one crack:** two or more of — diagonal cracks at door/window corners, sticking doors/windows, sloping floors — together indicate differential settlement. One hairline crack alone proves almost nothing.
- **Photograph with a ruler or coin for scale, re-check in two weeks, then monthly.** Growth over weeks = active movement; stable over months = old settlement.

**Brick veneer is a drainage wall, not a waterproof wall — inspect the drainage, not just the face:**
- Brick and mortar are porous by design; water gets behind the veneer on every rain. The wall survives because of a 1-inch air cavity, a water-resistive barrier, flashing at the base and above openings, and **weep holes** (small vertical gaps in the bottom mortar course, min. 3/16 in. per code) that let water out.
- If weep holes are mortared shut, caulked over, clogged, buried by soil/mulch, or never installed, water sits in the cavity, rots sheathing and studs, and grows mold — while the brick face looks fine from the street. Inspectors report opening veneer walls to find sheathing "rotted to compost" behind perfect-looking brick.
- Check: weep holes present and open at the base and above lintels; clearance between brick bottom and ground (no soil contact/wicking); efflorescence (white chalky salt staining) = water actively moving through the masonry — not cosmetic, a moisture signal; interior water stains/peeling paint/mustiness on lower walls that look like plumbing leaks but trace to failed exterior masonry.
- Brick veneer itself carries no roof load and does not brace the house — but failed ties, missing weeps, and rotted framing behind it are structural and hidden.

**The 60-second masonry walk (for the wholesaler's own eyes, before any contractor):**
1. Sight along each elevation — any belly, bow, or lean? (Wall ties / lateral pressure — rebuild territory.)
2. Check every window/door corner for stair-step cracks; check lintels for rust staining, sagging brick, or cracks directly above the opening.
3. Check the chimney: lean, missing crown/cap, spalled brick, open joints.
4. Check the base of the walls: weep holes open? Soil piled against brick? Efflorescence? Spalling at grade?
5. Check retaining walls: lean, bulge, cracking, water seeping through.
6. Inside: same crack pattern on the opposite side? Sticking doors, sloping floors?
7. Note the house age: pre-1930s + hard gray Portland repointing = assume spalling damage and price brick replacement, not repointing.

**Underwriting numbers to carry:** repointing $15–$35/sq ft (metro), chimney partial rebuild $1,500–$8,000 / full $10,000–$30,000, lintel $500–$2,500 each, underpinning $1,000–$3,000 per pier × ~10 piers, full foundation replacement $20,000–$100,000. If the masonry signs point to foundation work, the deal needs a structural engineer report ($500–$1,000) before the contract price is final — never price a foundation house off a visual guess.

**Distilled rules (3)**
40. **Price the mortar, underwrite the movement.** Repointing is a cheap, scheduled maintenance item ($15–$35/sq ft); cracks are a diagnostic, not a repair line. Underwrite from the crack pattern — width over 1/4 in., horizontal, stair-step, through-brick, or matching interior cracks — because that pattern is what turns a $1,500 job into a $10,000–$30,000 foundation job, and the pattern is visible for free if you know what to look at.
41. **Never pay for a repair that repeats the cause.** On pre-1930s brick, hard Portland mortar, sandblasting, and grinding are not repairs — they destroy the brick permanently and convert a repointing job into a rebuild. Match the mortar to the brick (lime mortar softer and more permeable than the brick), fix the water path (weeps, flashing, lintel, drainage) first, and only then replace brick. A cheap patch that traps water costs more than no patch at all.
42. **A clean brick face proves nothing — check the drainage and the steel behind it.** Weep holes, flashing, wall ties, lintels, and shelf angles are the parts that fail first and hide longest. Sight the walls for belly and bow, confirm weeps are open, look for rust staining above openings, and treat a bulging veneer or a leaning retaining wall as a rebuild, not a repair — there is no small version of either.

**Signed opportunities — need, how, what must be acquired**
- **Opportunity 1 — Masonry-first deal screening as a wholesaler edge.** Need: buyers lose trust (and deals collapse) when a wholesaler's repair estimate is far off; foundation/masonry is the category that most often kills deals and scares buyers. How: use the 60-second masonry walk above plus the cost table in this section as a standard walkthrough checklist on every brick/masonry property, so the wholesaler's number is within a credible band before the buyer runs theirs. What must be acquired: nothing to buy; the checklist and the per-sq-ft / per-pier / per-lintel cost bands from this research. Never money from Mac, never spend, never hold money.
- **Opportunity 2 — Chimney camera-evidence packet for the buyer.** Need: buyers discount vague repair allowances; documented scope builds trust and justifies the assignment fee. How: where a chimney is a material item, request the seller's last chimney inspection or a Level 2 video-scan inspection during the inspection period, and hand the buyer the report plus the priced repair menu (crown, liner, partial/full rebuild) from this research. What must be acquired: a chimney inspector's report (paid by whoever the contract assigns — not by Mac, not held by the wholesaler); the cost menu comes from this research. Never money from Mac, never spend, never hold money.
- **Opportunity 3 — Historic-brick buyer niche.** Need: preservation-minded buyers pay for historic-correct work, and most flippers price old brick wrong. How: identify pre-1930s brick houses with wrong-mortar damage, underwrite them as brick replacement/lime-mortar restoration, and market the corrected scope to buyers who specialize in historic stock. What must be acquired: knowledge of lime-mortar types (L/O/K) and local preservation-board rules, all from free public sources cited below; no purchase, no trial, no card. Never money from Mac, never spend, never hold money.

**Brought back to Mac — gray areas and boundaries (not acted on)**
- **A. The "free chimney Level 2 inspection" leverage (gray area / value idea).** Who: a wholesaler (or the end buyer) on any deal involving a house with a working fireplace. What: a Level 2 chimney inspection with video scan ($200–$600) is the standard required when buying/selling a home, and it produces camera evidence of flue, crown, and liner condition that a seller cannot argue with. Several chimney companies offer free on-site estimates and a Level 1 inspection bundled into a service visit ($99–$199 standalone). When/where: during the inspection period, before the contract price is final, on any brick house with a chimney. Why: chimney condition is the most commonly underpriced masonry item — a seller's "fine" chimney can hide a $2,500 liner or a $10,000–$30,000 rebuild. Camera evidence converts a vague repair allowance into a documented number and gives the wholesaler a credible, specific price reduction to take to the seller and a credible repair scope to hand the buyer. Gray area: using a free estimate as a negotiating tool is legitimate, but presenting a contractor's free estimate as a firm quote, or implying the work was already priced by a hired professional, would misrepresent it — the estimate is a starting point, not a quote. Mac decides per case.
- **B. The historic-brick / wrong-mortar discount (value idea).** Who: a wholesaler looking at pre-1930s brick houses. What: many older brick houses have already been repointed in hard Portland cement by well-meaning contractors, which guarantees progressive spalling. That damage is visible (flaking brick faces, hard gray joints) and permanent, yet most wholesalers and many buyers price it as a simple repointing job. When/where: on any pre-1930s brick property where fresh hard pointing is visible. Why: correctly underwriting it as brick replacement ($20–$40/sq ft, or a section rebuild) instead of repointing ($15–$35/sq ft) produces a materially lower, defensible offer — and the buyer who understands lime mortar can market the corrected restoration as a value-add (historic-correct restoration commands a premium with preservation-minded buyers). The gray area: in locally designated historic districts, the preservation board's mortar rules (e.g., Type L/O/K lime mortars) are enforceable standards, not preferences — a flipper who repoints in Portland cement there may face a stop-work order or a forced redo, which is a cost the wholesaler must disclose to the buyer rather than bury in the spread. Mac decides per case.
- **C. The weep-hole / drainage "invisible rot" contingency (gray area — disclosure).** Who: wholesaler, seller, and end buyer on any brick-veneer house. What: water trapped behind brick veneer rots framing invisibly for years; the brick face looks fine. A wholesaler who notices blocked/missing weep holes, efflorescence, or interior dampness on lower walls has information the seller may not have — and the buyer cannot see. When/where: at walkthrough, on any brick veneer property, especially post-1970 builds. Why: this is a genuine information advantage, but it sits on a disclosure line. Pricing the deal with a hidden-rot contingency without telling the buyer why the price is low keeps the advantage; telling the buyer exactly what was seen (blocked weeps, efflorescence) and letting them price the risk themselves is the honest version and builds the buyer trust that the wholesaling literature says is the whole business. The loophole to avoid: using the knowledge to buy low from an uninformed seller while concealing a known defect from the end buyer would cross from underwriting skill into misrepresentation in many states' disclosure regimes. Mac decides per case how this knowledge is used.
- **D. Retaining-wall permit threshold as a pricing signal (value idea).** Who: wholesaler on a property with a retaining wall over ~4 ft. What: walls over ~4 ft of retained fill generally require a permit and engineered design, and an unpermitted tall wall is both a cost (engineering + permit + possible rebuild) and a flag that the wall was likely built without drainage or geogrid. When/where: at walkthrough, any property with a tall retaining wall. Why: a leaning or bowing tall wall is not a $700 repair — it is a permitted engineered replacement at $25–$70/sq ft. Spotting the height threshold on the walkthrough lets the wholesaler price the wall correctly where a competitor prices a patch, and a wall that was built without a permit can also be a negotiating point with the seller. No action taken; Mac decides.
