## 14. The scientist's method — hypothesis, test, evidence; the engineering view of a building and a market

**Researched 2026-11-20 (run 11).** Method: public, free sources only, via browser search. No paywall, login, or bot-check circumvention used. No file written, nothing sent, signed, spent, published, or contacted. No trial accepted. One source (CliffsNotes study notes) showed a partial paywall preview; only the freely visible text was used.

### The scientific method, translated for a wholesaler

The classic cycle — observe, ask a question, form a hypothesis, test it, read the evidence, revise — maps directly onto deal work, and the appraisal profession already treats valuation this way. Appraiser Randall Bell's paper on the scientific method and valuation lays out the same five steps for property valuation: identify the problem, collect relevant data, propose a hypothesis, test the hypothesis, and assess its validity — and notes that courts (under the Daubert standard) can exclude expert testimony that does not meet scientific standards. That is the frame: a wholesaler's offer is a claim about value, and it should survive the same kind of scrutiny a court would apply to an appraiser.

The key refinement comes from Karl Popper's falsification idea: a good hypothesis is one that can be proven wrong. Science does not advance by collecting confirmations; it advances by trying to disprove its own guesses. A wholesaler who only looks for evidence that a deal works is doing the opposite of science — and the research on housing-investment bias confirms this is the dominant failure mode. A 2023 literature review (Singh, Kumar, Goel & Johri) found anchoring, loss aversion, and herding are the most-studied biases in real estate investing, with confirmation bias, familiarity bias, and framing bias less explored but present. A 2026 scoping review (Validi et al.) organizes investor biases into six clusters — information-salience, reference-point, overconfidence, imitation/social influence, information-selection/interpretation, and loss/fear/mental-accounting — and stresses they operate differently at each decision stage (attention, valuation, entry, holding, exit).

**The wholesaling loop, in practice:**
1. **Observe** — a lot or house, a seller's situation, a neighborhood, a price pattern.
2. **Ask** — a specific question with a number in it: "Is this lot worth $X to a cash buyer?" not "Is this a good deal?"
3. **Hypothesize** — a testable, falsifiable prediction: "A cash buyer will pay $65,000 for 403 W Church because six comparable small lots sold for $50,000–$90,000." The prediction must name the evidence that would kill it: "If no comparable CMX lot sold above $55,000, the hypothesis fails."
4. **Test cheaply** — free public data first (see below): county assessor/recorder, city GIS, Redfin Data Center, Zillow Research, FRED, FHFA HPI, Census ACS. Then a low-cost live test: a buyer conversation, a seller's answer to "what's your price?", days-on-market movement.
5. **Read the evidence, including the negative kind** — a buyer who passes, a comp that comes in low, a seller who won't budge are all data. A failed test is a successful experiment: it cost nothing and prevented a bad contract.
6. **Iterate** — Popper's cycle is P1 → tentative theory → error elimination → P2 (a new, sharper problem). Each deal that dies should leave behind a sharper rule, not just a shrug.

**What counts as evidence, ranked:**
- Strongest: closed cash sales of truly comparable properties (same zoning, size band, condition, recent).
- Middle: pending sales, list-price movement, days on market, sale-to-list ratio, months of supply.
- Weakest: asking prices, portal estimates (Zestimate/Redfin Estimate), a seller's stated price, a guru's rule of thumb, your own feeling. The 70% rule is a filter, not evidence.

**The three biases that cost wholesalers the most, and their antidotes:**
- **Confirmation bias** — only reading comps that support your number. Antidote: deliberately pull the three *lowest* comparable sales first and argue against your own price.
- **Anchoring** — the first number heard (seller's ask, county assessed value, your own opening offer) drags every later number toward it. Antidote: compute MAO from comps *before* hearing the seller's price, and write it down.
- **Outcome bias** — "the deal closed, so the analysis was right." A profitable deal can still be a lucky one, and a lucky method will eventually fail. Antidote: judge the decision by whether the evidence available at the time supported it, not by whether money arrived.

### The engineering view of a building — measure, don't guess

A building is a machine for carrying loads to the ground and keeping water, air, and people moving through systems. A wholesaler does not need to be an engineer, but needs to know what engineers measure, because repair cost is the second-biggest input to every offer (after ARV).

**Load path — the skeleton.** Every force (roof weight, occupants, wind, earthquake) must travel a continuous chain: roof → rafters → wall plates → load-bearing walls → floor joists → beams/columns → foundation → soil. The most common residential structural failures are breaks in this chain, not extraordinary loads: a missing hold-down at a shear wall, studs above not aligning with studs below, a load-bearing wall removed for an open-plan remodel without a properly sized beam, an undersized header over a window, footings poured on uncompacted fill or expansive clay. ASCE 7 requires a continuous load path; the International Residential Code sets footing and framing minimums. When a load path breaks, the evidence is readable: diagonal cracks at door/window corners, sloping floors, sticking doors, racking, sagging. The diagnostic distinction that matters: **settlement vs. failure.** Uniform settlement (the whole house drops evenly) causes little damage; differential settlement (one part drops more) racks the structure; failure means a member in the load path has stopped carrying load — rot at a sill, a crushed shim stack, sheared brick. Settlement gets measured across a season; failure gets supported and inspected now.

**The six systems and their failure signatures (with typical cost bands from public inspection guides):**
- **Foundation/structure** — hairline shrinkage cracks are normal; horizontal or stair-step cracks, bulging walls, growing gaps are serious. Structural repair typically $3,000–$8,000; minor sealing under $1,500. A general inspection runs $296–$424; a foundation specialist $300–$3,000.
- **Roof** — missing/curling shingles, failed flashing at chimneys and skylights (a leading cause of attic water intrusion), sagging deck. Asphalt shingles last ~20–25 years; a roof near that age is a near-term replacement risk. Patch $500–$1,000; replacement $8,000–$15,000. Roof inspection $125–$357.
- **Plumbing** — galvanized or polybutylene pipe, corroded valves, weak pressure, active leaks; cast-iron drains past lifespan. Simple repair ~$150; full repipe $4,000–$10,000. Inspection $100–$500.
- **Electrical** — Federal Pacific or Zinsco panels, knob-and-tube, aluminum wiring (1965–1973), ungrounded outlets, missing GFCI/AFCI, scorched panels. Outlet upgrades $200–$800; new panel $2,000–$4,000. Inspection $100–$400.
- **HVAC** — cracked heat exchanger (carbon-monoxide risk), refrigerant leaks, poor airflow, age past lifespan (furnace 15–20 yrs, heat pump 12–15, central AC 10–15). Inspection $70–$500.
- **Water/damp** — damp accelerates every other failure by degrading timber and masonry; damp findings and structural findings must be read together, not separately. Mold cleanup can reach $6,000; asbestos removal $15,000; lead paint remediation $15,000.

**The wholesaler's engineering checklist (a hypothesis test for each house):**
- Roof age? (Hypothesis: "roof has 5+ years left" — test: shingle condition, attic stains, permit history.)
- Foundation: uniform or differential? (Test: door/window racking pattern, floor slope direction — one low area, one direction = one story.)
- Panel type and pipe material? (Test: open the panel, look at the pipes — two minutes, no tools.)
- HVAC age from the data plate? (Test: serial number decodes the year.)
- Water: where does it go? (Test: grading, gutter discharge at least 6 feet from foundation, stains in attic/crawlspace.)
- **Walk-away signal from the guides:** when multiple major systems are failing, or estimated repairs reach ~10% or more of the home's value, pause and reassess — the MAO math usually dies before the house does.

**Adaptation and growth (the engineering view of improvement):** buildings adapt by addition (ADUs, extensions), by conversion (open-plan, loft), by system upgrade (panel, repipe, HVAC), and by resilience retrofit (hold-downs, shear walls, drainage). Each adaptation is itself a hypothesis — "adding a unit raises value by $X" — testable against permit data and comparable sales of adapted vs. unadapted properties. The wholesaler's edge is knowing which adaptations buyers actually pay for in that market, and which are expensive traps (a remodel that breaks a load path is a liability, not an upgrade).

### The engineering view of a market — supply, demand, signals

A market is also a system with measurable inputs. The single most useful gauge is **months of supply (months of inventory)**: active listings divided by the average monthly sales pace. It answers one question — how long would it take to sell everything currently listed if nothing new appeared?

- **Under ~3 months:** seller's market — prices rise, bidding wars, almost no negotiating room.
- **~3–6 months:** balanced — prices flat to slight gains, moderate negotiating room.
- **Over ~6 months:** buyer's market — prices dip, sellers offer concessions, real negotiating room.
- Researchers at the Texas Real Estate Research Center put balanced at 6–6.5 months (with local equilibrium usually 5–7 months), and note the new-home equilibrium is lower, around 3–4 months. The 6-month line is an industry convention, not a federally published threshold — NAR defines buyer/seller markets qualitatively and publishes the months'-supply ingredients monthly. National existing-home supply was 4.6 months in July 2026 (NAR, released August 2026) — below the buyer-market line nationally, while individual metros sat above it. **The lesson: compute months of supply for your county and your price band, not the country.**

**The signal stack — confirm with three signals, not one:**
1. **Months of supply** (the ratio — moves first).
2. **Days on market** rising against that market's own norm (moves second).
3. **Share of listings taking price cuts, and sale-to-list ratio drifting below ~98%** (moves third; headline median price moves last).

Other measurable market signals: permits and starts (future supply — Census/FRED HOUST), mortgage rates (demand throttle — FRED MORTGAGE30US), unemployment and wage growth (BLS, local), migration (IRS SOI, Census), price-to-income and price-to-rent ratios (FHFA HPI, Zillow ZORI, HUD fair market rents), and delinquency (FRED DRSFRMACBS). A wholesaler who tracks five numbers monthly — months of supply, median days on market, sale-to-list ratio, share of price cuts, and 30-year rate — has a better market model than most agents, for $0.

**Market hypothesis template:** "In [county/price band], months of supply is [X] and rising, days on market is [Y] vs. a [Z]-month norm, therefore buyers' negotiating room is [growing/shrinking], so my offer ceiling should move [down/up] by [amount]." Kill condition stated in advance: "If months of supply falls below 4 next month, this read is wrong."

### The math — MAO as a falsifiable claim

The Maximum Allowable Offer is the hypothesis in number form. Two versions, both public and widely documented:

- **Quick (filter):** MAO = ARV × 70% − Repairs. Example: $250,000 ARV × 70% − $40,000 repairs = $135,000. The 30% buffer must cover financing, holding, buying/selling closing costs, and profit — for a wholesaler, the assignment fee comes out of the buyer's side of that buffer, which is why the wholesale contract price is MAO minus the assignment fee.
- **Detailed (the real offer):** MAO = ARV − Repairs − Buy/Sell/Hold costs − Your profit − Investor buyer's profit. BiggerPockets' version uses buy/sell/hold at ~15% of ARV for a flipper (less for a wholesaler who never closes, roughly $1,500 in their example) and investor-buyer profit of $1.00–$1.25 per dollar of rehab, floored at $10,000 and capped around $30,000.

**The scientific point:** the 70% is a starting assumption, not a law — sources stress adjusting it by market (65–75%) and by exit strategy. Every input is a sub-hypothesis: ARV rests on comps (testable), repairs rest on a scope and bids (testable), holding time rests on days-on-market data (testable). A deal where ARV is built on one cherry-picked comp and repairs on a guess is not a hypothesis — it is a wish. The honest version of the 70% rule is: "I believe the market will pay 70% of ARV for this house in this condition, and here are the three sales that would prove me wrong."

### Free public data — the test lab (all $0, no account needed unless noted)

- **County assessor & recorder** — Legal source of truth: ownership, lot size, tax history, deeds, mortgages, liens. County site, free.
- **City/county GIS portals** — Parcel boundaries, zoning, land use, flood/hazard layers, shapefiles. Public ArcGIS/open-data portal, free.
- **Redfin Data Center** — Median price, inventory, days on market, price drops, sale-to-list ratio, months of supply — down to ZIP/neighborhood, weekly. Free downloads, no account.
- **Zillow Research** — ZHVI home values, ZORI rents, forecasts, sale-to-list — metro/city/ZIP CSVs. Free downloads; API is partner-gated.
- **FRED (St. Louis Fed)** — Mortgage rates, housing starts, existing-home sales, unemployment, CPI, delinquency. Free, fred.stlouisfed.org.
- **FHFA House Price Index** — Repeat-sales price index, quarterly, state/metro/ZIP. Free downloads, fhfa.gov.
- **Census ACS / data.census.gov** — Income, population, vacancy, tenure, year built, mobility — county/tract. Free.
- **HUD (PD&R / USHMC)** — Supply, demand, finance, ownership/vacancy indicators, fair market rents. Free, huduser.gov.
- **BLS** — Local unemployment, employment, wages. Free, bls.gov.
- **Realtor.com research** — Monthly listing prices, inventory, days on market, competitiveness. Free public reports.

Note: there is no single national property database — records sit in ~3,000+ county offices, each with its own system. That fragmentation is itself a fact about the market, and a source of edge for anyone willing to do the county-level work others skip.

**Distilled rules (3)**
43. **Write the kill condition before you write the offer.** Every deal hypothesis must name the evidence that would make you walk away — a comp below $X, a repair above $Y, months of supply above Z — and you must walk away when it appears. A hypothesis you cannot lose is not a hypothesis; it is a sales pitch to yourself.
44. **Test with the cheapest evidence first, and count the test as a win when it kills the deal.** Free public data, a GIS zoning check, three low comps, a buyer phone call — in that order. A dead deal that cost $0 and one hour is a successful experiment; a live deal built on untested numbers is the expensive one.
45. **Read the building like a system and the market like a gauge.** Roof, foundation, panel, pipes, HVAC, water — six systems, each with a measurable age and failure signature. Months of supply, days on market, sale-to-list ratio — three signals, checked monthly, at county and price-band level. If you cannot measure it, you are guessing, and guessing is what the other wholesalers are already doing.

**Signed opportunities — need, how, what must be acquired**
- **Opportunity 1 — The base-rate table.** Need: wholesalers (starting with Mac) need to know, before offering, how often deals in a given ZIP/price band actually survive — the base rate — instead of judging each deal on its own story. How: file a one-page autopsy for every deal evaluated (hypothesis, kill condition, evidence, verdict, outcome) and tally the results monthly. All inputs are free public data plus the wholesaler's own notes. What must be acquired: a simple tally sheet (free spreadsheet), discipline to record dead deals, and 30–50 evaluated deals to make the rates meaningful. Never money from Mac, never spend, never hold money.
- **Opportunity 2 — The three-signal market brief.** Need: cash buyers want a short, honest monthly read on their farm area (months of supply, days on market, sale-to-list ratio) to time offers — most agents send hype, not numbers. How: pull the three signals monthly from the free Redfin Data Center and FRED, write a half-page brief in plain language, and share it with the buyer list as a trust-building touch (not a sales pitch). What must be acquired: a free spreadsheet, a monthly 30-minute routine, and the buyer contacts Mac already has. Never money from Mac, never spend, never hold money.
- **Opportunity 3 — The six-system walkthrough card.** Need: sellers and buyers both need a fast, honest read on a house's six systems (roof, foundation, plumbing, electrical, HVAC, water) before money is discussed. How: a pocket card listing each system's age test, failure signature, and typical cost band (from the public ranges above), used on every walkthrough to produce a repair estimate that is a hypothesis with named tests — not a guess. What must be acquired: the card itself (written once, from the public sources above) and the habit of checking the panel, pipes, and data plates on every visit. Never money from Mac, never spend, never hold money.

**Brought back to Mac — gray areas (not acted on)**
- **A. The "free public data as a product" gray area.** Who: any wholesaler or data packager. What: county assessor/recorder data, GIS zoning layers, and Redfin/Zillow public downloads are free public records, but they are scattered across ~3,000 county systems with inconsistent formats — the work of assembling them into a clean, ZIP-level "months of supply + distress + zoning" sheet is real labor. When: now; the data is published continuously. Where: any county, starting with Fresno County. Why it matters: the gap between "public" and "usable" is the whole business — but the line between repackaging public records (generally lawful) and scraping a portal in violation of its terms of use, or reselling data whose license forbids commercial redistribution (Redfin/Zillow terms reserve rights; IDX/MLS data is licensed for personal, non-commercial consumer use only), is a genuine legal gray area. Selling a report built on MLS-derived data you are not licensed to redistribute could breach terms and, in some states, licensing rules. **Brought back to Mac — no action taken.**
- **B. The appraisal-standard gray area.** Who: wholesalers who present valuations to sellers or buyers. What: Bell's paper notes courts can exclude valuation testimony that fails scientific standards (Daubert). A wholesaler is not an appraiser and must not present as one, but the same logic cuts the other way: a wholesaler who hands a seller a written "value analysis" built on cherry-picked comps is making a quasi-appraisal claim without a license. When/where: any seller conversation where numbers are put in writing. Why: the safe side is labeling analysis as an opinion for negotiation, citing sources, and never claiming licensed-appraiser authority. **Brought back to Mac — no action taken.**
- **C. The "kill-condition" licensing idea (new value idea).** Who: wholesalers who lose deals to their own optimism. What: a one-page "deal autopsy" sheet — hypothesis, kill condition, evidence found, verdict — filed for every dead deal, so the dead deals become the training data. The compounding value is that after 30–50 autopsies, the wholesaler owns a private base-rate table ("in this ZIP, at this price band, X% of deals die on repair cost, Y% on ARV") that no guru sells. When: immediately, using only free data and the wholesaler's own notes. Where: the wholesaler's own files. Why: base rates beat anecdotes, and the table is an asset that can later be taught, licensed as a course, or used to price a buyers list — but only after it is built from real outcomes, never invented. **Brought back to Mac — no action taken.**
- **D. The engineering-report arbitrage (gray area / value idea).** Who: a wholesaler, a licensed home inspector, and a cash buyer. What: inspection reports cost $300–$500 and are typically commissioned by one buyer for one deal, then discarded. A wholesaler who commissions (or obtains with permission) a proper inspection and shares its findings transparently with the end buyer reduces the buyer's uncertainty — which is worth money — but reselling or reusing an inspection report commissioned by someone else, or presenting an old report as current, can mislead and may breach the inspector's terms. When: at the point a property is under contract. Where: any house deal. Why: transparency about real defects is a trust asset; stale or second-hand reports are a liability. **Brought back to Mac — no action taken.**

**Sources (26)**
- Randall Bell, "The Scientific Method and the Valuation Process," The Appraisal Journal — https://static1.squarespace.com/static/5af617637106994d8097cbee/t/5afb3f5bf950b764b0199c0e/1526415198235/Scientific-Method-and-the-Valuation-Process.pdf
- Karl Popper: Falsification Theory, Simply Psychology — https://www.simplypsychology.org/karl-popper.html
- Popper — Science versus non-science: Conjecture and refutation, Northeastern University — https://www.khoury.northeastern.edu/home/lieber/evergreen/specker/scientific-method/Popper-Carla-Fehr.html

## 15. Loopholes and gray areas found (grows as found — reported, never used without Mac's per-case say-so)

**Researched 2026-11-20 (run 11).** Sources: public, free web sources only. This is research, not legal advice. No law was invented; where sources conflict or a claim is unverified, it is said so. Mac decides per case. Nothing here was acted on.

### Headline finding

Wholesaling itself is **legal in all 50 states — no state bans it outright** as of Oct–Nov 2026 — but that headline hides the real risk. The legality turns entirely on **how the deal is structured, marketed, and disclosed**. At least 16–22 states now have wholesaler-specific statutes (counts differ by tracker), most enacted in 2024–2026, and the trend is from unregulated toward disclosure, cancellation windows, licensing, and registration. California — Mac's market, Fresno — has **no wholesaler-specific statute in force**, but its general broker-licensing law is actively enforced, and a licensing bill (AB 1850) stalled in committee in May 2026.

### 1. The core gray line: equitable interest vs. unlicensed brokerage

**Who:** Any wholesaler without a real-estate license; state real-estate commissions / Departments of Real Estate; sellers and end buyers.
**What:** When you sign a purchase agreement you gain *equitable interest* — a contractual right to buy, not legal title. Contract law generally lets you transfer that right unless the contract forbids it. You are selling *your contract*, not the house. The moment you market *the property itself* — "house for sale," photos, features, showings, negotiating for the seller — regulators treat it as brokerage for another person for compensation.
**When:** At signing and at every advertisement, text, call, and listing, in every state, now.
**Where:** Every state. California, New York, and others enforce it through general license law even without a wholesaler statute.
**Why:** Licensing laws target people who negotiate or market property on someone else's behalf for a fee. A principal selling their own interest is exempt; an agent is not. Regulators focus on whether you bear genuine transaction risk or are just collecting a fee for connecting two parties.
**Legal / lawful?** Lawful **if** you are a genuine principal with a signed contract, market only your equitable interest / assignment of contract, and do not represent either side. Unlawful if you market property you do not own or act as the seller's agent without a license. Penalties include cease-and-desist orders, fines, voided contracts, and in some states Class A misdemeanor / jail.
**Mac decides per case:** Whether any given ad, script, or listing crosses from "assignment of contract for sale" into "property for sale" — wording by wording, before it goes out.

### 2. Marketing the contract, not the property — the wording loophole that is also the trap

**Who:** Wholesaler marketing to a buyers list, on social media, SMS, or a deals site.
**What:** Compliant ads describe the *contract terms* — purchase price, assignment fee, closing timeline — and state plainly that you are offering an assignment of your purchase contract, not the property. Non-compliant ads describe the house as if you own or list it.
**When:** Before and during buyer marketing, after the purchase contract is signed. Marketing *before* you have a signed contract means you have no equitable interest at all — no principal status, no legal basis.
**Where:** Everywhere; Oregon's rule (Or. Admin. Code § 863-017-0025, eff. 7/1/2025) is the clearest example — disclosure in at least 10-point bold type to sellers, buyers, and in all advertising, stating you hold only equitable interest, do not hold title, may not be licensed, and may not be able to give a value opinion. Social-media ads are exempt only if they link to a compliant page.
**Why:** The ad is usually the evidence regulators and sellers' lawyers use first.
**Legal / lawful?** Marketing the contract is lawful; marketing the property without a license is not, in most states.
**Mac decides per case:** Approve the exact ad copy for each deal; never reuse a "house for sale" template.

### 3. The assignment-fee visibility gray area — and the double-close / novation workaround

**Who:** Wholesaler, seller, end buyer, title/escrow company.
**What:** In a straight assignment, the fee usually appears on the settlement statement, so the seller sees the spread and may renegotiate or walk. A double close (two closings, A→B then B→C) and a novation (new contract substituting the end buyer, fee paid through a side agreement) both keep the spread off the seller's settlement statement. Novation spreads are typically reported at $10,000–$30,000+ vs. $5,000–$10,000 for assignments.
**When:** At exit-structure choice, before the assignment agreement or second contract is signed.
**Where:** Nationwide; several states are now restricting undisclosed assignment fees, which is reported as pushing operators toward novation. Ohio's regulator is reported to be watching for "novation misuse as a disclosure workaround."
**Why:** Privacy of margin vs. transparency. Hiding the fee is not itself illegal in most places, but using a structure *to evade a disclosure duty* is where it becomes a gray area / enforcement target.
**Legal / lawful?** Assignment, double close, and novation are all lawful structures in themselves. Lawfulness depends on whether the applicable state disclosure was made and whether the fee was run through escrow vs. paid as a concealed side payment. One California-focused source advises: keep the fee on the settlement statement and run everything through escrow; a side payment "looks like concealment even when it is not."
**Mac decides per case:** Assignment (transparent, cheap) vs. double close (private, costs ~1.5–2% transactional funding + second closing costs) vs. novation (retail buyer, seller consent required) — per deal, per spread size, per state disclosure rule.

### 4. Assignor residual liability — assignment does NOT release you

**Who:** Wholesaler as assignor; assignee (end buyer); seller.
**What:** An assignment transfers only *rights*, not obligations. If the assignee backs out or cannot close, the seller can still pursue the original wholesaler for damages. Only a novation — all three parties agreeing the assignee completely replaces the assignor — extinguishes that liability. Many standard contracts also contain anti-assignment clauses; courts routinely enforce them.
**When:** From signing through closing, and after a failed closing.
**Where:** General contract law, all states.
**Why:** Wholesalers who assume "I assigned it, I'm out" are the ones caught by this.
**Legal / lawful?** Lawful structure; the gray area is *assuming* release without getting it in writing.
**Mac decides per case:** Whether to seek a written release / novation on each assignment, and whether the contract's assignment clause is unrestricted or requires seller consent.

### 5. Memorandum of contract / clouding title — protection that becomes slander of title

**Who:** Wholesaler recording a memorandum/affidavit of contract; seller trying to sell to someone else; county recorder; title company.
**What:** Recording a memorandum creates a public record of your equitable interest and can stop the seller selling to another buyer while your contract is live. It becomes abusive when the contract has expired, been cancelled, or the wholesaler never had the intent/ability to close — then the seller cannot sell, and the wholesaler demands a payout to release it. That is the fact pattern in multiple BiggerPockets / Justia cases: $20 earnest money, missed closing, memorandum filed, seller told to pay to free the title.
**When:** Only while a valid, live contract exists, with contingencies satisfied/removed and written readiness to perform. Never after expiry/cancellation.
**Where:** All states; Connecticut, Oklahoma, and Louisiana are reported to now ban wholesalers from recording anything that clouds title.
**Why:** A recorded claim is powerful leverage — which is exactly why misusing it draws slander-of-title and quiet-title lawsuits, compensatory and punitive damages, and attorney-general / real-estate-commission complaints.
**Legal / lawful?** Lawful when the contract is valid and live. Unlawful / actionable as slander of title when the claim is false, expired, or filed to extort a release payment. One practitioner source puts it bluntly: if you are tying up properties hoping to find a buyer and have no intent/ability to close yourself, "you DO NOT even have valid contracts" and clouding title exposes you to suit.
**Mac decides per case:** Whether to record a memorandum at all on any Fresno lot; never record after a deal dies, and never use a release as a bargaining chip.

### 6. Daisy chains, co-wholesaling, and JV agreements — the second-hand-deal trap

**Who:** Wholesaler A (on contract), wholesaler B (marketing A's deal), end buyer; also JV partners.
**What:** A "daisy chain" is marketing someone else's deal when your name is not on the contract with the owner. In Louisiana discussion and general license law, that is described as illegal unless all parties' names are on the contract with the owner — the non-contract party marketing the property/contract is practising real estate without a license. Documented JV / co-wholesaling structures, where each party is a principal or is named on the contract, are reported as still lawful in California, New York, and Illinois when done through the proper structure.
**When:** Whenever a deal is passed between wholesalers before reaching the end buyer.
**Where:** All states; flagged explicitly in Louisiana, California, New York, Illinois guidance.
**Why:** Each link in the chain adds a fee and a party with no equitable interest — the exact profile of a broker.
**Legal / lawful?** Daisy-chaining another wholesaler's deal without being on the contract: treated as unlicensed brokerage — not lawful. A documented JV with a cash buyer, or co-wholesaling where parties are named / principal: lawful if structured and disclosed correctly.
**Mac decides per case:** Any JV / co-wholesale split must be documented, named on the contract or by separate JV agreement, and disclosed — never a silent hand-off of someone else's contract.

### 7. State licensing / registration thresholds — the "one free deal" loophole is closing

**Who:** Wholesalers operating across state lines; state regulators (IDFPR in Illinois, etc.).
**What:** No state bans wholesaling, but the licensing line varies sharply:
- **Illinois (225 ILCS 454/1-10, 2019):** 2+ qualifying contract transactions in any 12 months = broker, license required. One deal per rolling 12 months is permitted unlicensed. Penalties up to $25,000 per violation. Rotating deals through multiple LLCs under common ownership does *not* reset the counter — the statute aggregates across entities.
- **Virginia (Va. Code § 54.1-2100):** same 2-in-12-months line.
- **Pennsylvania (Act 52 of 2024, eff. 4 Jan 2025):** any wholesale transaction is broker activity — license required from deal one; seller can cancel until midnight on the 30th day after signing or until conveyance, whichever is first. Philadelphia adds its own city wholesaler license on top.
- **South Carolina (Act 204 / HB 4754, 2024):** marketing a property you only have under contract is brokerage; licensed firms are *barred* from wholesaling as defined — a license alone does not solve it, and getting licensed can close the door. Assigning the contract right itself is carved out.
- **Iowa (Iowa Code § 543B.6A):** broker or representation by one required.
- **Rhode Island:** license required from 1 Jan 2027.
- **Oregon:** registration (fee up to $300, criminal-records check); **Connecticut:** registration from 1 July 2026 ($285).
- **Oklahoma (HB 1148 / SB 1075):** license to publicly market deals + pre-contract disclosure, 2-business-day cancellation, earnest money in Oklahoma escrow.
- **Nebraska / Kentucky:** regulated through license law / advertising rules.
**When:** Now, and tightening — 8 of 17 statutes tracked in one Oct 2026 review took effect in 2025–2026; two more licensing deadlines hit in early 2027.
**Where:** As listed above; West Virginia's proposed Abolishment of Wholesaling Act (HB 4493, 2026) died in committee — not law.
**Why:** States are moving from "unregulated" to "consumer-protection regulation" — disclosures, cancellation windows, licensing.
**Legal / lawful?** Lawful only within each state's threshold. The "one deal a year" or "use another LLC" workarounds are either expressly aggregated (Illinois) or simply not a defence elsewhere.
**Mac decides per case:** State-by-state before any out-of-state deal; in California the question is different (see §9) — but any deal in IL, VA, PA, SC, IA, OK, OR, CT, RI, or NE needs a state-specific check first.

### 8. Disclosure and cancellation windows — the new compliance core

**Who:** Wholesaler and seller (and assignee in several states).
**What:** A growing list of states require *written* disclosure *before* the purchase contract is signed — that you are a wholesaler, will only hold equitable interest, will not take title, may profit by assignment, and may not be licensed — plus a seller cancellation right if disclosure is missing or even, in some states, even when it is perfect:
- Texas (SB 1577, Jan 2024): written disclosure to seller and assignee, or it counts as brokerage.
- Indiana (HEA 1068, Jul 2024): disclose role/intent; written contracts with set expiration dates.
- Ohio (SB 155, eff. 2 Mar 2026; ORC §5301.95): standalone disclosure in boldface 12-point font, signed/dated by both parties, *before* the purchase agreement. Missing it: seller can cancel any time before closing, sue for actual + up to $5,000 non-economic damages, treble damages under the Consumer Sales Practices Act; licensed agents face OREC fines up to $2,500 per violation.
- Tennessee: bold, large-print disclosures + 3 business days' notice before an assignment.
- Missouri (eff. 28 Aug 2026; §407.3600): disclosure **14 calendar days before** the contract — the longest lead time found.
- Oklahoma: 2 business days to cancel; Connecticut / Oregon: 3 business days; Pennsylvania: 30 days or until conveyance.
- If disclosure is missing, in Arizona, Iowa, Missouri, North Dakota, Ohio, Oklahoma, Oregon, and Wisconsin the seller can generally walk away and keep the wholesaler's earnest money (in Ohio/Missouri the escrow agent pays it to the owner within 30 days).
**When:** Before signing, in the form the state prescribes (standalone document, bold type, specific font size) — burying it in the purchase contract is flagged as an enforcement target in Ohio.
**Where:** AZ, MD, MO, ND, OH, OK, TN, TX, WI, IN, plus OR/CT/PA as above.
**Why:** The disclosure is now the whole regulatory model in disclosure states — it replaces licensing.
**Legal / lawful?** Lawful only with the prescribed disclosure, in the prescribed form, at the prescribed time. A verbal disclosure is unprovable.
**Mac decides per case:** Which state's disclosure form, font, timing, and cancellation clock applies to each deal — and whether to disclose *more* than the minimum (fee, intent, no-title) even where California does not yet require it.

### 9. California — Mac's market: legal, but the enforcement line is wording, and a licensing bill is waiting

**Who:** Mac (Fresno / 93706 lots); California Department of Real Estate (DRE); California Association of Realtors (sponsor of AB 1850).
**What:** California has **no wholesaler-specific statute in force**. Assigning a purchase contract is lawful under Civil Code §954 (a right arising from an obligation can be transferred). The principal-buyer exemption under Business & Professions Code §10130 protects investors acting in their own interest. What needs a license is acting as a broker under BPC §§10130–10131: advertising a home you have no right to sell, or getting paid to match a buyer with someone else's property. The DRE sends cease-and-desist letters to wholesalers who market the property instead of their contract, can cite and fine unlicensed persons up to $2,500 per unlicensed act, and publishes enforcement monthly. Wilful unlicensed brokerage: fines up to $20,000 and up to 6 months in county jail for individuals ($60,000 for corporations) under BPC §10139. The standard C.A.R. Residential Purchase Agreement gives only a *limited* assignment right requiring seller approval (17-day window in one account); wholesalers commonly add a separate assignment addendum (AOAA) stating an unrestricted right to assign.
**When:** Now. AB 1850 (introduced 11 Feb 2026) would have defined wholesaling as brokerage, required a DRE license, and required written disclosure that the wholesaler will not take title and intends to assign for profit — failure deemed substantial misrepresentation. It was **held in the Assembly Appropriations Committee on 14 May 2026 and is not law**. A second California source mentions "AB 1720" tightening assignment disclosure — **that reference could not be verified from the primary sources in this search; treat it as unverified/conflicting and do not rely on it.**
**Where:** California statewide, including Fresno County / City of Fresno lots. No separate Fresno wholesaler ordinance was found in this search.
**Why:** California is the "no statute, but regulators enforce anyway" state — the DRE uses general broker law, so a wholesaler often does not know they crossed the line until the DRE letter arrives, and the violation is almost always the marketing wording.
**Legal / lawful?** Lawful as a principal assigning an equitable interest with proper contract language and escrow handling. Unlawful as unlicensed brokerage if you market the property or represent the seller.
**Mac decides per case:** (a) Use the C.A.R. RPA + AOAA assignment language or a California land-adapted agreement with an explicit unrestricted assignment clause — per deal; (b) disclose in writing in the contract itself (not a side addendum the seller may not read) that you may assign and may profit, and that the seller's proceeds do not change; (c) run the fee through escrow on the settlement statement; (d) watch for a revived AB 1850-style bill in 2027 — if it passes, the model changes to license-required.

**Opportunity sign — California's current gap:**
- Need/desire/want: Sellers of off-market, vacant, and distressed lots who need speed, not top dollar; developers who need CMX land by right.
- How it can be done: Principal-buyer assignment of land contracts, marketed as assignment of contract only, with written no-title disclosure voluntarily given even though not yet statutorily required in California.
- What must be acquired to do it: A California attorney-reviewed assignment clause / land agreement; a title/escrow company that handles assignments; a buyers list of developers. Never money from Mac, never spend, never hold money.

### 10. Vacant-land carve-out? — the under-enforced gray area most relevant to Mac's four CMX lots

**Who:** Wholesalers of vacant land (Mac's 333/403/433 W Church and 2233 S Fruit lots); state regulators.
**What:** Most of the new wholesaler statutes are written for *residential* property / residential wholesale transactions (e.g., South Carolina defines wholesaling for residential property; Pennsylvania's Act 52 is residential; Oregon's rule is residential property wholesalers). That raises a genuine gray question: does a vacant-land assignment fall outside a *residential* wholesaler statute? No source in this search answered that for California or for land generally, and California has no residential-wholesaler statute to be carved out of anyway — general broker law (BPC §10131) covers "real property," not just houses.
**When:** On every land deal, before assuming the residential rules do not apply.
**Where:** States with residential-only statutes; California is not one of them for this purpose.
**Why:** Land has no occupant, no foreclosure-occupant protections, and often no MLS listing — so it attracts less regulator attention, which is *under-enforcement*, not an exemption.
**Legal / lawful?** **Unresolved — treat as gray, not as a loophole.** General unlicensed-brokerage law still applies to land in California and everywhere else; marketing the *lot* as if you own it is still brokerage. Do not treat "it's land, not a house" as a defence without a California attorney's written view.
**Mac decides per case:** Whether to seek that attorney view for the four CMX lots specifically, and whether to voluntarily apply the residential disclosure standard to land deals anyway.

### 11. Double close and transactional funding — lawful privacy, with a cost and a lender trap

**Who:** Wholesaler; transactional funder; title company; end buyer's lender.
**What:** Two closings (A→B, then B→C, often same day). The funder wires A→B money straight to the title company (never to the wholesaler), is repaid from B→C proceeds at closing. Fees typically 1–2% of the A→B price, sometimes plus a $499 document fee / $30 wire fee; some funders require no proof of funds and no credit check. The seller sees only the A→B price; the end buyer sees only the B→C price.
**When:** When the spread is large (guides suggest >$20,000–$30,000), the end buyer is institutional / financed, or the contract bars assignment.
**Where:** 21+ states served by the funders cited; not every lender allows same-day resales — seasoning / title-of-record issues can block a financed end buyer.
**Why:** Privacy and a clean title chain, at the price of two closing-cost sets and funding fees (worked example: $150,000 contract, $15,000 spread → ~$10,250 net after ~$4,750 in extra costs).
**Legal / lawful?** Lawful. The gray area is using a double close *solely* to hide a fee that a state requires to be disclosed — then the structure does not cure the disclosure breach.
**Mac decides per case:** Assignment vs. double close per deal, weighing the funding cost against the risk that the seller sees the spread and renegotiates.

**Opportunity sign — Transactional funding as a no-cash bridge:**
- Need/desire/want: Wholesaler needs to take title for a few hours to keep the spread private or to satisfy a financed buyer, without holding cash.
- How it can be done: Same-day double close funded by a transactional funder wired to title, repaid from the B→C closing.
- What must be acquired to do it: A funder that funds in California, a title company that does same-day double closes, and an end buyer whose lender accepts the structure. Never money from Mac, never spend, never hold money — the funds go title-to-title.

### 12. Subject-to and wraps — lawful, but the due-on-sale clause is a lender option, not a crime

**Who:** Seller (loan stays in their name); buyer/investor taking title; wholesaler assigning a subject-to contract; lender.
**What:** In a subject-to deal the buyer takes title while the seller's mortgage stays in the seller's name and the buyer makes the payments. Nearly every modern mortgage has a due-on-sale (alienation) clause letting the lender demand the full balance if the property is transferred without written consent. Under the Garn–St Germain Act (12 U.S.C. §1701j-3, 1982), that clause is enforceable almost everywhere, notwithstanding contrary state law. It is an *option*, not an automatic event — nothing happens on recording day; the lender decides later whether to act. The nine statutory exemptions are almost all family / death / divorce / estate transfers (e.g., transfer on death, divorce, a lease ≤3 years without a purchase option) — none describes a stranger buying the house and taking over payments. The seller can also trigger the call themselves by calling the lender.
**When:** For years after closing — the exposure lasts as long as the loan is open.
**Where:** Federal law, all states.
**Why:** Wholesalers who treat subject-to like a cash flip — big entry fee, short balloon, assign within a month and disappear — leave the *investor* exposed and the *seller* exposed on credit, with no relationship to manage it.
**Legal / lawful?** Subject-to itself is not a crime; it is a breach of the mortgage contract's terms if the lender enforces it, and enforcement can mean acceleration or foreclosure. The gray area is selling a subject-to *contract* to a third party without the seller understanding the loan stays in their name and can be called.
**Mac decides per case:** Whether to touch subject-to at all; if so, only with a plain-language due-on-sale risk statement signed by the seller, a plan for a called loan, and no assignment of a subject-to deal to a stranger without the seller's informed consent.

### 13. Seller carry / seller financing, wraps, and the SAFE Act / Dodd-Frank line

**Who:** Seller financing a sale; wholesaler structuring or assigning a seller-carry deal; end-buyer occupant.
**What:** The moment a seller extends credit — promissory note, carry-back mortgage, land contract / contract for deed — federal mortgage rules apply. The SAFE Act requires loan-originator licensing for those who, habitually/repeatedly, take applications and negotiate residential mortgage terms for compensation. Dodd-Frank / Regulation Z requires an ability-to-repay determination. There are numerical exemptions: a natural person financing **one** property in 12 months need not determine ability-to-repay (no balloon before 5 years, no negative amortisation, adjustable rate tied to a national index and fixed ≥5 years); **three or fewer** properties in 12 months avoids the full ATR/QM weight but must be fully amortising (≤30 years), no balloon at all, no negative amortisation, rate fixed ≥5 years, and the seller must determine in good faith the buyer can repay. Entities (LLC/corp/trust) do *not* get the one-property exemption — they fall under the three-property rule. Above three, use a licensed Residential Mortgage Loan Originator (RMLO); reported RMLO cost $800–$1,500 per deal (other sources $99–$2,000+). These rules do **not** apply to commercial sales, sales to an investor not living in the home, or properties with 5+ units.
**When:** Per 12-month rolling count of seller-financed deals.
**Where:** Federal, all states, plus state usury, land-contract, and recording rules that vary sharply.
**Why:** A wholesaler who *negotiates* seller-financing terms for a seller and a buyer, repeatedly, can cross into loan origination without a licence.
**Legal / lawful?** Lawful within the 1- / 3-property exemptions and their loan-feature limits; unlawful without a licence / RMLO above them, or with prohibited features (balloon, negative amortisation).
**Mac decides per case:** Count of seller-financed deals in any 12 months; whether the buyer will occupy (consumer loan) vs. is an investor; whether to use an RMLO on any seller-carry structure rather than drafting terms himself.

### 14. Cold calling, texting, skip tracing — TCPA / DNC is the highest-dollar gray area in outreach

**Who:** Wholesaler (or their dialer/text vendor); homeowner on a purchased/skip-traced list; FTC/FCC.
**What:** TCPA (47 U.S.C. §227) requires **prior express written consent** before autodialed calls, AI/prerecorded voice, or texts to cell phones — even to a business line, even if no call is answered. The National Do Not Call Registry applies to wholesaler telemarketing calls; lists must be scrubbed at least every 31 days. The Telemarketing Sales Rule requires disclosures, calls only 8 a.m.–9 p.m. local time, and records kept ≥2 years. Private right of action: **$500 per violation, tripled to $1,500 if wilful** — a 1,000-number text blast can mean $1.5M in exposure. An established business relationship (inquiry within 3 months / purchase within 18 months) is a narrow exception. Manual dialing to a number *not* on the DNC list is generally described as permissible cold calling; autodialers, AI voices, ringless voicemail, and bulk SMS are not without consent. The FCC treats AI voice cloning the same as prerecorded messages. As of 11 Apr 2025, revocation by "STOP"/"QUIT" must be honoured within 10 business days.
**When:** Every campaign, before a single dial/text.
**Where:** Federal, all states; Florida has its own broader autodialer law (FTSA) and its own DNC list.
**Why:** This is where a $0-marketing wholesaler can create six-figure liability in an afternoon — and where "the list vendor said it was clean" is not a defence without consent records.
**Legal / lawful?** Manual cold calling off-DNC numbers: lawful with TSR compliance. Autodialed / AI / bulk-text outreach without prior express written consent: unlawful. Skip tracing itself is not the violation — *calling/texting* the traced number without consent / DNC scrub is.
**Mac decides per case:** Whether any outreach list is scrubbed, consent-documented, and manually dialled — or whether to use only inbound / opt-in and door-knock / mail channels for the Fresno lots. Never approve a bulk SMS blast to a purchased list.

### 15. Vulnerable sellers and foreclosure — where "gray" becomes predatory

**Who:** Homeowners in foreclosure, probate, divorce, or with diminished capacity; wholesaler making a low offer.
**What:** Targeting distressed owners and pressuring very low offers is described across sources as unethical and, in some cases, illegal. California has additional protections for homeowners selling while in default — the Home Equity Sales Contract Act — including contract requirements and a cancellation right, precisely because that situation attracts bad actors. A practitioner source adds a second test: do not ignore a seller's mental-capacity issues.
**When:** At first contact and at signing, especially in foreclosure / probate / divorce.
**Where:** California (HESCA) and equivalent foreclosure-sale protections in other states; general consumer-protection / fraud law everywhere.
**Why:** A low price freely agreed by a competent seller is a deal; a low price obtained by deception, pressure, or concealment of the assignment intent is a fraud / predatory-practice claim.
**Legal / lawful?** Lawful only with full disclosure, no misrepresentation about being the end buyer, no pressure, and compliance with the foreclosure-sale statute. The "weasel clause" model — sign with no ability to close, then cloud title or renegotiate down — is the fact pattern that draws lawsuits and regulator complaints.
**Mac decides per case:** Whether any seller is in foreclosure / distress — if so, HESCA compliance and attorney review before signing, and a meaningful earnest deposit held by title (not a token $20–$100 that signals no intent to close).

### 16. Other gray structures noted (not fully developed — flagged for a later pass)

- **Reverse wholesaling:** Find the buyer and their criteria first, then find the property. Reported as lawful in CA, NY, IL when the wholesaler is still a principal on the contract — but marketing a *wanted* property you do not own, or soliciting sellers for a named buyer for a fee, can look like brokerage. Mac decides per buyer-first campaign.
- **Wholetailing:** Buy (actually close), light rehab/clean, resell retail on the MLS with an agent. Lawful; it removes the assignment question entirely but requires taking title and funding.
- **Option contracts:** An option to buy, then selling/assigning the option, is the same equitable-interest logic as an assignment, with the same marketing and disclosure lines. No separate exemption was found.
- **New York private right of action:** RPL §442-e(3) lets a seller sue for **four times** the unlicensed fee, without waiting for a regulator. Misdemeanor exposure under §442-e(1). Relevant only if Mac ever wholesales in NY.
- **Assignment-fee size:** National average assignment fee is reported at ~$13,000–$15,400 (2025), with novation spreads higher. Size alone is not illegality — but a large, undisclosed spread is what triggers seller renegotiation, regulator interest, and lender scrutiny.

**Distilled rules (3)**
46. **Sell the contract, never the house.** Put your name on a signed, assignable purchase agreement first, then market only your right under that contract, in writing, with a plain disclosure that you do not own the property, may not close yourself, and may profit by assigning it. Every ad, text, and script is checked against that sentence before it goes out.
47. **Disclose before signing, in the form the state requires, and run the money through escrow.** Written disclosure to seller (and assignee where required) before the contract, standalone and signed — not buried, not verbal — and the assignment fee on the settlement statement through title/escrow, with a real deposit that proves intent to close. If a state gives the seller a cancellation clock, assume they will use it.
48. **Never use a recorded document, a second wholesaler, or a creative-finance structure to do what disclosure would have prevented.** No memorandum after a deal dies, no daisy chain of someone else's contract, no subject-to or seller-carry assignment without the seller signing that they understand the loan stays in their name / the financing terms. If the structure's purpose is concealment, pick a different structure.

**Brought back to Mac — opportunities / loopholes to decide per case (not acted on)**
1. **Novation to retail buyers** — see §3 above. Larger spreads ($10k–$30k+), financed buyers, seller consent required. Mac decides whether to test it on one deal with attorney review.
2. **Double close with transactional funding** — see §11. Privacy for large spreads without Mac holding cash; funder wires to title only. Mac decides per large-spread deal.
3. **California voluntary disclosure as a trust advantage** — see §9. No statute yet requires it, so a wholesaler who discloses in writing anyway (no title, may assign, may profit, seller proceeds unchanged) stands out to sellers and to the DRE if AB 1850 returns. Mac decides whether to make it standard on the Fresno lots.
4. **Vacant-land focus** — see §10. Land may sit outside *residential*-only wholesaler statutes in some states, but that is unproven and does not help in California, where general broker law covers all real property. Mac decides whether to get a written attorney view before treating land as a lighter-touch market.
5. **Seller-carry within the 1-/3-property exemptions** — see §13. Up to 3 seller-financed deals in 12 months without an RMLO if the loan features comply (fully amortising, no balloon, no negative amortisation, rate fixed ≥5 years). Mac decides whether any Fresno deal should be structured this way, and counts it against the 12-month tally.

**Gaps / uncertainties to note**
- No source in this search gave a California-specific answer on whether vacant-land assignments escape any rule — because California has no residential wholesaler statute to escape. The land "loophole" is therefore **unverified**.
- The "AB 1720" reference in one California source could not be verified against primary text in this search; AB 1850's stalled status *was* verified against the Legislature's own bill page.
- State-tracker counts differ (16 vs. 17 vs. 22 states) because trackers count disclosure-only states, registration states, and "regulators enforcing anyway" states differently. The underlying statutes cited are consistent.
- No paywalled, login, or bot-checked source was bypassed. No source was blocked in this pass.
