### 27. Gray and dark areas, loopholes people feared to touch (studied 2026-11-20)
Mac's premise tested here: the barrier to these areas is mostly lack of knowledge, insight, and application credibility — not the area being truly off-limits. That premise held up in most areas surveyed, with important exceptions noted below. Nothing below is legal advice; every area is state- and county-specific and needs a licensed attorney's check before any use. Mac decides per case; nothing here has been used.

**The sorting — genuinely unlawful vs. merely unfamiliar vs. reputationally uncomfortable.** Genuinely unlawful / hard legal lines (not gray): marketing a property you do not own, as if you owned it or represented the seller, for compensation — unlicensed brokerage in most states (the line nearly every 2024–2026 wholesaling statute polices); practising law without a licence while recovering someone else's surplus — drafting court petitions, preparing legal filings for a client, advising on legal rights — as a non-attorney recovery agent; charging a surplus-recovery fee where the state forbids non-attorneys charging at all, or above a statutory cap, or soliciting before a statutory waiting period; entering an occupied or apparently empty house without permission — trespass in every state, however empty it looks; squatting to "claim" a house is not a shortcut — it can be criminal trespass and, in Florida since 2024, a felony if damage over $1,000 is caused; taking an assignment of a surplus claim obtained by prohibited in-person or phone solicitation (e.g. Texas), or contacting a foreclosure-surplus owner inside a statutory no-contact window (e.g. California, 90 days after the trustee's deed is recorded, for a residence). Merely unfamiliar (lawful in structure, avoided through ignorance): tax-sale surplus / overage recovery; tax-lien certificates; redeemable deeds; code-lien negotiated settlement; HOA super-priority lien mechanics; buying a non-performing note; buying a fractional heirs' interest and using partition law; zombie-foreclosure owner tracing; land-bank purchases; lien-payoff negotiation (mechanic's, judgment, municipal); quiet-title actions after a tax deed. Reputationally uncomfortable but lawful in structure: buying one heir's small fractional interest and forcing a partition sale of the whole family property; buying at an HOA foreclosure where a tiny dues debt wipes out a large mortgage; profiting from a former owner's unclaimed surplus. All are lawful in the states where the underlying statute allows them — and all are the areas where the reputational and ethical cost is highest, and where reform laws are actively closing the gap. Flagged as such below, not recommended.

**1. Tax-sale surplus / overages — the flagship "dark" area.** Who: former owners (and their heirs, and junior lienholders) whose property sold at a tax or foreclosure auction for more than was owed; third-party recovery agents who find them. What: when a property sells at auction for more than the tax debt (taxes + interest + fees + costs), the difference — the overage, excess proceeds, or surplus — belongs to the former owner, not the government or the bank. The county or court holds it in trust until claimed. If nobody claims it within the statutory window (commonly 1–3 years, varying by state), it is forfeited to the government permanently. When: after any tax sale or foreclosure sale where the hammer price exceeds the debt. Claim windows run from the sale or from recording of the deed; California tax-sale excess must be claimed within 1 year after the tax deed is recorded, after which unclaimed balances go to the county general fund. Florida foreclosure surplus must generally be claimed within 1 year of the sale before it goes to the Department of Financial Services. Where: everywhere in the U.S., county by county. Surplus lists / excess-proceeds lists are public records held by the county treasurer, tax collector, or clerk of court; many counties post them online, others require an in-person records visit. Why it is feared / avoided: people assume the sale "took everything," the county is not required to find the former owner, notice goes to the address of a property the person no longer owns, and the deadline runs quietly. Recovery is paperwork-heavy and deadline-driven, so most money sits unclaimed — described by recovery firms as billions of dollars a year nationally. The legal anchor: *Tyler v. Hennepin County*, 598 U.S. 631 (2023). Geraldine Tyler, a 94-year-old woman, owed about $15,000 in taxes, interest, and penalties on a Minneapolis condominium; Hennepin County seized it, sold it for $40,000, and kept the roughly $25,000 surplus. The Supreme Court held unanimously that keeping the surplus was a taking without just compensation under the Fifth Amendment. Since then Minnesota, Maine, South Dakota, New Jersey, and (on July 10, 2026) Illinois rewrote their laws so former owners can recover surplus, and Colorado ended automatic deeds to certificate holders. The gray edges — where agents get into trouble: **fee caps** — most regulating states cap recovery fees at roughly 10–25% of the amount recovered. Florida caps foreclosure-surplus assignment compensation at 12% of the surplus, and 30% once funds are with the Department of Financial Services. Texas allows only an attorney to charge for recovering excess proceeds, capped at 25% or $1,000, whichever is less — non-attorneys may charge nothing. California tax-sale excess has no statutory percentage cap found in the current text, but any assignment must disclose the amount of the excess and the value of the right being assigned; state-held unclaimed property investigator fees are capped at 10%. **Solicitation bans and waiting periods:** Texas bars assignments resulting from in-person or phone solicitation, and no assignment until at least 36 days after the surplus is deposited with the court. California (Civil Code 2924.21, added by AB 295, effective July 18, 2024) bars contacting or soliciting an owner to claim surplus from a foreclosure sale of the owner's residence before 90 days after the trustee's deed is recorded. **Unauthorised practice of law:** locating the owner and gathering factual documents is generally the safe boundary for a non-attorney; drafting court petitions or advising on legal rights can cross into unlicensed practice, depending on the state. **Priority:** senior lienholders claim before the former owner; junior lienholders (judgments, mechanic's liens) claim in recording-date priority; heirs inherit the claim through the estate.

**2. Heirs property and partition sales — the most reputationally loaded area.** Who: families who inherited land without a will, holding it as tenants in common — each heir owning a fractional undivided interest in the whole, not a specific piece. Disproportionately low-income and Black families in the South, in some cases land held since Emancipation. Outside investors who buy one heir's share. What: any co-owner can ask a court to divide the property (partition in kind) or sell it and split the proceeds (partition by sale). Historically courts ordered forced sales by public auction even when division was feasible, and auction prices run far below market value. An investor can buy a single heir's interest — sometimes for a few hundred dollars — then use that foothold to force a sale of the entire property and buy it at auction. When: at any time a co-owner petitions; risk is highest within two years of the owner's death in some states, when heir-to-buyer deeds can be vulnerable to the estate's personal representative and creditors unless statutory steps are satisfied. Where: nationwide, but concentrated in the South and in states that have not adopted reform. A documented case pattern: in Buncombe County, North Carolina, an investor's companies bought two elderly heirs' shares for $250 each, forced a sale, bought the whole property at auction for $3,750, and resold it three months later for $55,000; the eight heirs received $445 each. The investor's attorney's position, reported in the same investigation, was that partition actions are "completely legal." Why it is feared / avoided: it looks and feels like taking a family's land for pennies. The legal profession itself treats it as an abuse risk: the American Bar Association's real-property section helped drive reform, and the Uniform Law Commission promulgated the Uniform Partition of Heirs Property Act (UPHPA) in 2010. The reform that changes the map: UPHPA — enacted in 24 states plus the District of Columbia and the U.S. Virgin Islands by the end of 2025 (some sources count 26 states including California, New York, Florida, Illinois, and Virginia as of November 2025; counts differ by source and date, so verify per state). Where adopted, it gives co-owners a right of first refusal / buyout right, requires a court-appointed appraisal instead of a lowball auction, and lets a judge partition in kind rather than order a sale. North Carolina — the site of the Buncombe County cases — has not adopted it. The federal HEIRS Act (H.R. 1640), advanced 51–0 by the House Financial Services Committee on September 1, 2026, would authorise $10 million per year (FY2026–2030) in HUD grants to states adopting UPHPA and to nonprofits/legal-aid clinics helping heirs clear title; no House floor vote date had been scheduled as of mid-September 2026. **California note for Mac:** California has adopted UPHPA, so the forced-sale-at-auction play is substantially blunted there relative to non-UPHPA states — buyout rights and appraisal apply. Risks for any buyer of a fractional interest: you take only what the seller actually owns, subject to hidden liens, estate creditor claims, and a later court-ordered partition or sale; sales by minors or incompetents need proper authority; many heir sales are by quitclaim or limited warranty, giving no protection against undiscovered title defects; judicial sales commonly carry a 10-day upset-bid period.

**3. Code-enforcement lien negotiation — the "municipal liens survive everything" area.** Who: owners (and buyers) of properties carrying city/county code-violation fines that have become liens — tall grass, open structures, trash, unsafe conditions, unpermitted work. What: code fines accrue daily (Florida's Chapter 162: up to $250/day for a first violation, $500/day for a repeat, up to $5,000 per violation if irreparable) and, once recorded, become liens that can last up to 20 years. But boards and departments can settle them for less than the full amount once the violation is fixed. Miami-Dade's Implementing Order 2-5 expressly lets departments settle civil penalties and liens "for amounts less than the maximum continuing penalty, costs and accrued interest," extend compliance deadlines, and set up payment plans on written proof of economic need. Hillsborough County, Florida, runs a formal lien-settlement application: bring the property into compliance, submit the notarised application to the lien-settlement mailbox, expect a response in 7–10 business days, then 30 days to pay the offered amount, after which the lien is released. Practitioners report Florida code liens commonly negotiated to 10–20% of face (some report 20–30%) once the underlying violation is corrected and the owner cooperates. When: after the violation is corrected — compliance first is the near-universal precondition; hardship consideration (homestead, severe financial hardship, active-duty/veteran, senior, medical) can improve the offer. Where: city and county code-enforcement departments everywhere; the exact procedure, form, and mailbox are local. Texas practitioners report average lien-settlement discounts of 30–70% through negotiation. Why it is feared / avoided: the headline lien amount looks enormous and permanent, and most investors assume it must be paid in full at closing. Two traps drive the fear: (a) municipal/code liens commonly **survive a tax deed** — unlike most private liens, which a properly noticed tax deed extinguishes — so a "cheap" tax-deed win can carry a six-figure code lien; (b) the liens often do not show up in a standard county-records search and need a separate written municipal lien / code-enforcement search with the city. Why it matters to wholesaling: a property that looks dead on the numbers because of a $60,000 code lien can become a deal if the lien settles for $6,000–$12,000 after the violation is fixed — but the settlement is negotiated by or for the owner, and the fix must actually happen; promising a settlement you have not obtained is misrepresentation.

**4. Tax liens and tax deeds — buying the debt vs. buying the property.** Who: counties selling delinquent-tax claims; private investors bidding at auction. What: three systems, by state: (a) **tax lien** — you buy the county's lien and earn statutory interest when the owner redeems, or the right to foreclose if they do not (Florida, Illinois, Arizona, New Jersey, and 16 other jurisdictions — 20 total); (b) **redeemable deed** — you get a deed subject to the owner's right to buy it back for a fixed penalty (Texas, Georgia, and 9 others — 11 total); (c) **tax deed** — the county forecloses first, then auctions the property itself (California, Washington, Pennsylvania, New York, Michigan, Minnesota, and 14 others — 20 jurisdictions sell no lien to investors). When: redemption periods vary widely — as short as 3 months plus a 90-day notice for some Iowa sales, 6 months in D.C. and Maryland, 1 year in Missouri, 2 years in Florida, up to 3 years in Illinois; California's power to sell arises after 5 years of default, and redemption ends at close of business on the last business day before the sale. Where: county auctions, increasingly online. Headline rates are statutory ceilings, not what bidders get: Florida 18%/year (5% minimum on redemption, certificates bid down in quarter-point steps), Arizona 16%, New Jersey 18%, Ohio 18%, Illinois up to 36% (bid down; Cook County winning bids routinely hit 0%), Iowa 2%/month, Nebraska 14%, Kentucky 12%. Texas redeemable deeds pay a 25% penalty in year 1 and 50% in year 2 on homesteads; Georgia 20% then 10%/year. Why it is feared / avoided: the pitch ("18% guaranteed" / "houses for back taxes") describes the ceiling, not the outcome. Competitive bidding drives winning rates to 0.25%–2% in Florida and Arizona; redemption rates run 95–98%, so most liens never become property; unredeemed parcels are often unredeemed for a reason (sliver lots, demolition orders, environmental contamination). Post-*Tyler*, the windfall is capped: a lienholder can no longer expect to keep surplus value above the debt in the reformed states. Federal tax liens survive most state tax-lien foreclosures; environmental liability (CERCLA) can follow a foreclosed commercial parcel; missing a post-purchase notice deadline (60 days in Illinois, for example) can void the right to foreclose entirely. **California note for Mac:** California is a tax-deed state — there is no lien certificate to buy. The play in Fresno is the deed auction after 5 years of default, with a 1-year excess-proceeds claim window for the former owner afterwards.

**5. Distressed-debt-adjacent plays — buying the note, not the house.** Who: banks, funds, and special servicers selling non-performing loans; private/hard-money lenders and seller-financers who end up holding a defaulted note; note investors who buy the right to collect. What: a mortgage loan is two documents — the promissory note (the promise to pay, usually a negotiable instrument under UCC Article 3) and the mortgage/deed of trust (the security that lets the holder foreclose). Buying the note transfers the lender's rights: you earn the interest and the discount if the borrower pays, and you hold the lender's remedies, including foreclosure, if they do not. A buyer who takes a negotiable note for value, in good faith, and without notice of defences can become a holder in due course (UCC 3-302), cutting off many borrower defences — but not every note qualifies as negotiable. When: performing notes (paying on time) are priced on remaining payments discounted to a target yield; non-performing notes (typically 90+ days delinquent, in foreclosure, or in bankruptcy) are priced on property value, lien position, timeline, and cost to resolve. U.S. 1–4 unit mortgage delinquency was 4.37% in Q2 2026 (MBA); Freddie Mac sold 1,968 non-performing loans in its October 2026 offering. Where: loan-sale agreements from banks/funds (sold as-is, limited representations, short survival periods, repurchase/cure as the main remedy); also seller-carried notes and private-lender notes in local deals. Why it is feared / avoided: you buy every defect in how the loan was documented, transferred, and enforced. Standing is often the first fight (the holder must hold the note or have it assigned when the action starts); a missing original note needs a lost-note affidavit; New York's Foreclosure Abuse Prevention Act (2022), held retroactive in November 2025, can time-bar a loan accelerated years ago; residential loans add pre-foreclosure notice, settlement-conference, and servicer-registration rules. The discount to the unpaid balance is not a discount to the property's market value — collateral value, lien priority, title, borrower defences, guaranties, bankruptcy risk, and foreclosure cost/time decide the real price. Why it is adjacent to wholesaling: a wholesaler already values houses, reads title, and talks to owners in distress; a seller-carried note shows up in the wholesaler's own deals when a seller carries financing or a buyer wants to exit a seller-financed property.

**6. HOA super-priority liens — the tiny debt that can erase a mortgage.** Who: homeowners associations foreclosing for unpaid assessments; speculators buying at HOA foreclosure sales; first-mortgage lenders whose deeds of trust are at risk. What: in about two dozen states plus D.C., part of an HOA's lien ranks ahead of a previously recorded first mortgage — a "super-priority" slice. Nevada gives nine months of assessments super-priority; Colorado six months; Florida caps a foreclosing lender's exposure at 12 months or 1% of the mortgage. In Nevada, a properly conducted foreclosure of the super-priority slice can extinguish the first deed of trust entirely. *SFR Investments Pool 1, LLC v. U.S. Bank*, 334 P.3d 408 (Nev. 2014) (en banc): an investor bought a home at a nonjudicial HOA trustee's sale and sued to quiet title against the bank — the Nevada Supreme Court held the super-priority piece (last nine months of unpaid dues plus maintenance/nuisance-abatement charges) is prior to the first lien, so the HOA sale could wipe the mortgage out. The court noted the windfall can be extreme — an HOA foreclosing on a $500 lien and taking title to a $5 million home free and clear. The Nevada Legislature amended the law in 2015 to shift the balance back toward lenders and homeowners, and federal law may protect Fannie/Freddie-backed loans from extinguishment. When: when assessments go delinquent and the HOA forecloses; the super-priority slice is measured over the months immediately preceding the enforcement action. Where: Nevada is the true-super-priority-with-extinguishment case; roughly 23 other jurisdictions have some super-lien form, but most are limited-priority or safe-harbour regimes where the mortgage survives. Why it is feared / avoided: it inverts the normal priority ladder everyone assumes — first mortgage first — and the downside is total: a buyer at an HOA sale in a non-super-priority state takes title subject to the first mortgage, while in Nevada the mortgage can vanish, and in California an HOA foreclosure does not extinguish a first deed of trust recorded before the HOA lien. Title defects after HOA sales are common: senior mortgages that survive, defective foreclosure procedure, bankruptcy-stay violations, federal tax liens, property-tax liens, judgment and mechanic's liens, unreported easements, pending special assessments, and redemption rights. **California note for Mac:** California is not a Nevada-style extinguishment state — an HOA foreclosure there does not wipe out a first deed of trust. The Nevada play does not transfer to Fresno.

**7. Zombie foreclosures and abandoned houses — title still lives there.** Who: owners who left after a foreclosure started, believing they had lost the house; lenders who never finished the foreclosure; the county, which still taxes the owner of record. What: a zombie foreclosure is a home where the owner walked away after foreclosure began but the lender never completed the sale — no auction, no deed transfer, no REO listing. Title stays in the name of an owner who moved away, while the property sits empty, deteriorating, and accruing tax bills and code violations that legally still belong to that person. ATTOM's Q3 2026 report: national residential vacancy 1.3% of roughly 104.6 million homes; of 259,666 homes in pre-foreclosure that quarter, only 8,482 (3.3%) were vacant zombies — a small, regenerating pool, concentrated in judicial-foreclosure states where cases stall, with zombie rates as high as 13% in Baltimore and Youngstown, Ohio. The Q4 2026 report put it at 8,040 homes, about 3% of homes in foreclosure. When: identified by aged foreclosure initiations (12+ months old) with no subsequent trustee's deed, sheriff's sale, REO transfer, or dismissal-plus-reinstatement, layered with vacancy flags (USPS vacancy, utility shutoff, code violations). Where: county recorder filings (notice of default / lis pendens), vacant-property registries (many cities require lenders/owners to register vacant buildings and pay a fee), and code-enforcement records. Why it is feared / avoided: the owner is gone and often does not know they still own the house or still owe taxes and violations; finding them takes skip tracing; the property is usually distressed and lien-loaded; and entering the house is trespass. Adverse possession is not a shortcut: it requires open, notorious, hostile, actual, exclusive, continuous possession for the statutory period (5 years in California, plus payment of taxes for the entire period), and the owner can have the occupier removed and arrested for criminal trespass during that period. The lawful paths to an abandoned house are the ordinary ones: direct purchase from the owner/heirs, probate or estate sale, short sale/pre-foreclosure with lender approval, REO purchase, or tax sale/land bank. Land banks (the public-sector version): about 250 land-bank programs are active in the roughly 20 states plus Puerto Rico with enabling laws. Land banks acquire vacant/tax-foreclosed property, clear delinquent taxes, liens, and clouded title, and resell — often cheaply — to buyers who commit to rehab and occupancy. Detroit Land Bank: auction bids start at $1,000, $1,000 deposit on winning, close in about 30 days, clear title on auction homes, but the buyer signs a development/renovation agreement and a reconveyance deed letting the land bank reclaim the property if rehab is not completed, usually within 6 months; side lots go for $100–$250 but only to adjacent homeowners. Lackawanna County requires a $5,000 good-faith deposit held in escrow until rehab passes final inspection, forfeited with the purchase price and all invested costs if the buyer defaults.

**8. Obscure public-record angles and lien negotiation (the wholesaler's daily version of the same knowledge).** Probate / pre-probate: probate filings are public at the county level. Executors and heirs — often out of state, often not wanting to manage the property — are among the most motivated sellers. Approach by letter to the executor or family, never with mail that looks like it came from the county or the lender. The title risk: within two years of death, heir deeds can be vulnerable to the estate and creditors unless the personal representative joins after notice to creditors. Vacant-property registries and code-violation lists: a house with repeated citations and an out-of-area owner is one of the strongest abandonment signals available — and the list is a public record, free to request. Lien payoff negotiation (mechanic's, judgment, municipal): mechanic's liens must be filed within a statutory window (often 90–180 days after work) and take priority from when work began, not when filed — so verify validity first; inflated or invalid ones can be negotiated down or removed by legal action. Judgment creditors often accept a discounted payoff (commonly reported at 40–70% of balance) because a contested lien on a distressed property may yield nothing in foreclosure. Municipal liens: contact the city department for a payoff; some cities offer reduction programs or payment plans. IRS liens: the IRS may accept a short payoff through a certificate of discharge where the property value is less than the lien. A deal that looks impossible — $30,000 of liens against $20,000 of seller equity — can become viable if the liens settle for $15,000. The negotiation happens with the lienholder, at or before closing, through the title company; it is not a wholesaler discounting someone else's debt unilaterally. Quiet title after a tax deed: a tax deed rarely delivers insurable title by itself; a quiet-title action in court is the usual cleanup step, and title companies may insure over old, questionable judgments in rare cases. The wholesaling-regulation wave itself (the gray area that is closing): wholesaling is legal in all 50 states — no state bans assigning a purchase contract — but roughly 20 states now regulate how it is done, with six new laws across five states in 2025 alone. The pattern: **license required or effectively required** — North Carolina, Oklahoma, Pennsylvania, Kentucky, Nebraska (public marketing), Illinois past one deal a year, South Carolina (wholesaling defined as brokerage; marketing the property requires a licence, while assigning the contractual right itself is expressly not "wholesaling" under HB 4754); **registration** — Oregon (from July 1, 2025), Connecticut (from July 1, 2026), Pennsylvania (from January 9, 2025); **disclosure and cancellation windows** — Alabama, Maryland, North Dakota, Ohio, Tennessee, Texas, Wisconsin and others; Oklahoma SB 1075 (effective November 1, 2025) now expressly includes double closings in its definition of "wholesaler" and adds a 48-hour cancellation window, so the double-close workaround no longer avoids the licence requirement there. The universal rule that survives everywhere: **market your contract (your equitable interest), not the property.** Texas and Florida remain the friendliest states — assign with disclosure, no licence. This is not a loophole to exploit; it is the compliance map that decides whether any of the other plays can be marketed lawfully.

**What is NOT in this survey (checked, found to be a dead end or out of scope).** Adverse possession as an acquisition strategy: real doctrine, very high bar, multi-year timeline, court scepticism, and criminal-trespass exposure during the period. Not a loophole; reported so it is not mistaken for one. "Buy a lien and own the house": a tax-lien certificate is a claim for interest, not ownership, in lien states; only foreclosure after the redemption period (and only if the owner does not redeem, which happens 95–98% of the time) can lead to a deed. Unclaimed-property "finder" fees in general: state unclaimed-property programs are free to search and claim through the official program; private finders are legal in most cases but many states require registration, and fees are capped (e.g. 10% for California state-held unclaimed property investigators). The surplus-funds version above is the real-estate-specific form.

### Rules (82–84)

- **Rule 82 — The fear is usually about the map, not the territory — but read the map for that state and that county before you move.** Almost every area above is lawful in structure somewhere and unlawful or capped somewhere else: the same surplus-recovery fee that is a normal 12% contingency in Florida is prohibited for a non-attorney in Texas; the same HOA foreclosure that erases a mortgage in Nevada leaves it intact in California; the same partition sale that is a routine court remedy in a non-UPHPA state is blunted by buyout and appraisal rights in California. The state statute, the county procedure, and the waiting period are the deal — not the national pitch.
- **Rule 83 — Market the contract, never the property — and never the problem you have not solved.** The line that decides wholesaling legality everywhere is marketing your equitable interest versus marketing someone else's house. The same discipline applies inside these gray areas: never promise a code-lien settlement, a surplus recovery, or a title cure you have not obtained, and never present a lien, a surplus, or a fractional interest as though it were clean title. Title, priority, and the claim window are verified through the title company and the county record before anything is offered to a buyer.
- **Rule 84 — Take the knowledge, leave the extraction — bring the uncomfortable ones back to Mac untouched.** The areas where the money is easiest (a $250 heir's share forcing a $55,000 sale; a $500 HOA debt erasing a mortgage; an unclaimed surplus signed away for an over-cap fee) are the areas where the reputational cost, the reform risk, and the harm to a vulnerable owner are highest. Study them, price them honestly, and put the full who/what/when/where/why in front of Mac per case. Never use them alone.

**Brought back to Mac — gray areas, loopholes & new value ideas (not acted on)**
- **(a) Tax-sale surplus recovery as a finder service.** Who: former owners and heirs who want money they do not know exists, before a 1–3 year deadline forfeits it; counties do not find them. What: monitor county surplus/excess-proceeds lists (public records), identify the former owner or heirs, contact them only after any statutory waiting period and only by permitted means, and help them claim — or refer the legal filing to a licensed attorney — for a contingency fee within the state's cap. When/where: after any tax or foreclosure sale where the hammer price exceeds the debt; county by county. Why: the largest pool of money owners do not know exists. Opportunity sign — need: owners want money they don't know exists before the deadline; how: monitor public surplus lists, find owners/heirs, help claim or refer to attorney for a capped contingency fee; acquire: knowledge of each county's list location and claim form, the state's fee cap, solicitation and waiting-period rules, the UPL boundary; a written fee agreement and limited power of attorney from the claimant; skip-tracing skill. No money from Mac, no spend, no holding money. The Texas non-attorney fee ban and the California 90-day no-contact rule are the tripwires. Mac decides per case.
- **(b) Heirs-property partition play.** Who: outside investors buying one heir's fractional share; families holding inherited land without a will. What: buy a single heir's interest (documented at $250 a share), force a partition sale, buy the whole property at auction ($3,750) and resell ($55,000) — lawful in non-UPHPA states, and the most reputationally damaging play in the survey. UPHPA (24–26 states) and the federal HEIRS Act are closing it. The title-clearing version of the same knowledge (helping families keep the land) is the constructive mirror. Mac decides per case; reported in full, not used.
- **(c) Code-lien negotiated settlement.** Who: sellers with code-lien-encumbered property; buyers who want the lien cost known and reduced before committing. What: municipal liens survive tax deeds, hide from standard title searches, and commonly settle for 10–20% (Florida) or 30–70% off (Texas) once the violation is fixed. When/where: after the violation is corrected; city/county code-enforcement departments. Why: the most directly usable for Mac's Fresno work — a separate municipal lien search on every deal is the operational takeaway. Opportunity sign — need: sellers want a sale that can close; buyers want the lien cost known and reduced; how: pull the separate municipal lien/code search, identify open violations, get the violation cured, apply through the published settlement process; acquire: knowledge of the local settlement application, mailbox, and compliance-first rule; the discipline to run the municipal lien search on every deal. No money from Mac, no spend, no holding money. Mac decides per case.
- **(d) HOA super-priority foreclosure.** Who: HOAs foreclosing for unpaid assessments; speculators buying at HOA sales; first-mortgage lenders. What: in Nevada, a properly conducted foreclosure of the super-priority slice can extinguish the first deed of trust entirely (*SFR Investments Pool 1 v. U.S. Bank*); does not transfer to California — an HOA foreclosure there does not wipe out a first deed of trust. Reported so it is not mistaken for a Fresno play. Mac decides per case.
- **(e) Note buying and zombie-owner tracing.** Who: banks/funds selling non-performing loans; zombie owners who left after a foreclosure started and don't know they still own the house. What: note buying (price on collateral value and resolution cost, not unpaid balance; verify endorsement/assignment chains) and zombie tracing (cross aged lis pendens against completion records and vacancy indicators; skip-trace the owner; approach with the news they still hold title and offer a lawful purchase). Opportunity signs — note resolution as a service: need: lenders want a defaulted loan converted to a known amount on a known date; how: price on collateral/resolution cost, verify the complete chain, resolve by workout first; acquire: loan-file diligence skill, state foreclosure-timeline knowledge, a licensed servicer where required. No money from Mac, no spend, no holding money — note buying requires capital, so it is knowledge for Mac's buyers, not a no-capital play. Zombie-owner tracing: need: zombie owners want to learn they still own the house and may still gain from a sale; neighbours/cities want the blight resolved; how: cross aged filings against completion records + vacancy signals, skip-trace, approach with a lawful purchase offer; acquire: recorder-filing access, vacancy-signal sources, skip-tracing skill. No money from Mac, no spend, no holding money. Mac decides per case.

### 28. Credit — history, knowledge, utility, usage; the insider gatekeeping (studied 2026-11-20)
The last queue topic, and the one that explains the others: credit is the gatekeeper in real estate — who can borrow, who can close, who must sell below market because they cannot carry. The gate is not a secret. It is a stack of public documents almost nobody reads. The wholesaler who reads them can price a deal and pick a structure in minutes; the seller who does not discovers the gate only when the bank says no — and by then the discount is the price of the lesson.

**History — how the gate was built.** 1841: Lewis Tappan founded the Mercantile Agency in New York after the Panic of 1837 left merchants unable to tell whether distant trading partners would pay; it tracked businesses, using correspondents across the country. Bradstreet's followed in 1849 — the ancestor of Dun & Bradstreet. 1899: brothers Cator and Guy Woolford founded the Retail Credit Company in Atlanta to collect payment habits of individual shoppers for retailers and finance companies; it later became Equifax. In the early 1900s local merchants shared handwritten lists of reliable and unreliable customers; installment credit in the 1920s and revolving credit in the 1950s made that data a business. 1956: engineer Bill Fair and mathematician Earl Isaac formed Fair, Isaac and Company to replace subjective, character-based lending judgments with a standardized score; they pitched 50 large lenders in 1958 and only one adopted it. Montgomery Ward followed in 1963; banks and card issuers only began using it widely in the early 1970s. 1970: the Fair Credit Reporting Act (FCRA) — the first major federal law regulating credit bureaus — made bureau files transparent to consumers, gave consumers the right to see and dispute their reports, and limited who could pull a report ("permissible purpose"); most negative items fall off after 7 years, Chapter 7 bankruptcy after 10. 1974: the Equal Credit Opportunity Act (ECOA), signed October 10, 1974, banned discrimination on sex and marital status, amended in 1976 to add race, color, religion, national origin, age, and receipt of public assistance; its implementing rule is Regulation B, and enforcement moved from the Federal Reserve to the CFPB when the CFPB opened in 2011. 1989: Fair Isaac released the first widely used FICO bureau risk score, on the 300–850 scale still used today. The three bureaus later built a competing model, VantageScore (2006), and FICO 10T followed. 2003: the FACT Act amended the FCRA to guarantee free annual credit reports (the basis of AnnualCreditReport.com). 2010: Dodd-Frank created the CFPB, now the primary supervisor and enforcer of FCRA and ECOA. 2023–2026: free weekly credit reports, made available during the pandemic, became permanent in 2023; on April 22, 2026, FHFA and HUD announced VantageScore 4.0 and FICO 10T as approved mortgage scoring models for Fannie Mae, Freddie Mac, and FHA — the first new mortgage score models in decades. Lenders may now use VantageScore 4.0 broadly (since September 9, 2026); FICO 10T rollout follows. The arc in one line: credit went from a merchant's gossip ledger (1841) to a private data oligopoly (three bureaus) to a single number (1989) that now decides who can borrow, buy, rent, and close — and the rules governing it were written in public law that almost nobody reads.

**Knowledge — how the score and underwriting actually work.** The five FICO factors (myFICO's published weights): (1) payment history, 35% — whether bills were paid on time; how many lates, how late (30/60/90/120/150 days), how recent, and whether accounts went to collections, charge-off, or bankruptcy; the single biggest factor. (2) Amounts owed, 30% — total debt and, most importantly, credit utilization, the percentage of revolving limits in use: a $2,000 balance against $20,000 in limits is 10% utilization; the same balance against a $2,500 limit is 80% and scores very differently. Closing a card removes its limit from the denominator and can raise utilization overnight. (3) Length of credit history, 15% — age of oldest and newest accounts and average account age; closing the oldest card shortens the history. (4) New credit, 10% — recent applications and hard inquiries; rate-shopping inquiries within a window (usually 14–45 days) are generally counted as one. (5) Credit mix, 10% — having different types (cards, installment, mortgage) helps, but matters most on thin files. What the score does NOT use: income, age, address, marital status, race, sex, or religion. A mortgage decision is not the score alone — the underwriter runs three checks at once: credit (the qualifying score is the *middle* of the three bureau scores; with two borrowers, the lower of the two middle scores is used), capacity (debt-to-income ratio: total monthly debt payments ÷ gross monthly income; Fannie Mae's Selling Guide (B3-6-02) caps manual underwriting at 36%, up to 45% with the Eligibility Matrix score and reserves, and 50% through Desktop Underwriter; FHA manual limits are 31/43, rising to 40/50 with two compensating factors (HUD Handbook 4000.1); VA uses a 41% guideline plus a residual-income test; underwriters count minimum payments as reported — and where a card shows no minimum payment, Fannie Mae counts 5% of the balance as the monthly payment), and collateral (the appraisal and loan-to-value). Minimum scores, 2026 (from the guides themselves): Fannie Mae — no fixed minimum for Desktop Underwriter loans (since November 2025 it weighs the whole file), 620 for manual underwriting; FHA — 580 for 3.5% down, 500–579 needs 10% down; VA — no minimum in its regulation, each lender sets its own; USDA — no minimum, its automated system decides, lenders commonly require 640. Lender overlays (a lender's own stricter rules) sit on top of all of this and are the most common reason a "qualified" borrower is still denied — and overlays are not published in the guides. The new models: VantageScore 4.0 and FICO 10T use trended data over 24 months (are balances rising or falling?) and VantageScore 4.0 can count on-time rent payments — a buyer whose balances are trending down now scores better than one whose identical snapshot balance is trending up. The consumer rights most people never use: free weekly reports from all three bureaus at AnnualCreditReport.com — the only federally authorized site — permanent since 2023 (reports do not include the score); free dispute — dispute any error with the bureau *and* the furnisher; the bureau generally has 30 days to investigate (45 if you add information mid-investigation) and must correct or delete what it cannot verify; accurate negative information cannot be removed by anyone, no matter what a credit-repair company promises; adverse-action right — if denied credit because of a report, you can get a free copy of that report if you ask within 60 days; opt out of prescreened offers at OptOutPrescreen.com.

**Utility — what credit unlocks in real estate.** For buyers: score + DTI decide whether a bank will lend, at what rate, and with what down payment. A 580 FHA borrower needs 10% down where a 620 borrower needs 3.5%. A borrower at 50% DTI through Desktop Underwriter closes; the same borrower manually underwritten at 36% does not. The difference between "can buy" and "cannot buy" is often a single threshold in a guide the buyer has never seen. For sellers: credit decides whether a seller can *hold* or must *sell*. A seller who can refinance can pull cash out, lower the payment, and wait for a better price; a seller who cannot refinance — because the score dropped, DTI is too high, or the loan is underwater — cannot. A seller who can carry financing (seller finance) can sell on terms and collect income; a seller who needs the loan paid off at closing cannot. A seller whose mortgage is current and who has a low rate can do a subject-to deal, keeping the loan in place; a seller already in default may still do one, but the arrears must be cured first. A seller with no credit access at all cannot buy their next home either — which is why some sellers accept a lease-option: they need time to repair credit before they can qualify for their next mortgage. For the deal structures themselves: seller financing, subject-to, and lease-options exist precisely *because* the credit gate is closed to one side or the other. The end buyer on a terms deal is very often someone who cannot get a bank to say yes today. The wholesaler's job is to match a seller who cannot hold with a buyer who can close — and the buyer's proof is either cash (proof of funds) or a lender's pre-approval. How buyers prove they can close: proof of funds (POF) — a bank statement or bank letter showing liquid cash in the buyer's name, usually dated within 30–90 days; for cash offers, this is what sellers want. Pre-approval — a lender's written statement of a likely loan amount after a credit pull and often automated underwriting findings, typically valid 60–90 days; stronger than a pre-qualification (self-reported income and debts, no verification). Transactional/hard-money letters — a funding commitment from a short-term lender; real in the industry but weaker in a seller's eyes — a BiggerPockets thread notes some such letters state on their face that they do not indicate loan approval, and listing agents know it.

**Usage — how credit traps sellers and creates below-market deals.** (1) Can't refinance: a seller whose score fell below the program minimum, or whose DTI now exceeds the cap, cannot refinance out of a high rate or pull equity; the house becomes a carrying cost they cannot reduce, while the price they need keeps rising. (2) Credit-card debt and utilization: a seller who lived on cards during a rough patch can have utilization at 80–90%, which suppresses the score by itself — even if every payment was on time. Underwriting counts 5% of card balances as a monthly payment where no minimum is reported, so a $6,000 balance adds $300/month to DTI — the difference between a refinance approval and a denial. (3) Medical debt: the CFPB's January 2025 rule that would have banned medical debt from credit reports was vacated in full on July 11, 2025 by the U.S. District Court for the Eastern District of Texas (*Cornerstone Credit Union League v. CFPB*, No. 4:25-cv-16-SDJ). There is no federal ban. What remains: the three bureaus' voluntary reforms (which removed many small/old medical collections) and state bans in about 15 states, including California. The CFPB had estimated the ban would have lifted affected scores by about 20 points on average — the difference between an FHA 580 and a 560, between 3.5% down and 10% down, or between approval and denial. (4) Divorce credit damage: the divorce decree itself never appears on a credit report and filing for divorce does not change a score — but the decree does not bind creditors. A court can order one spouse to pay a joint card or mortgage, and if that spouse misses payments, the lender reports the late payment on *both* credit reports. Joint accounts stay joint until paid off, refinanced, or closed — and closing usually requires both signatures and a zero balance. One spouse can deliberately run up a joint card or stop paying the mortgage, and the other's score takes the hit for up to seven years. This is one of the most common sources of motivated sellers who must sell below market: they cannot refinance (the ex's missed payment is on their report), cannot carry (DTI is inflated by joint debt), and cannot wait (the house is the last joint asset to split). (5) The 7-year shadow: most negative items stay 7 years; Chapter 7 bankruptcy 10. A seller who had a foreclosure or short sale years ago may still be inside the waiting period for a new mortgage even if their score has recovered — Fannie Mae generally requires a waiting period after foreclosure — which pushes them toward lease-options and seller financing as buyers, not just as sellers. How a wholesaler reads a seller's credit situation — without crossing privacy lines: **never pull someone's credit without consent.** Under the FCRA, a report may be furnished only for a "permissible purpose" (15 U.S.C. §1681b); a wholesaler with no credit transaction, no application from the seller, and no existing relationship has no permissible purpose. Obtaining a report under false pretenses or without permissible purpose exposes the puller to liability of the greater of $1,000 or actual damages per violation, plus punitive damages and attorney's fees (15 U.S.C. §1681n), and potential criminal exposure. Written authorization is the safe practice even where an application might arguably create permissible purpose. What you *can* do, lawfully: ask the seller to tell you, in their own words, what they know about their situation — are they current or behind on the mortgage? Is there a second loan? Are they in a divorce? Have they tried to refinance and been told no? Ask whether they would be willing to share a copy of *their own* report or a redacted mortgage statement. The seller volunteering their own information is entirely different from you pulling it. Read the signals, not the file: arrears amount, whether the loan is FHA/VA/conventional (FHA and VA loans are assumable under conditions — a separate gate), whether the seller mentions a divorce, medical bills, or a failed refinance attempt, and whether the seller's timeline is driven by a foreclosure date. Those facts, volunteered by the seller, tell you whether the seller can hold or must sell — and which structure (subject-to, seller finance, lease-option, cash assignment) fits. Verify the buyer, not the seller: for the end buyer, ask for proof of funds or a pre-approval letter, check the lender's NMLS ID, and call the lender independently. A buyer who will not show proof of funds is not a buyer yet.

**The gatekeeping — what is public but unread.** The gate is a stack of public documents almost no borrower or seller ever reads: Fannie Mae Selling Guide B3-5, B3-6 (minimum scores, DTI caps, what debts count — singlefamily.fanniemae.com); Freddie Mac Seller/Servicer Guide §5401 (manual DTI guideline, credit requirements — guide.freddiemac.com); HUD Handbook 4000.1 (FHA ratios, compensating factors, score floors — hud.gov); FCRA (15 U.S.C. §1681 et seq. — who can see your report, dispute rights, 7/10-year clocks — law.cornell.edu / consumerfinance.gov); ECOA / Regulation B (what a lender may not use against you — consumerfinance.gov); CFPB credit-reporting guidance (how to dispute, sample letters, complaint portal — consumerfinance.gov); FHFA credit-score announcements (which scoring models count for mortgages now — VantageScore 4.0, FICO 10T, April 2026 — fhfa.gov / hud.gov); AnnualCreditReport.com (the free weekly reports themselves). The insider advantage is not access — it is *reading*.

### Rules (85–87)

- **Rule 85 — Credit is the gate, not the price.** A seller's ability to hold, refinance, or carry financing is decided by score and debt-to-income thresholds written in public guides (Fannie Mae Selling Guide B3-5/B3-6, HUD Handbook 4000.1), not by the house's value. Read the thresholds before you price the deal: the seller who cannot refinance cannot wait, and the buyer who cannot pass DTI cannot close with a bank, so terms — not cash — are the only bridge.
- **Rule 86 — Never pull a credit report you have no right to see.** Under the FCRA, a report may be furnished only for a permissible purpose, and a wholesaler with no credit transaction with the seller has none. Pulling one under false pretenses risks $1,000-plus per violation, punitive damages, and attorney's fees. Learn the seller's credit situation from what the seller volunteers — arrears, failed refinance, divorce, medical debt — and verify the *buyer* with proof of funds or a pre-approval instead.
- **Rule 87 — The 2025 medical-debt ban is dead; the 20 points it would have given are gone.** The CFPB's rule banning medical debt from credit reports was vacated in full on July 11, 2025 (*Cornerstone Credit Union League v. CFPB*). In most states medical collections still report and still suppress scores. Check the seller's state law (California is among the states that ban it) and check the bureaus' voluntary removals — and never assume a medical bill is invisible to an underwriter.

**Brought back to Mac — gray areas, loopholes & new value ideas (not acted on)**
- **(a) The VantageScore 4.0 / FICO 10T transition.** Who: renters and borrowers with improving credit trends; also sellers stuck just below a threshold. What: since April 22, 2026, Fannie Mae, Freddie Mac, and FHA accept VantageScore 4.0 and FICO 10T alongside Classic FICO; VantageScore 4.0 can count on-time rent payments, and both new models use 24-month trended data. When: VantageScore 4.0 broadly available to approved lenders since September 9, 2026; FICO 10T rollout follows. Where: nationwide, on loans sold to the GSEs and FHA-insured loans. Why it matters: a tenant-buyer whose rent history is clean but whose Classic FICO is thin may now qualify where they could not before; a seller who is also a future buyer may qualify sooner under the new models. The gray area: lender adoption varies, and most lenders still default to Classic FICO — knowing which lender uses which model is an edge most buyers do not have. Opportunity sign — need: tenant-buyers locked out by thin files want to buy; how: match tenant-buyers to lenders actually using VantageScore 4.0, and structure lease-options whose option period is timed to the new-model qualification; acquire: a current list of which local lenders accept VantageScore 4.0 (public information, gathered by calling lenders), and a rent-reporting path so the tenant-buyer's rent history is actually in the data. Never money from Mac, never spend, never hold money. Mac decides per case.
- **(b) The free dispute as a deal tool (gray area — lawful, but handle with care).** Who: sellers (and tenant-buyers) whose reports contain errors. What: disputing is free, the bureau has 30 days to investigate, and anything it cannot verify must be corrected or deleted. Incorrect information was the most common CFPB complaint in 2025 — errors are common. When: before a seller tries to refinance or a buyer tries to qualify — the 30-day clock means starting early matters. Where: AnnualCreditReport.com for the reports; bureau portals and the CFPB's sample letters for the dispute. Why it matters: a single wrongly reported 30-day late can cost 50–100 score points; removing it can move a seller from "cannot refinance" to "can refinance" — which changes whether they must sell at a discount at all. The gray area: you may *inform* a seller that free dispute rights exist and point them to the CFPB's own guidance; you must not file disputes on their behalf, charge for it, or promise a score outcome. That line is the difference between education and unlicensed credit repair. Opportunity sign — need: sellers who believe their credit is worse than it is; how: a one-page, plain-language handout (free public sources only) explaining weekly free reports and the 30-day dispute right, given to sellers at first contact; acquire: nothing but the CFPB's published sample letters and the AnnualCreditReport.com link. Never money from Mac, never spend, never hold money. Mac decides per case.
- **(c) The assumable-loan blind spot (loophole — public, underused).** Who: sellers with FHA or VA loans at low rates; buyers who cannot qualify for a new loan at today's rates. What: FHA and VA loans are generally assumable — a buyer can take over the seller's existing loan, rate and all, subject to lender approval and (for VA) entitlement rules. Most sellers and many agents do not know this, and most wholesalers never ask what loan type the seller has. When: whenever the seller's rate is well below current rates and the buyer can meet the assuming lender's credit requirements. Where: any property with an FHA or VA loan. Why it matters: an assumable 3% loan is worth more than a cash discount in many cases, and it changes the math on whether a seller must sell below market — they may instead sell at or near market to an assuming buyer. The gray area: assumption still requires the buyer to qualify with the servicer, and the seller's VA entitlement can stay tied up; get the servicer's written assumption terms before promising anything. Opportunity sign — need: buyers priced out by current rates; sellers with low-rate FHA/VA loans; how: add one question to the seller intake — "Is your loan FHA or VA?" — and, if yes, check assumability with the servicer (with the seller's written permission to speak to the servicer); acquire: the servicer's assumption requirements, obtained lawfully through the seller. Never money from Mac, never spend, never hold money. Mac decides per case.
- **(d) The divorce-credit timing play (gray area — lawful, sensitive).** Who: divorcing sellers whose ex-spouse's missed payments are on their report. What: a divorce decree does not bind creditors, so a seller can be credit-damaged by an ex's non-payment even when the court assigned the debt to the ex. The seller often cannot refinance or qualify for a new mortgage until the joint accounts are closed or the 7-year clock runs. When: during and after divorce, especially before the decree is final. Where: any divorce involving joint mortgage or card debt. Why it matters: this is one of the largest single sources of must-sell-below-market sellers, and it is also a situation where a lease-option or seller-financed exit helps the *seller* become a buyer again later. The gray area is human, not legal: this seller is in a painful life event, and the wholesaler's job is to solve the housing problem honestly, not to exploit the distress. Never present yourself as a lawyer or credit counselor. Opportunity sign — need: divorcing sellers who need out of a joint mortgage but cannot refinance; how: structure the exit (subject-to, lease-option, or cash assignment) around the seller's actual constraint — they need the mortgage payment covered and their name eventually off the loan; acquire: a divorce-attorney referral relationship (for the seller's own counsel) and a plain-language explanation of why the decree alone does not fix the credit report. Never money from Mac, never spend, never hold money. Mac decides per case.
