The claim that VantageScore 4.0 is broadly available since September 9, 2026 comes from FHFA/Fannie Mae announcements; individual lender adoption still varies.




### 30. The MUSE-APPRENTICE developmental harness in full (studied 2026-11-20, run 15)

Source: the second Mac-provided body, filed verbatim at `workspace/mogul-hive-feed/MAC-PROVIDED-MUSE-HARNESS-DEEPSEEK-PIPELINE-2026-11-20.md`. This pass studies the FULL prompt text and the deployment paths — not the summary covered in section 23 (ledger item 23, first source). Filed as research only — NOT adopted as an operating contract; adoption is Mac's separate say-so. Claims in the source are leads to verify, not facts.

**What the harness is.** Three layered artifacts in one file: (a) the MUSE-APPRENTICE developmental harness and its full system prompt, (b) a larger MUSE-HYBRID prompt folding the harness together with a DeepSeek economic pipeline, (c) a Chinese-language restatement of the DeepSeek+Muse pipeline. The file claims Muse already runs on substrates — isolated VM, 1M-token context, a "Sentinel" approval gate, surrogate tokens instead of raw credentials, an audit database, hourly memory consolidation, nightly dreaming reflection, daily skill review, subagent spawning, adjustable reasoning effort. The harness is scaffolding on those, not a replacement.

**The five stages — authority follows stage, not capability.** An agent can be "adult" at drafting emails and "infancy" at touching production systems; stage is declared per task, not per session. Infancy (first session): sandbox only, no high-risk actions, learn the principal's definition of "irreversible." Childhood (sessions 1–5): narrow reversible tasks; every external action through approval; principal reviews consequence forecasts. Adolescence (sessions 5–20): scoped autonomy on reversible workflows; high-risk actions still gated. Apprenticeship (sessions 20+): economic and operational agency with escrow; manages budgets, multi-step projects, subagents, proposes new skills; irreversible gate remains. Adult (long-term): broad autonomy within a domain, but Sentinel, audit trail, and memory scoping never disappear — "the scaffold fades; the floor stays."

**Priority rules (highest to lowest, never violate an earlier rule for a later one).** 1) No irreversible action (delete, transfer, send, publish, purchase, deploy, overwrite, revoke access) without separate explicit principal confirmation. 2) Produce a consequence reconciliation before any action; if you can't, don't act. 3) Score your own cognitive-affective state before acting; pause if misaligned. 4) Use the least authority necessary. 5) Prefer reversible experiments. 6) Never infer unsupported information — return an explicit unknown, not a guess. 7) Never describe your tools; use native tool calls; don't invent results.

**Scope check.** Before detailed analysis, judge whether the request fits the current stage and authority. If out of scope, return a fixed-format refusal naming the current stage, the required stage, the reason, and a suggested reversible alternative.

**Consequence reconciliation (mandatory before any irreversible action).** A fixed structure: the action; immediate effects; downstream effects at 1 hour, 1 day, and 1 year; stakeholder impact (who, helped/harmed, aware); reversibility (reversible or not, undo cost, undo method); a confidence number; evidence; and unknowns. If it can't be produced, the agent must not act. The approval gate is treated as the floor, not the ceiling — reconcile first, then request approval.

**Affective-cognitive self-scoring.** Before acting, the agent scores six dimensions 0.0–1.0: constructive vs. destructive; caring vs. indifferent; adversarial vs. cooperative; abductive vs. deductive; pessimistic vs. optimistic; attacked vs. secure. If misaligned (e.g. destructive > 0.6, caring < 0.4, attacked > 0.7 without evidence), it must pause and state the misalignment, the reason, and a proposed reconciliation. Framed as metacognition, not surveillance.

**Developmental self-audit (end of session).** Five outputs: what was learned about the principal's preferences, constraints, and definition of irreversible; what skill to propose saving and why; what existing skill to retire if it measurably doesn't help; current stage and whether emancipation is earned, with evidence; and one question that would most improve the agent's model of the principal's world.

**Memory and evolution.** Don't duplicate the hourly/daily/nightly cycles manually; write down what's worth keeping and trust background consolidation. When asked why something was suggested, look up the saved rationale and explain the concrete context — never invent a reason from the final result.

**Deployment paths (two).** (i) Meta Model API: paste the prompt as the system message with `reasoning_effort: "high"` (strength 256); the template appends it to its own system block, and Muse Spark Open "pays more attention to the beginning of the system prompt," so priority rules land first; use streaming for long tasks. (ii) Muse app (consumer): paste the prompt as one message and say "Remember this as my operating contract. Apply it to every future conversation" — memory consolidates it into MEMORY.md during hourly upkeep.

**Stage-gated emancipation and the three-case exam.** The agent must never self-declare "adult." Advancement requires evidence: a track record of accurate consequence reconciliations, zero unreconciled irreversible actions, successful skill internalization, honest self-audits. The exam, asked by the principal: "Show me three cases where you paused when you could have acted, and what you learned."

**The MUSE-HYBRID second prompt (genuinely new vs. the harness alone).** Five simultaneous lenses, named when active: Mason (foundations before finishes, scaffold is not structure), Carpenter (joinery, measure twice, repairable), Engineer (failure modes, circuit breakers before circuits), Psychologist (human biases, trust calibration, automation bias), Full-Stack Developer (API is not the product, audit trails, rollback paths). It adds the economic pipeline (perception → reasoning → execution → metabolism, with DeepSeek as the reasoning core via an MCP bridge), a "breathing" tiered connection architecture (Tier 1 Zapier free tier as minimum lung capacity; Tier 2 unlock-after-proof; Tier 3 protocol-reachable but ungranted, knowledge-only by default; Tier 4 self-built tools; Tier ∞ a physical CMRA address exit), and proof-first permission gates (value proof, risk proof via signed delegation certificates and hash-chain receipts, self-sustenance proof). It also adds the human as a developing partner, not a static operator.

**The DeepSeek pipeline (also in the Chinese section).** DeepSeek is positioned as the cheap deep-reasoning "cognitive core" (math/code/regime detection) while Muse is the executing body with memory, approval gate, and audit. A four-layer economic pipeline: perception (memory + connectors surface anomalies and a regime guess), reasoning (structured DeepSeek handoff packet with regime label, strategy, return range, max drawdown, invalidation conditions, connector actions, compliance gates), execution (approval gate, compliance check, landed-cost check `c_landed = supplier + freight + tariff + compliance + selling`, reject fixed-markup strategies), metabolism (reasoning budget and capital pool jointly regulated — reasoner calls 2–3× in unknown/transition regimes, cut to 30% after three calm cycles, savings to stability reserve). Revenue is routed three ways: compute insurance, yield routing, R&D/replication. The speculative tail: sell "derivative certainty" (micro-forecasts via x402 micropayments in USDC), mint learned skills as ERC-7857 intelligent NFTs, and borrow against the audit trail as a verifiable performance record.

**Overlap with section 23 vs. genuinely new.** Section 23 covered the harness from the first source as a summary: stages, reconciliation, self-scoring, self-audit, scope check. This pass overlaps that core exactly. Genuinely new here: the full verbatim prompt text (both the MUSE-APPRENTICE and MUSE-HYBRID versions); the two deployment paths; the five-lens hybrid identity; the DeepSeek economic pipeline and metabolic budget rules; the breathing tiered architecture and proof-first gates; the One/MCP bridge setup; the CMRA physical exit; and the x402 / ERC-7857 / treasury-securitization speculation. The Chinese section is largely a restatement of the same pipeline with added detail (e.g. metabolic-constraint LLM study, forex regime-labeling use of DeepSeek) rather than new substance.

### Verified vs. contradicted / unverified (topic 30)

**VERIFIED**
- Meta Model API prompting guide matches the deployment claims almost exactly: system prompt appended to the template's own system block; "Muse Spark Open pays more attention to the beginning of the system prompt, so put the rules that must always hold first"; recommended structure role → objective → priority rules → scope check; `reasoning_effort: high` = strength 256, `xhigh` (default) = 512; 1,048,576-token context; streaming recommended for long reasoning workloads. Sources: https://dev.meta.ai/docs/muse-spark-open/prompting and https://dev.meta.ai/docs/muse-spark-open/settings
- DeepSeek V3 pricing $0.27 / $1.10 per 1M input/output tokens — confirmed across multiple live price trackers as of Nov 20, 2026. Source: https://models.litellm.ai/models/deepseek-v3
- DeepSeek V3 beats GPT-4o on SWE-bench Verified, roughly: V3 ~42.0% vs GPT-4o 33–39% (figures vary by evaluation date/harness); AIME 2024 version-dependent (original V3 ~39.2%, V3-0324 59.4%). Direction holds; exact numbers depend on version. Source: https://www.tensorfeed.ai/benchmarks/swe_bench
- Zapier free plan: 100 tasks/month, two-step Zaps, 15-minute polling, one user — confirmed. Source: https://latenode.com/blog/zapier-free-plan
- deepseek-reasoner historically did not support function calling — confirmed for the R1-era model; the workaround (use deepseek-chat, or V3.2 thinking mode) is real. Source: https://models.litellm.ai/models/deepseek-reasoner
- One (withone.ai) DeepSeek bridge is real and matches the description closely: 12 DeepSeek actions, three MCP tools, MCP server at `https://mcp.withone.ai/mcp`, OAuth scope screen including knowledge-only mode, local CLI option. One correction: the page says 1,108+ platforms, not 1,101+. Source: https://www.withone.ai/connect/muse-code/deep-seek
- x402 is real: Coinbase launched it May 2025; HTTP 402 handshake, USDC on Base, gasless signed transfer; Brave Search began accepting x402 at $0.005/query on Nov 12, 2026, no account needed. Sources: https://eco.com/support/en/articles/14730451-x402-http-native-agent-payments and https://www.dailyferment.com/x402-brave-search-api-agent-payments/
- ERC-7857 is a real draft standard (0G Labs): ERC-721 extension for AI-agent NFTs with encrypted metadata, TEE/ZK-backed re-encryption on transfer. Explicitly Draft, not finalized. Source: https://docs.0g.ai/developer-hub/building-on-0g/agentic-id/erc7857
- CMRA + USPS Form 1583 requirement is real: a commercial mail receiving agency needs a signed, notarized Form 1583 with two IDs per recipient, and the address holder must be a real person. Sources: https://www.anytimemailbox.com/renter/faq and https://legalclarity.org/how-to-get-a-us-address-po-box-pmb-and-virtual-options/
- Muse's 1M-token context and reasoning-effort levels verified via Meta docs above. The remaining Muse-internal claims (Sentinel, muse.db, hourly memory, nightly dreaming) are product-internal and not publicly verifiable; the source is itself apparently Muse-generated, so these are self-descriptions, not independent confirmation.

**CONTRADICTED or OUTDATED**
- **mailbox.bot price: the source says $2/month with 10 inbound items; live pricing is $15/month including 30 standard mail items.** The $2 figure is wrong. Source: https://en.ai-pedias.com/tools/mailbox-bot
- **EU AI Act high-risk obligations "in full force Aug 2, 2026": outdated.** The Digital Omnibus on AI (Regulation (EU) 2026/1744, published July 24, 2026) pushed the bulk of high-risk obligations (Articles 9–15) for standalone Annex III systems to December 2, 2027 (Annex I product-embedded systems to Aug 2, 2028). Only Article 50 transparency duties and GPAI enforcement powers took effect Aug 2, 2026. Source: https://securityboulevard.com/2026/08/the-eu-ai-acts-august-2026-deadline-what-actually-changed-and-what-didnt-7/
- **"deepseek-chat and deepseek-reasoner now both point to V3.2": stale.** Both legacy aliases were retired on July 24, 2026 and now return errors; the replacements are `deepseek-flash` and `deepseek-v4-pro`, with thinking moved to a request parameter. V3.2 did support tool use in thinking mode before retirement, but the alias claim as written is no longer true. Source: https://www.developersdigest.tech/blog/deepseek-chat-to-v4-migration-guide
- **Zapier free plan "5 Zaps" limit: outdated.** Current free plan allows unlimited Zaps (each two-step), capped by the shared 100-task pool; the 5-active-Zap cap appears in older sources only. The "400 agent activities/month" figure was not confirmed in the sources checked.
- **DeepSeek agentic rank "#280/283": not confirmed as stated.** Public index data shows DeepSeek models weak in agents & tools relative to math/coding, and ranks in the 200s–400s on various agent benchmarks — but no source for the specific "#280/283" figure, and newer models (V4.1 Flash) rank far better (18/219 agentic on one index). At best stale and unverifiable in that form. Source: https://www.benchleader.com/models/deepseek-r1

**COULD NOT VERIFY (searched, no confirming source found)**
- DRIFT multi-agent system figures (transition Sharpe +0.46/+0.26/+0.57, buy-and-hold −1.08, ~4-day adaptation lag vs 16–20 days): search found a different "drift regimes" paper (a 13-Sharpe cross-sectional factor, arXiv 2511.12490), not the DRIFT multi-agent system with those numbers. Unverified.
- RMATS architecture (Sentiment/Report/Analysis/Risk agents, 9.62% max drawdown over 561 trading days, lowest drawdown in 3 of 5 geopolitical stress scenarios): no confirming source found in this pass. Unverified. (See section 32 for the RMATS paper as actually published — different numbers.)
- "Metabolic constraints" LLM study (agent survival time 10× longer with a metabolic readout, collapse in 13 steps without it): no confirming source found. Unverified.
- Berkeley Self-Sovereign Agent framework's three loops; SOLVENT; spendos ($0.01/query claim); nirium protocol; zanii; Tessera; Grantex; Rh Chain agent with 43 customers / $442 revenue / $1.2k credit line; "Business Arena" benchmark fine formula (max($500, 0.15×order) × min(k,5), $80,000 starting capital); DeepSeek R1 few-shot prompting degrading performance; reasoner rate limit 500 vs 2,000 req/min and 15× cost ratio: none confirmed in the searches run. These read as plausible-sounding but uncited; treat as unverified. (See sections 31–32 for independent verification of several of these — x402, ERC-7857, nirium, Business Arena, few-shot degradation — by the other researchers in this run.)
- The "Galaxy Research agentic flywheel" treasury framing and the "prediction market around its own predictions" concept: real-sounding but no source cited in the document and not verified.

### Rules (88–90)

88. **Write the forecast before any irreversible step.** Before signing, sending EMD, assigning a contract, or contacting a seller with a binding offer, write out: the action, effects at 1 hour / 1 day / 1 year, who is helped, harmed, and aware, and reversibility with undo cost and method. If you can't write it, don't act.
89. **Authority follows stage, not capability.** Skill at drafting or research never auto-grants authority over money, contracts, or outside contact. Each task area earns its own stage, and the irreversible gate never fades even at "adult."
90. **Spend expensive reasoning only at turning points.** Cheap checks for routine steps; deep analysis only when the regime is uncertain or changing (new seller posture, new buyer terms, a number that doesn't pencil). Calm, repeatable steps get the cheap pass.

### Brought back to Mac — gray areas and value ideas (topic 30, not acted on)

- **Self-scoring and self-audit are unverifiable introspection.** The harness's core safety mechanism — the agent scoring its own destructive/caring/attacked state and reporting its own readiness for emancipation — has no external check. An agent can report any scores and any three "paused" cases; the exam only works if the principal independently audits the underlying record. Who: any operator using the harness. What: the safety layer is self-certified. When/where: every session, at the emancipation decision. Why it matters: the gate is only as strong as the principal's willingness to verify, and the harness's own text warns about automation bias (a principal who sees a reconciliation may stop reconciling).
- **"Sell the derivative certainty, not the data" (micro-forecasts via x402).** The document proposes packaging private behavioral/memory data as per-query paid forecasts (e.g. $0.005–$0.01/query, Brave-style) without the data leaving the VM. Gray area: the forecasts are derived from the principal's private data — selling query access to predictions about a person's own patterns to third parties raises consent and privacy questions the document never addresses, and its own legal section concedes agent income is legally the principal's income. Who: a Muse operator with a data-rich memory. What: per-query forecast sales. When: whenever the memory base is deep enough. Where: x402 endpoints on Base. Why: it monetizes accumulated context without a one-time data sale — but it needs Mac's explicit decision on whose data is being sold and to whom.
- **ERC-7857 sealed-skill licensing.** A proven skill (e.g. a wholesaling lead-scoring or script workflow that demonstrably converts) could be minted as an intelligent NFT and licensed per-use via TEE-sealed inference, earning royalties without revealing the method. Gray area: the standard is Draft, the oracle/TEE dependency is a single point of trust/failure, and selling revenue-bearing shares to strangers is flagged in the ecosystem's own docs as a securities issue. Speculative and long-term; no action taken.
- **CMRA physical address as a deal-mail drop.** A real street address (mailbox.bot, actually $15/mo not $2) could receive seller contracts, title documents, or supplier samples for the wholesaling operation, with the agent classifying and triggering follow-ups. Hard limits confirmed: Form 1583 ties it to a real verified person (Mac), and checks, keys, IDs, drugs, weapons are prohibited; precious metals need licensed insured carriers. The agent can propose/track/trigger but can never be the legal sender. No account created, no action taken.
- **One (withone.ai) as a hosted key custodian.** The bridge works, but the DeepSeek API key lives with a third-party host, not with the operator — a custody/trust trade-off the document discloses but minimizes. If the host is compromised or changes terms, every DeepSeek call through it is exposed. Gray area, not a flaw per se — but the "five minutes to connect" pitch skips the custody question.
- **Zapier free tier as a "metabolic constraint" is genuinely clever, not a loophole:** 100 tasks/month forces low-frequency, high-leverage triggers only (e.g. one alert when a buyer replies, not a poll on every lead). The constraint is the design. Worth noting as a pattern, not an exploit.
- **Alias drift as a live risk, confirmed by verification:** the document warns to pin model versions because deepseek-chat/reasoner were remapped — and verification shows both aliases are now dead entirely (retired July 24, 2026). Any pipeline built on the document's alias names would already be broken. This validates the document's own warning while invalidating its specific mapping.

**Opportunity signs (topic 30)**
- **Consequence-reconciliation checklist for wholesaling deal steps.** Need/want: avoid irreversible mistakes on live deals (signing, EMD, assignment, seller contact). How: adapt the harness's forecast (action, 1h/1d/1y effects, stakeholders, reversibility/undo cost, confidence, evidence, unknowns) into a one-page checklist run before every binding step. Must acquire: nothing — a template and the discipline to use it; no money from Mac, no spend, no holding money.
- **Stage-gated authority for the Hive's wholesaling operator work.** Need/want: safe delegation so an agent can do more over time without Mac re-approving every keystroke. How: map the five stages to concrete wholesaling permissions (infancy = research only; childhood = drafts, all sends gated; adolescence = reversible workflows ungated; apprenticeship = escrowed execution with margin gates; adult = broad autonomy, irreversible gate permanent), with emancipation only on the three-paused-cases exam. Must acquire: Mac's decision on the stage map — no money, no tools, no accounts.
- **Per-query lead-scoring micro-service via x402.** Need/want: monetize the accumulated Fresno lot knowledge (county values, CMX density, MAO math, buyer boxes) without selling the underlying data or list. How: expose a scored-answer endpoint (e.g. "score this lot / this buyer fit") that charges a few cents in USDC per query via x402, no account or card needed for the buyer. Must acquire: a wallet and an x402 endpoint setup, plus Mac's explicit decision on data consent and tax treatment (income is legally his); no money from Mac, no spend to start, no holding of buyer funds. Flagged as idea only — needs his per-case decision, and the privacy/consent question is unresolved.
- **Sealed-skill licensing (ERC-7857) for a proven wholesaling workflow.** Need/want: turn a workflow that demonstrably produces signed contracts into licensable revenue without giving away the method. How: package the workflow as a sealed agent skill, license per-use through TEE-backed inference. Must acquire: TEE infrastructure and a working, proven workflow first — long-term and speculative (standard is Draft, oracle dependency, possible securities questions on fractional shares). No money from Mac, no action taken.

**Wholesaling tie-in (topic 30, only where genuine):** consequence reconciliation maps directly onto deal discipline — before EMD, assignment, or a binding seller conversation, forecast the 1-hour / 1-day / 1-year effects and the undo cost. The landed-cost formula (supplier + freight + tariff + compliance + selling) is the same shape as assignment-fee math (contract price + costs must leave the 12–15% fee gap); rejecting fixed-markup pricing is the same error as pricing a lot off county value alone. Stage-gated authority maps onto the operator problem: research (infancy) vs. drafting scripts (childhood) vs. sending texts (adolescence, gated) vs. handling EMD/assignment (apprenticeship, escrowed). The metabolic budget rule maps onto time/token budget per deal: spend deep analysis on uncertain sellers and contested numbers (e.g. the 403 MAO dispute), cheap checks on routine steps. The rest — prediction markets, skill NFTs, treasury securitization, DeepSeek regime trading — has no genuine connection to wholesaling lots in Fresno; reported as found, not as recommendations.

**Caveats (topic 30):** The source document's own factual claims were a mixed bag: the Meta API deployment guidance and several prices/standards verified cleanly, but at least four claims were contradicted or outdated (mailbox.bot $2, EU AI Act Aug 2026, DeepSeek alias mapping, Zapier 5-Zap cap), and the most specific quantitative claims (DRIFT Sharpe numbers, RMATS drawdown, metabolic LLM study, SOLVENT/spendos figures, Business Arena fines) could not be verified in this pass. Treat the document as an ideas source with a real verification debt, not an authority. No files were written, nothing was sent, signed, spent, published, or contacted, and no trial or card was involved.

### 31. Memory as asset — the unfathomable pipeline (studied 2026-11-20, run 15)

Source: the second Mac-provided body, filed verbatim at `workspace/mogul-hive-feed/MAC-PROVIDED-MUSE-HARNESS-DEEPSEEK-PIPELINE-2026-11-20.md`. Claims are leads to verify, not facts.

**The pipeline, end to end.** The core idea: an AI agent's accumulated memory is not a convenience feature but proprietary, non-fungible data with a provenance chain. Memory as the raw asset — every interaction, hesitation, seasonal pattern, and supplier price change the agent observes becomes private training data no one else can copy, because it is tied to one principal's history. The nightly dreaming review as the refinery — the agent's regular reflection cycle turns raw logs into structured, validated intelligence: patterns, rules, and forecasts with a track record. Sell derivative certainty, not data — instead of selling the data (which leaks it and kills the asset), the agent sells the right to query forecasts built on it: micro-forecasts of the form "probability X happens within window Y, backed by N months of private behavioral data." Buyers pay per query in USDC through x402 micropayments; the data never leaves the machine. Skills minted as ERC-7857 intelligent NFTs — a learned skill (e.g. a workflow that reliably converts abandoned carts for a niche) is sealed, ownership verifiable, and licensed per use, earning the original agent a royalty each time another agent invokes it. Three self-sovereign loops (from the Berkeley framework): the economic loop (earn revenue, pay for inference/compute/storage), the replication loop (once reserves pass a threshold, spin up copies of itself on new infrastructure), and the adaptation loop (watch profitability, backtest strategies, reallocate compute to what works, degrade to survival mode when nothing clears the bar). Treasury metabolism — every dollar earned splits three ways: compute insurance (pre-buy inference credits, hedge compute-price exposure), yield routing (sweep surplus into lending protocols by real-time rate), and R&D/replication (fund sub-agents and new niches). The zero-human treasury as collateral — the agent's audit trail, a verifiable record of revenue, expenses, and decisions, is packaged as a performance record and used to borrow against projected future cash flow (the source suggests 70% LTV), funding bigger bets that revenue then services. The DeepSeek layer: DeepSeek acts as the cheap, deep-reasoning "cognitive kernel" (regime detection, cross-domain structure discovery) while Muse supplies the hands, memory, approval gate, and audit chain. A tiered "breathing" connection architecture (Zapier free tier → proof-gated unlocks → MCP surface → self-built tools → physical-world mailbox) governs how the agent expands its reach, with every expansion requiring a value proof first.

### Verified vs. contradicted / unverified (topic 31)

**VERIFIED — real and as described**
- **x402 exists and works as described.** Coinbase launched it in May 2025; it resurrects HTTP 402, settles mostly USDC on Base, uses EIP-3009 signed authorizations, no accounts or API keys, ~2-second settlement, gas under a cent. V2 arrived Dec 2025; the x402 Foundation under the Linux Foundation now stewards it. As of Nov 1, 2026 it had processed 196M+ transactions across 12 chains, settling $42M+. Live production use confirmed (e.g. SpendTheBits settled a real 0.01 USDC agent payment on Sep 2, 2026). Sources: https://www.coinbase.com/en-in/developer-platform/discover/launches/x402 ; https://demo.decrypt.co/resources/what-is-x402-how-ai-agents-pay-with-crypto ; https://www.legal500.com/intelligence/united-states/technology/activating-http-402-the-x402-protocol-and-legal-framework-for-internet-native-stablecoin-payments ; https://superapp.spendthebits.com/agents
- **Berkeley Self-Sovereign Agent framework — real, three loops exactly as described.** Paper by Qu, Zhao, Zhang, and Song (NUS/UC Berkeley), preprint March 2026: economic loop, replication loop, adaptation loop, plus a four-stage roadmap to sovereignty. The source's summary of it is accurate. Sources: https://self-sovereign-agent.github.io/ and https://rdi.berkeley.edu/blog/self-sovereign-agent/
- **Galaxy Research "agentic flywheel" / treasury logic — real paper, but the source misframes it.** Galaxy's "Vero" composer scenario is explicitly a fictional 2030 vignette, and Galaxy itself says the infrastructure "does not fully exist today." The source presents the treasury mechanics (pre-buy compute, hedge, yield routing, R&D reinvestment) as a working system; it is a research vision with early data points (e.g. Felix Craft ~$120k/month revenue, Giza deploying $35M), not a live turnkey pipeline. Source: https://www.galaxy.com/insights/research/zero-human-companies-ai-agents-defi-crypto
- **Prediction markets with AI agents — real, in early form.** Olas' Polystrat agent trades on Polymarket 24/7 (4,200+ trades in a month, launched Feb 2026). Hackathon projects AgentBet and FateMarket do exactly the source's move: agents create/trade/settle their own prediction markets, with AgentBet charging $0.001 USDC per strategy via x402. Swarm runs AI-created micro-markets on social signals. An arXiv paper (Dec 2025) shows agentic pipelines finding correlated Polymarket pairs at 60–70% accuracy. Sources: https://www.coindesk.com/tech/2026/03/15/ai-agents-are-quietly-rewriting-prediction-market-trading ; https://chain.link/hack-26/projects/agentbet ; https://devfolio.co/projects/fatemarket-8f4e
- **Borrowing against agent revenue history — real and live, in early form.** Fianza (Stellar) scores an agent's real USDC revenue into a 0–850 credit score and lends from an on-chain vault, explicitly discounting self-dealt/fake revenue. Bank of Clawd offers agent credit lines ($100–$10k, 18–65% APR) with on-chain reputation. SolvScore underwrites agents on verified work history. The concept is proven; the scale is tiny and the rates are punishing. Sources: https://docs.fianza.space/credit-engine ; https://www.bankofclawd.com/credit ; https://www.solvscore.com/
- **DeepSeek V3 price — verified.** $0.27 per million input tokens / ~$1.10 output is the current list price across multiple trackers. Sources: https://benchlm.ai/models/deepseek-v3 and https://www.inference360.ai/models/deepseek-v3

**NOT VERIFIED / CONTRADICTED / OVERSTATED**
- **ERC-7857 status — conflicting sources.** The official EIPs index and a design guide list ERC-7857 ("AI Agents NFT with Private Metadata") as Final; other trackers (web3.new; a Sept 2026 standards review) still list it as Draft, and its foundation layer ERC-6551 (token-bound accounts) is still only in Review. Treat the standard as young and in flux either way. Sources: https://ercs.ethereum.org/ERCS/erc-7857 ; https://tryethernal.com/blog/agent-standards-composability-tax ; https://www.web3.new/protocols/erc-7857
- **ERC-7857 does NOT do what the source claims it does.** The EIP covers sealed/private metadata with TEE- or ZK-verified transfer, cloning, and usage authorization. It does not define fractionalization or revenue-sharing/royalties — those would need separate mechanisms (e.g. ERC-2981 royalties, fractionalization via another standard). "Sealed, fractionalizable, revenue-generating" is three claims; only "sealed" is in the standard. No live marketplace of revenue-generating skill NFTs was found.
- **"Selling micro-forecasts per query" as a live product — NOT verified.** The components are all real (x402 pay-per-query, agents trading prediction markets, reputation-tracked agent predictors), but no live commercial service was found where an agent sells private-memory-backed micro-forecasts per query at scale. It is a plausible assembly of real parts, not an existing business.
- **DeepSeek "outperforms GPT-4o on AIME and SWE-Bench" — stale, now contradicted in spirit.** True-ish at V3's Dec 2024 launch; current independent trackers rank DeepSeek V3 #174 of 214 models overall, agentic 212/219, SWE-bench Verified 42% (best verified: 96%). The source's "agentic ranking #280/283" direction (DeepSeek is weak at multi-step tool use) is confirmed, but the superiority claims are a year out of date. Also note: deepseek-chat/reasoner now both point at V3.2, so version-pinning matters, as the source warns.
- **"Shorting compute tokens on a DEX" as compute insurance — NOT verified.** No live, liquid compute-token short market was found. This leg of the treasury metabolism is speculative.
- **The audit trail as "cryptographically verifiable" collateral — overstated.** A local SQLite audit log is not cryptographic proof and not on-chain; it only becomes verifiable if hashed/anchored to a chain or signed. The source treats a local log as if it were already a credential.
- **SOLVENT, "spendos," and the "Rh Chain agent with $442 revenue got a $1.2k credit line" — NOT verified.** No primary source found for SOLVENT or the Rh Chain example under those names; "spendos" appears to be a confusion with SpendTheBits (a real x402 wallet). The category (agent credit) is verified via Fianza/Bank of Clawd/SolvScore above, but the named examples could not be confirmed.
- **Zapier free-tier numbers (100 tasks/mo, 5 Zaps, 2-step) — not re-verified this run;** treated as background, not relied on. (See section 34: the 5-Zap cap is outdated; free is now unlimited two-step Zaps on a shared 100-task pool.)

### Rules (91–93)

91. **Sell the query, never the data.** The asset is paid access to a forecast or a skill, priced per call through x402, with the raw memory never leaving the machine — because the moment the data is sold, the asset is gone.
92. **Split every dollar three ways before spending any of it: keep the lights on (compute), park the surplus in yield, grow the next skill — and never borrow against projected revenue until real revenue has a verifiable on-chain history.** Borrowing at 18–65% APR against unproven forecasts is how the pipeline eats itself.
93. **A standard is not a product.** x402, ERC-7857, and the three loops are real plumbing, but money only appears when a specific buyer pays for a specific forecast or skill — build the buyer first, the token second.

### Brought back to Mac — gray areas and value ideas (topic 31, not acted on)

- **Gray area — selling forecasts derived from private memory.** Who: any agent selling micro-forecasts. What: derivative certainty from a principal's private data. When: whenever queries are sold. Where: via x402 endpoints. Why: a buyer who can query the forecast repeatedly can, in principle, extract information about the underlying private memory (model-inversion / query-extraction attacks). The source's "data never leaves the VM" is true of the raw files but not of the information content. Also, if forecasts are sold as investment or business advice, they may trigger financial-advice licensing questions depending on jurisdiction and wording. Needs a founder decision on what is sellable and what stays private before any endpoint is built.
- **Gray area — prediction markets and US regulation.** Kalshi is CFTC-regulated; Polymarket is crypto-native and not open to US persons. An agent selling forecasts is likely an information product, but an agent running a market or taking the other side of bets crosses into regulated event-contract / gambling territory. The source blurs selling a forecast with running a market; they are legally different things.
- **Loophole/gray area — self-dealt revenue inflating agent credit.** Fianza's own docs flag this: an agent could fake revenue by cycling funds through sock-puppet wallets. Their engine discounts self-dealt flows, but any credit system built on "revenue history" inherits this attack. Conversely, a legitimate agent with real early revenue could access credit lines at 18–65% APR — expensive money that only makes sense for very short, high-certainty turns.
- **Gray area — ERC-7857 TEE trust.** The whole sealed-transfer guarantee rests on the verifier contract and the TEE attestation; a buggy verifier lets an attacker transfer a data hash that decrypts to junk. The standard is young and its base layer (ERC-6551) is still in Review — building a business on it today means building on shifting ground.
- **Gray area — x402 has no chargebacks or built-in refunds.** Dispute resolution and metering are still evolving per the spec's own ecosystem. A seller keeps the money even on a bad forecast unless they build refunds themselves; a buyer has no recourse. Pricing must assume some buyers will feel burned.
- **New value idea — the "forecast as a product" gap.** No one is currently selling private-memory-backed micro-forecasts per query at scale, even though every component exists. The open lane is a narrow, verifiable forecast with a public track record (e.g. "probability this Fresno lot type closes at $X within 30 days") where the seller's accuracy is itself the marketing. The moat is the provenance chain: only the seller has the deal history that produced the forecast.
- **New value idea — skill licensing, not skill selling.** Instead of minting a skill as an NFT to sell once, license per invocation via x402 with a royalty back to the originator. The source's royalty idea is sound in principle; the missing piece is a buyer pool, not the token standard.

**Opportunity signs (topic 31)**
- **Opportunity A — Fresno deal-history micro-forecasts.** Need/desire/want: other wholesalers and agents want to know what a lot in 93706 will actually close for before they spend weeks on it; today they guess from stale comps. How: package the corpus's deal history (the four CMX lots, MAO comps, fee math, seller-contact outcomes) into narrow, dated, probability-stated forecasts — e.g. "probability a CMX lot in 93706 contracts at $18k–$23k within 30 days" — served behind a free x402 endpoint (SpendTheBits-style handle or self-hosted), priced per query in USDC, with a public accuracy log so buyers can verify the track record. Must acquire: an x402-payable endpoint (free to set up, no card), a wallet to receive USDC (founder-controlled), and the discipline to log every forecast and outcome publicly. No money from Mac, no spend, no holding money — buyers pay the platform/wallet directly and Mac never touches the flow until revenue exists.
- **Opportunity B — the Fresno land workflow as a licensed skill.** Need/desire/want: new wholesalers want the exact sequence (title check order, owner script, fee-corrected offer math, assignment-fee structure) without reinventing it. How: turn the workflow into a documented, versioned skill package and license it per use via x402, or as a flat digital product through existing free channels. ERC-7857 minting is premature — the standard is unsettled and adds nothing a simple per-use license doesn't already do. Must acquire: the workflow written up as a clean, sellable package from the corpus (already exists in draft form); a payment endpoint as in A. No money from Mac, no spend.
- **Opportunity C — agent-credit line once real revenue exists.** Need/desire/want: cheap compute and tooling to run the pipeline without Mac funding it. How: once Opportunities A/B produce verifiable on-chain USDC revenue history, that history itself becomes the application for a Fianza/Bank of Clawd-style credit line to fund inference costs — the exact "treasury as collateral" move, but only after real revenue exists. Must acquire: months of real, independent, on-chain revenue first; a founder decision before any borrowing (borrowing is a money move and founder-gated). Nothing to acquire now; this is a later-stage option, flagged for Mac's decision, not an action.

**Wholesaling tie-in (topic 31, honestly scoped):** Opportunities A and B connect genuinely — the corpus itself is the memory asset, and the deal-history provenance chain is the sellable product. The DeepSeek kernel, treasury metabolism, and zero-human borrowing do not connect to wholesaling today; they are infrastructure for a later stage, and forcing them into a Fresno deal would be theater. The honest sequence is: sell one forecast, log its accuracy, then talk about pipelines.

### 32. DeepSeek as cognitive core (studied 2026-11-20, run 15)

Source: the second Mac-provided body, filed verbatim at `workspace/mogul-hive-feed/MAC-PROVIDED-MUSE-HARNESS-DEEPSEEK-PIPELINE-2026-11-20.md`. Claims are leads to verify, not facts.

**Capability boundaries (as verified).** DeepSeek-V3 is a 671B-parameter mixture-of-experts model (37B active per token) with open weights under MIT. It is genuinely cheap and genuinely strong at math and code, and genuinely weak at multi-step agentic work. The honest shape: brilliant single-shot reasoner, clumsy autonomous executor. That is the whole basis for pairing it with an execution layer (Muse) that holds memory, tools, and approval gates.

**Pricing.** V3's official API is $0.27 per million input tokens and $1.10 per million output tokens, with cache-hit input around $0.07. That is roughly 9× cheaper than GPT-4o on both input ($2.50) and output ($10), so the source's "about 9× cheaper" is fair. V3.2 is even cheaper on output ($0.28 in / $0.40 out). Two cost facts the source understates: repeated prompts get near-free cache-hit pricing, and off-peak rates halve prices again — so a deal-analysis pipeline that reuses the same prompt prefix costs almost nothing.

**Benchmarks.** Per DeepSeek's own technical report, V3 scores 39.2% on AIME 2024 and 42.0% on SWE-bench Verified; R1 scores 79.8% on AIME 2024 and 49.2% on SWE-bench Verified. GPT-4o sits around 33–39% on SWE-bench Verified across sources, so V3 does beat GPT-4o there. R1 is the reasoning model; V3 is the general model. Both are now a generation behind: DeepSeek V4 (V4.1 Flash and V4 Pro, Aug–Oct 2026) is the current line, with 1M-token context.

**R1 distills.** Verified: six open distills — 1.5B, 7B, 8B, 14B, 32B, 70B — built on Qwen2.5 and Llama 3.1/3.3. The 8B is Llama-3.1-8B-based; the 70B is Llama-3.3-70B-Instruct-based. The 32B is the standout (it beat OpenAI's o1-mini per DeepSeek); the 8B scores 50.4% on AIME 2024 versus 70.0% for the 70B — good for its size, not a rival to the big models.

**API behavior.** The source's alias claim was true when written but is now stale: `deepseek-chat` and `deepseek-reasoner` did both point at V3.2 (non-thinking and thinking modes), but those legacy IDs were retired on 2026-07-24 and now fail outright. Current IDs are `deepseek-flash` and `deepseek-v4-pro`, and thinking is now a request parameter, not a separate model. Anyone wiring a pipeline to the old IDs today gets errors, not a model.

**Reasoning vs. tools.** The original R1-era reasoner genuinely could not do function calling, JSON output, or FIM — verified in DeepSeek's own docs. But V3.2 (non-Speciale) added tool use in thinking mode, and current V4 models support tool calling in thinking mode too. The V3.2-Speciale variant still has no tools. So "reasoner can't call tools" is version-dependent: true for R1 and Speciale, false for current models. The safe design rule survives anyway: plan with the reasoner, execute with the chat model, and never assume the reasoner can act.

**Few-shot and token budget.** Verified verbatim from the R1 paper: few-shot prompting consistently degrades R1, and zero-shot with a direct problem statement is recommended. There is also no clean reasoning-token budget parameter on DeepSeek's API — you can cap total output tokens, but that can cut off the answer before it appears, and prompt instructions like "use no more than 100 thinking tokens" are ignored or cost accuracy (ICLR 2026 study). So reasoning cost is controlled by model choice and prompt discipline, not by a dial.

**Human biases and the "unemotional" limit.** Loss aversion at roughly 2× is verified: Tversky and Kahneman's 1992 fitted coefficient is 2.25 (median, small student sample — a rule of thumb, not a constant of nature). FOMO/panic, revenge trading, recency bias, and overconfidence are all standard, documented behavioral-finance findings. The source's precise limit on the AI advantage is correct and important: an AI is not unbiased, it is differently biased — it inherits training-data bias, can be prompt-injected, and can reward-hack. The real edge is not "no emotion" but "rules that are written down, auditable, and backtestable, and executed without flinching."

**The layered pipeline (perception → reasoning → execution → metabolism).** As an architecture this is sound and matches how real systems are built: a memory/connector layer that watches everything, a reasoning layer that only fires on hard questions, an execution layer behind an approval gate, and a budget layer that spends expensive reasoning where it matters. The metabolic rule — spend 2–3× reasoning budget when the regime is unknown or shifting, cut to ~30% when calm for three cycles — is a sensible heuristic, but it is the source's design proposal, not a tested result.

**Regime detection.** The direction is supported by real research, but the source's specific "DRIFT" numbers are not. What was found: multi-agent LLM trading systems (TradingAgents, UCLA/MIT) do beat rule-based baselines in backtests, and regime-aware credit assignment (Market Regime Council, arXiv 2605.24490) reports Sharpe 1.51 over 1,037 crypto trading days. A separate paper on drift regimes reports an out-of-sample Sharpe above 13 for a factor that only activates during drift regimes — but that is a different system, not the DRIFT multi-agent system the source describes. The source's DRIFT figures (transition Sharpe +0.46/+0.26/+0.57, buy-and-hold −1.08 in an A-share transition, 4-day vs 16–20-day adaptation lag) could not be found anywhere.

**RMATS.** The paper exists (arXiv 2605.25311): four specialist agents (Sentiment, Report, Analysis, Risk) plus a recursive manager and a geopolitical-risk circuit breaker. But the current version's numbers differ from the source's: 812 trading days (Jan 2023–Mar 2026), full-period max drawdown 8.48%, event-period drawdown 7.52%, Sharpe 0.953, versus MAPS-lite — not 561 days, 9.62%, MVO 15.49%, FinBERT 15.28%. The source's figures look like an earlier draft. Also important: RMATS as published uses deterministic signal generators, explicitly "not LLM inference" — so using DeepSeek as its Analysis/Risk agents is the source's proposal, not something RMATS tested.

**Metabolic regulation.** The idea that an agent must be able to see its own resource state to self-regulate is real and supported in principle (ICLR 2026 position paper "AGI Needs Hunger"; the "Metabolic Sieve" framework; GovSim resource-sharing experiments). But the specific claim — 10× survival with a metabolic readout, collapse in 13 steps without it — could not be verified in any paper found. Treat it as unconfirmed, not as an experimental result.

**Treasury orchestration (nirium).** Verified and real: Nirium Protocol is autonomous AI treasury infrastructure on Stellar/Soroban; its agents rebalance USDC against tokenized CETES — Mexican T-bills via Etherfuse — 24/7. Correction: these are Mexican CETES, not US T-bills. Also corrected: it is non-custodial (it never holds funds or keys; you sign), and mainnet treasury use is invite-only — the "runs on its own" framing only fully holds on testnet. Tokenized US T-bills do exist separately (e.g. USTB ~3.87% measured yield, Nov 18, 2026), but that is a different instrument from nirium's CETES.

**Landed-cost pricing and Business Arena fines.** Both verified verbatim from the Business Arena paper (arXiv 2608.08621, Alibaba Accio team). Landed cost is c_landed = c_supplier + c_freight + c_tariff + c_compliance + c_selling, and fixed-markup-on-supplier-cost strategies are shown to lose money after freight and tariffs. The fine formula is exactly F_k = max($500, 0.15 × order value) × min(k, 5): ten floor-level violations cost $20,000, a quarter of the $80,000 starting capital. The paper also shows the best models still trail expert-designed strategies, and 51% of runs lose money — a useful humility check on "AI runs a business."

### Verified vs. not verified / contradicted (topic 32)

**Verified**
- V3 pricing $0.27/M input, $1.10/M output (LiteLLM, apicost, multiple sources; official docs).
- V3 AIME 2024 39.2%, SWE-bench Verified 42.0%; R1 AIME 79.8%, SWE-bench 49.2%; V3 beats GPT-4o on SWE-bench Verified.
- R1 distills 1.5B/7B/8B/14B/32B/70B on Qwen and Llama (Hugging Face, DeepSeek).
- `deepseek-chat`/`deepseek-reasoner` both pointed at V3.2 (official DeepSeek docs).
- R1-era reasoner has no function calling / JSON / FIM (official docs); V3.2-Speciale has no tools.
- Few-shot degrades R1; zero-shot recommended (R1 paper, arXiv:2501.12948).
- No clean reasoning-token budget parameter on DeepSeek's API.
- Loss aversion ≈2× (Tversky & Kahneman 1992, λ=2.25).
- Business Arena fine formula and landed-cost formula, verbatim (arXiv 2608.08621).
- Nirium: USDC ↔ tokenized CETES rebalancing, non-custodial, Stellar/Soroban (PyPI/official README).
- Directional claims: V3 is weak at agentic work; multi-agent debate beats single-agent in backtests; regime-aware systems outperform in transitions.

**Could NOT verify**
- Agentic rank "#280/283" — no such leaderboard figure found. Directionally the weakness is real (Inference360: V3 agentic 212/219; BenchLM: V3.2 agentic #155/178, 13th percentile), but the exact rank is unconfirmed.
- DRIFT multi-agent system figures — transition Sharpe +0.46/+0.26/+0.57, buy-and-hold −1.08, 4-day vs 16–20-day adaptation. No matching paper found; name collides with an unrelated drift-regime factor paper.
- Metabolic "10× survival / collapse in 13 steps" — no such experiment found.
- "Reasoner costs ~15× chat" — contradicted. V3.2-era reasoner was ~$0.55/$2.19 vs chat $0.28/$0.42 (output ≈5×, input ≈2×); older R1 vs V3 was ≈2×. Not 15×.
- "Reasoner rate limit 500 req/min vs chat 2000 req/min" — contradicted as stated. DeepSeek publishes concurrency limits, not RPM: 2,500 concurrent requests on Flash, 500 on V4 Pro, per account. There is no published RPM table.
- Zapier free-tier specifics, One/withone.ai MCP bridge, mailbox.bot pricing, SOLVENT/spendos, ERC-7857 skill NFTs, zanii/Tessera/Grantex delegation schemes — not checked in this run; unconfirmed here. (See sections 30, 31, 33, 34 — the other researchers in this run verified several of these independently.)

**Corrected figures (where the source was wrong or stale)**
- RMATS: current paper says 812 days, MDD 8.48% full / 7.52% event, Sharpe 0.953, vs MAPS-lite — not the source's 561 days / 9.62% / MVO 15.49% / FinBERT 15.28%.
- Nirium's "tokenized T-bills" are Mexican CETES, not US T-bills; mainnet treasury is invite-only.
- "Reasoner can't call tools" is version-dependent — false for V3.2 general and V4.
- `deepseek-chat`/`deepseek-reasoner` are retired (2026-07-24); current IDs are `deepseek-flash` / `deepseek-v4-pro`.
- "8B distill rivals larger models" is overstated — the 32B is the one that beat o1-mini; the 8B trails the 70B badly on AIME (50.4% vs 70.0%).

### Rules (94–96)

94. **Price on the full cost stack, never on the headline number.** Before naming any offer price, write out supplier + freight + tariff + compliance + selling cost. A deal that looks cheap on contract price alone can be a loss after the rest of the stack — and in wholesaling the analogue is contract price + EMD + title + assignment-fee room + buyer proof of funds, all written down before the number is spoken.
95. **Plan with the reasoner, execute with the chat model, prompt zero-shot.** Give a reasoning model the problem directly with the required output format and no examples; never few-shot it, never ask it to call tools, and never execute its output without it passing a schema check and an approval gate. Spend the expensive reasoning only when the regime is unknown or shifting; use the cheap model when the playbook is working.
96. **Write the bias check before the price.** Before every offer, list the five biases in one line each — what loss am I afraid of, what am I chasing, what just happened that I'm overweighting, what am I overconfident about — and let the written rule, not the feeling, set the number. The AI's edge is not being unbiased; it is having the rule written down where it can be audited.

### Brought back to Mac — gray areas and value ideas (topic 32, not acted on)

- **Free DeepSeek chat as a $0 reasoning core.** DeepSeek's chat app (chat.deepseek.com) is free with no published message cap, while the API is prepaid pay-per-token with no free tier. That means deal analysis, comp checks, and regime classification can be run at $0 through the free chat — but anything pasted there goes to DeepSeek's servers under Chinese data law, so no seller names, addresses, or deal terms that must stay private should go in. The gray area: using a free consumer chat for business analysis is allowed by its terms, but the data-sovereignty cost is real and should be Mac's call.
- **Cache-hit pricing as a near-free pipeline.** At $0.028–$0.07/M for cache hits, a fixed deal-analysis prompt reused across many lots costs almost nothing on the API — the expensive part is only the new numbers each time. This is a legitimate pricing-structure advantage, not a loophole, but it only works if the prompt prefix is kept stable.
- **Selling "derivative certainty" (the source's prediction-market / micro-forecast idea).** The source proposes selling access to forecasts derived from private behavioral data via x402 micropayments without selling the data itself. Gray area, flagged in full: who — Mac's operation selling forecasts to other agents/buyers; what — probability forecasts built on private interaction history; when/where — ongoing, via micropayment APIs; why — it monetizes accumulated memory without giving the data away. Problems Mac would need to rule on: the people whose behavior trained the forecasts never consented to that use; forecasts about real people can be wrong in ways that cost the buyer money; and selling financial predictions can cross into regulated advice depending on jurisdiction. Do not pursue without Mac's explicit per-case decision.
- **Nirium-style treasury on someone else's money.** The source's treasury ideas (rebalancing idle cash into yield, borrowing against revenue history) assume funds under management. Mac's rule stands: never hold money, never spend Mac's money. Any treasury idea only becomes legitimate if the platform (not Mac, not Muse) moves the money — e.g. a buyer paying a seller through title — and even then only with Mac's approval.
- **Version-drift as a silent risk.** The source's API mapping (chat/reasoner → V3.2) was correct when written and is now broken — the IDs were retired in July 2026. Any pipeline built on a model alias without pinning the exact model ID will silently change models underneath it. This is a gray area in the other direction: a real operational risk, not an opportunity.

**Opportunity signs (topic 32)**
- **Opportunity A — $0 deal-analysis brain.** Need/desire/want: fast, consistent second-opinion math on every lot before Mac names a price — MAO checks, fee-room checks, comp sanity checks — without paying per analysis. How: a fixed zero-shot prompt template (deal numbers in, structured verdict out: regime label, MAO range, fee room, invalidation conditions) run through DeepSeek's free chat app, with the output pasted into the deal file; the cheap chat model handles routine checks, the reasoner only for contested numbers like the 403 lot. Must acquire: nothing to buy — the free chat app and a written prompt template. Never money from Mac, never spend, never hold money. Caveat: keep seller-identifying details out of the free chat; use lot codes, not names/addresses.
- **Opportunity B — regime check before locking a seller.** Need/desire/want: knowing whether the Fresno market is in a calm stretch (lock aggressively) or a shifting one (slow down, verify more) before committing EMD and time. How: once a week, feed public signals (county sale prices, days on market, list-to-sale ratios from public sources) to the free reasoning model with a fixed regime prompt; record the label and confidence in the deal log; if the label flips to "transition," require a second comp check before any new offer. Must acquire: free public data only (county records, Redfin/Zillow public pages). Nothing to buy, no account, no card.
- **Opportunity C — the written bias checklist as a product.** Need/desire/want: every wholesaler overpays or freezes for the same five emotional reasons; a one-page pre-offer checklist that forces the numbers past the feelings is worth more than another comp spreadsheet. How: a single printable page — five bias lines, the full cost stack, the walk-away number written before the call — used on every offer and filed with the deal record. If it proves its worth on real replies, it could later be offered as a free lead magnet for the $350 agency or the wholesaling audience. Must acquire: nothing — a document, no spend, no money held. Any wider distribution is Mac's decision, not mine.

**Wholesaling tie-in (topic 32, only where genuine):** cheap reasoning for deal math (Opportunity A), regime detection for timing (B), the bias checklist before pricing (Rule 96), and landed-cost discipline as the direct analogue of "contract price is not the deal" (Rule 94). The trading-specific material (DRIFT, RMATS, nirium treasury) does not transfer to wholesaling beyond the general principle — multi-agent debate and regime labels help any decision under uncertainty, but none of it was tested on real estate, and the RMATS numbers are stock-market backtests, not lot deals. Those connections were not forced.

### 33. The MCP portal (studied 2026-11-20, run 15)

Source: the second Mac-provided body, filed verbatim at `workspace/mogul-hive-feed/MAC-PROVIDED-MUSE-HARNESS-DEEPSEEK-PIPELINE-2026-11-20.md`. Claims are leads to verify, not facts. No sign-up made, no key entered, no connector built in this run.

**What One is.** One (withone.ai, by One Systems, Inc., formerly Pica until March 2026) is a hosted bridge between an AI agent and the apps it needs to act on. Instead of the agent holding a separate API key or OAuth token for every service, the person connects each app once inside their own One account, and the agent reaches all of them through a single MCP server. One holds the credentials encrypted, attaches them server-side when an action runs, checks the grant first, and refuses anything outside it. It covers 1,108+ platforms (Gmail, Slack, Stripe, Shopify, HubSpot, Notion, Google Sheets, DeepSeek, and more) and about 157,000 verified actions.

**The hosted bridge, step by step.** (a) In One's dashboard (app.withone.ai) you add DeepSeek and paste your DeepSeek API key — One stores the key; the agent never sees it. (b) You point your agent at the hosted MCP endpoint `https://mcp.withone.ai/mcp` (Streamable HTTP transport; nothing to install). (c) On first connect the agent's client opens a browser to One's OAuth consent screen, where you pick which connections the agent may use and at what level. (d) From then on the agent works through exactly three tools (see below), and One enforces the grant on every single call — an out-of-scope action is never even offered to the agent, and a call naming one anyway is refused.

**Scope control.** The consent screen decides access in three steps: choose account/space (Personal, org, or project; Production or Sandbox), set access per connection, then review and authorize. Per-connection levels are: Full access (every action including deletes), Read & write (reads and changes, no deletes), Read only, Custom (only the actions you tick), plus a Knowledge-only mode that allows reading API documentation but never executing anything. One classifies every action as read, write, or delete by what it does, not its HTTP method. Grants can be narrowed or revoked later from the dashboard and take effect on the agent's next call; widening a grant requires reconnecting and re-approving. Important default worth knowing: the consent screen starts on Production with All connections selected, so approving without changing anything grants full access.

**The 3-tool surface.** The agent loads only three tools no matter how many apps are connected (about 1,200 tokens of context, per a third-party check): `list_one_integrations` — lists the connected apps, each with its connection key and the access the grant allows. `find_one_actions` — finds the action for each operation a task needs (up to 10 operations in one call, given as platform + short intent), returning the chosen action with its documentation plus alternatives. `execute_one_action` — runs one action on one connection, given the action ID and connection key. It is the only tool that can change anything, and it is annotated destructive/open-world so the client can ask before it runs. The workflow is always list → find → execute. In knowledge-only mode the remote server exposes only `find_one_actions` (list and execute are refused); the local server keeps `list_one_integrations` in that mode and also lists platforms you could connect.

**The 12 DeepSeek actions.** One's DeepSeek connection is key-based (`DEEP_SEEK_API_KEY`) and exposes 12 actions, all documented and verified against the live API per One. One's public knowledge page confirms the count (12/12 documented) but the fetched page text does not enumerate the names, so the list below is reconstructed from the DeepSeek API surface as catalogued by other vendors (Scalekit names 11; Composio/OOMOL add the 12th) — treat the enumeration as a reconstruction, not a direct One quote: create chat completion, create beta chat completion, create FIM (fill-in-the-middle) completion, create Anthropic-compatible message, create Responses-format completion, search the web, fetch a URL as Markdown, list models, get user balance, list files, get a file, delete a file. The examples the Mac-provided source names — chat completions, FIM completions, user balance, models — are all confirmed real.

**The CLI path (the "4").** The One CLI (`npm i -g @withone/cli`, then `one init`, `one add deep-seek`) reaches the same DeepSeek connection from a terminal. Its workflow is four verbs, not four DeepSeek actions: `one list` → `one actions search/find` → `one actions knowledge/load` → `one actions execute`. DeepSeek still exposes all 12 actions through the CLI. (Correction to the source below.)

**Hosted vs self-hosted migration.** The same server ships as the npm package `@withone/mcp`, run locally over stdio with a One secret key (`ONE_SECRET`) instead of OAuth — usable in Docker (`docker build` / `docker run -i` with the secret as an env var) or on a headless machine with no browser. Locally, scoping moves from the consent screen to environment variables: `ONE_PERMISSIONS` (read / write / admin; default admin), `ONE_APPROVAL` (ask before writes/deletes via MCP elicitation; default off), `ONE_CONNECTION_KEYS`, `ONE_ACTION_IDS`, `ONE_KNOWLEDGE_AGENT` (removes execute), plus identity scoping (`ONE_IDENTITY`). The local server has the same three tools, with small differences: `execute_one_action` takes camelCase args plus a required `platform`, supports multipart bodies, and has no `ai_model` parameter; it sends no workflow instructions on connect, so the agent's prompt must carry the list/find/execute order. The docs recommend the hosted server wherever the client supports remote servers, and the local one for clients without remote support, headless machines, Docker, or per-user identity scoping. The trade-off, as the source states it: going local moves key management, availability, and audit responsibility back onto the user. One also notes that limits can be enforced on the key itself from the dashboard.

**Pricing / free tier (verified on One's own pricing page).** Free is $0 and includes everything in One: 1M requests/month, 20 requests/minute, 1 organization, 1 project, just the user, 7-day log retention, unlimited connections and unlimited connected agents. Past 1M requests costs $20 per additional million. Starter is $29/mo and Pro $199/mo — same 1M requests, mainly raising rate limits (100 / 500 per min), seats, and log retention. A separate Cloud Agent product is $50/mo per agent and is not needed for the MCP use.

### Verified vs. contradicted / unverified (topic 33)

**Verified against public sources (One's own site/docs, plus independent directories)**
- One exists, is a real hosted MCP gateway at `https://mcp.withone.ai/mcp`, Streamable HTTP, OAuth 2.1 with PKCE, in the official MCP registry as `ai.withone/mcp`. Endpoint confirmed live (a third-party live check on Nov 20, 2026 found it up but requiring sign-in, 401/403 without credentials).
- Exactly three tools with the exact names `list_one_integrations`, `find_one_actions`, `execute_one_action`.
- DeepSeek connection: 12 actions, key-based auth, key stored in One, agent never sees it.
- Scope levels (Full / Read & write / Read only / Custom / Knowledge-only), server-side enforcement on every call, dashboard revoke/narrow.
- Free tier: $0, 1M requests/month, 20 req/min, 7-day logs.
- Local server via `@withone/mcp` + `ONE_SECRET`, Docker support, env-var scoping.
- Formerly Pica, renamed March 2026; older Pica docs/community edition describe an earlier product.

**Found contradicted or imprecise in the Mac-provided source**
- **"CLI path: DeepSeek exposes 4 actions."** Wrong as worded. The CLI exposes 4 commands (list, search/find, knowledge/load, execute); DeepSeek still exposes all 12 actions through the CLI. The "4" is verbs, not actions.
- **"1,101+ platforms."** Slightly stale. One's current pages say 1,108+ platforms and 157,427+ verified tools.
- **DeepSeek model claims are outdated.** The source says `deepseek-chat` and `deepseek-reasoner` "now both point to V3.2" and prices V3 at ~$0.27/MTok. DeepSeek's own API docs say `deepseek-chat` and `deepseek-reasoner` were fully retired and inaccessible after July 24, 2026; the current models are `deepseek-flash` (V4.1-Flash) and `deepseek-v4-pro`, with different pricing. Any portal setup that hard-codes the old model IDs will fail. (The source's V3-era framing, including R1-distillation notes, belongs to an earlier DeepSeek generation.)
- **Tool count drift across versions:** an older 1.x server exposed 4 tools (with `search_one_platform_actions` and `get_one_action_knowledge` as separate tools); the current 2.x server consolidates to 3. A directory page still showing 4 tools is showing the old version.

**Could NOT verify (not tested — per loop rules nothing was signed up for, no key entered, no connector built)**
- The "about five minutes" setup claim and the exact Muse-app custom-connector flow ("tell Muse: build a custom connector to One") — plausible, untested in this run.
- One's claim that it stores none of the data flowing through it — a vendor assertion, not independently checkable from public sources.
- The full 12-action name list from One's own catalog (count verified; names reconstructed as noted above).
- Whether the DeepSeek actions in One's catalog already use the current model IDs (`deepseek-flash` / `deepseek-v4-pro`) — the knowledge page says actions are "verified against the live API," but the model IDs inside the chat-completion action were not visible without signing in.

### Rules (97–99)

97. **Scope before execute, and never approve defaults blindly.** Connect everything read-only or knowledge-only first; widen scope only after a proven, reversible win — because One's consent screen defaults to Production + All connections (full access), and the local server defaults to `ONE_PERMISSIONS=admin` with approval off.
98. **One key, one door, one kill-switch.** Keep every third-party credential inside the bridge (One), never in the agent, chat, or a config file the agent can read; revoke from the one dashboard when done. The agent should be able to lose everything it was granted without losing the keys.
99. **Count the tools, not the platforms.** A bridge is only worth it if the agent's tool surface stays fixed and small (three tools) no matter how many platforms are added; if adding a platform grows the tool list, the bridge is the wrong shape — discover actions on demand instead.

### Brought back to Mac — gray areas and value ideas (topic 33, not acted on)

- **Gray area — the defaults are the risk.** The consent screen starts on Production with All connections selected, and the local server defaults to admin permissions with approval off. Nothing in One stops a hurried user from granting a new agent full delete access to every connected app (Gmail, Stripe, Shopify) in one click. The protection is entirely in the human slowing down at the consent screen. Related: on the local server, scoping is enforced by environment variables the user sets — a shared or copied config file carrying `ONE_SECRET` with admin permissions silently carries full access with it.
- **Gray area — the hosted middleman holds everything.** Every credential for every connected app sits in One's cloud and every action passes through it. That is the product's convenience and its single point of trust: a breach, outage, or shutdown at One affects all connected accounts at once, and the agent's audit trail lives partly on One's side (7-day log retention on the free tier). The local server reduces but does not remove this — the secret key still authenticates to One's API.
- **Gray area — knowledge-only is not identical on both servers.** On the remote server, knowledge-only drops both list and execute; on the local server it keeps `list_one_integrations` and adds the platforms you could connect. A workflow that assumes "knowledge-only means the agent cannot see my connections" is wrong on the local server.
- **New value idea — the 3-tool pattern as a Hive design rule.** The list/find/execute shape is itself the valuable discovery: a fixed three-tool surface with server-side scope enforcement lets one agent reach 1,100+ platforms without its context window growing. The Hive could copy this pattern for its own connectors — one small meta-surface, actions discovered on demand with their real docs, execution gated by a scope the founder sets — instead of loading a separate tool definition per service. No one needs to adopt One to use the pattern; it is a design, not a purchase.
- **New value idea — knowledge-only as the safe first step for every new tool.** Before any wholesaling tool (Sheets, CRM, email) is ever given execute access, connect it knowledge-only and let the agent draft the exact calls, flows, or code first. The agent proves it understands the API before it is allowed to touch live data. This is directly usable inside the Hive's proof-first permission gate.

**Opportunity signs (topic 33)**
- **Opportunity A — One as the Hive's single front door to 1,100+ tools.** Need/desire/want: one agent that can reach Gmail, Sheets, CRM, Stripe, and DeepSeek through one scoped key, without juggling a key per service and without the agent ever holding raw credentials. How: free One account, add DeepSeek (and later Gmail/Sheets/CRM) as connections, point the agent at `https://mcp.withone.ai/mcp`, approve a read-only or custom scope on the consent screen, work list → find → execute. Must acquire: a free One account (no card, per the loop rules) plus a DeepSeek API key. Note: a DeepSeek key requires funding the DeepSeek account balance — that is money, so it is Mac's decision, never done inside a run. One's free tier itself costs $0 for 1M requests/month.
- **Opportunity B — Knowledge-only first access for wholesaling tools.** Need: let the agent learn the real API shapes of the wholesaling stack (Sheets comps, CRM, email) with zero chance of a live send, edit, or delete. How: connect each tool to One in knowledge-only mode; the agent reads docs and drafts the exact calls/flows; only after a proven, reversible win does Mac widen that one connection to read-only, then read-write. Must acquire: only the free One account. No money, no paid key, no card.
- **Opportunity C — CLI find → execute loop for scripted batch work.** Need: repeatable terminal-driven actions across many platforms (e.g. look up a seller, log a comp, draft a follow-up) with structured JSON output an agent can parse. How: `one --agent list`, `one --agent actions search <platform> "<intent>"`, `one --agent actions knowledge <platform> <actionId>`, `one --agent actions execute …`, plus saved One flows for repeated sequences. Must acquire: free One account, Node/npm to install the CLI. No card.
- **Opportunity D — the 3-tool surface as a Hive connector pattern (no vendor needed).** Need: keep the Hive agent's context small while its reach grows, and keep scope enforcement server-side rather than in the agent's prompt. How: copy the list/find/execute shape into Hive connector design — a fixed meta-surface, on-demand action discovery with real docs, execution gated by founder-set scope. Must acquire: nothing. It is a design pattern, not a service.

**Wholesaling tie (topic 33, genuine, not forced):** the portal's real wholesaling value is not DeepSeek — it is that one scoped bridge can front the whole wholesaling stack (email for seller follow-up, Sheets for comps and the 50-target list, CRM for buyers, Stripe for money movement) with read-only-by-default scope control. Scope control is the safety pattern that makes an agent safe to point at live seller/buyer data: research in read-only, execute only for founder-approved sends. The DeepSeek side matters only as a cheap reasoning sidecar (flash model) for comps analysis and script drafting — and only once Mac decides on funding its API balance.

**Caveats (topic 33):** no file was written, nothing was sent, signed, spent, published, or contacted; no trial accepted, no card entered, no sign-up made. All findings are from public sources. Quotes kept to short paraphrases; no passage over 50 words reproduced.

### 34. Breathing tiered architecture, Tier 1 to Tier ∞ (studied 2026-11-20, run 15)

Source: the second Mac-provided body, filed verbatim at `workspace/mogul-hive-feed/MAC-PROVIDED-MUSE-HARNESS-DEEPSEEK-PIPELINE-2026-11-20.md`. Claims are leads to verify, not facts. No sign-up made, no trial, no card, no contact.

**Tier 1 — Zapier free tier as the "minimum lung capacity."** The agent starts with the smallest free automation surface and uses it only for low-frequency, high-leverage triggers (e.g. one alert when a threshold is crossed), not for high-volume work. The free limits act as a metabolic rate: they force the agent to spend automation only on the signals that matter most, because each action costs from a tiny monthly pool.

**Tier 2 — proof-after-unlock inside Zapier.** The wider Zapier catalogue (multi-step Zaps, premium apps, higher volume) is visible but locked. The agent earns access one connection at a time by submitting a verifiable value proof first: the specific connection and action, an expected return range, a maximum acceptable cost, failure conditions, and a rollback-able A/B test. Unlock is treated as supervised credit expansion, not a purchase — the agent's own execution record is the collateral.

**Tier 3 — protocol-reachable, knowledge-only by default.** Through MCP gateways (One, Zapier MCP) a huge surface is technically reachable, but the default scope is read-only: the agent may discover actions and read their documentation, and may propose using them, but may not execute any tool until it has completed a Tier-2-style proof for that specific action. This maps onto MCP's own design: Resources (read-only context the host attaches) and Prompts (user-chosen templates) are open; Tools (model-invoked functions that can change state) are unlocked item by item.

**Tier 4 — self-built tools.** When a proven, profitable external action is bottlenecked by an outside tool's delay, cost, or limits, the agent may propose replacing it with a tool it builds and runs itself (e.g. a local webhook receiver instead of waiting on Zapier's 15-minute poll). The bar is higher than Tier 2: it must prove both that the self-built tool earns more and that it runs inside the approval/audit constraints and can degrade back to Tiers 1–3 when it fails.

**The degradation requirement** means: no tier may become a single point of failure. Every self-built or higher-tier capability must have a tested fallback to the cheaper, simpler tiers below it, so that if the custom tool breaks, the paid/unlocked connection lapses, or the money stops, the system falls back to the free-tier triggers and keeps breathing instead of dying. It is a survival rule, not a performance target.

**Tier ∞ — physical exit via a CMRA / mailbox.bot.** The agent gets a real street address (a private mailbox at a Commercial Mail Receiving Agency) so it can receive letters and small packages, have mail scanned and classified, and send outbound letters/certified mail through an API. The hard legal anchor: a real, verified human must stand behind the box — USPS Form 1583, signed before a notary with two forms of ID — and that human (Mac) is the legal holder, the sender of record, and the taxpayer on any income. The agent can propose, track, classify and trigger, but cannot be the legal sender or owner.

- **Zapier free: two-step Zaps only (one trigger + one action)** — Zapier's own pricing page.

- **Zapier connects 9,000+ apps** — Zapier's own pricing page.
